Here are my notes on automation of commands <a target="_blank" href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/jmenga/">Justin Menga</a> (<a target="_blank" href="https://github.com/mixja">mixja on GitHub</a>) typed in his video course <a target="_blank" href="https://app.pluralsight.com/library/courses/docker-production-using-amazon-web-services/table-of-contents">"Docker in Production Using Amazon Web Services</a>.
His course was released by Pluralsight. And I think it would be difficult to follow along here unless you get a subscription at Pluralsight.com (less than $300 USD per year).

Video in the course is rated as 10 hours, but it took me more like 60 hours of study
because Menga's <em>tour de force</em> covers most of the intricacies one needs to know and be able to actually do in a real job as an AWS Cloud Engineer.

## Parts list (ingredients)

<strong>"Production"</strong> in the course title means that we need to cover management of secrets. Production also requires use of additional tools. There are so many that product names are listed alphabetically here, with links to my blog about it or the vendor's marketing page, plus the version shown in the video course

   * AWS account from email with credit card
   * Ansible from Red Hat (IBM) 2.4.0
   * Authenticator app (from Google) on iPhone/Android
   * Bash (shell) scripts
   * Boto3 (Python library for AWS) 1.4.7 <tt>pip install boto3 netaddr</tt> 0.7.19 
   * Bower (npm install -g bower)
   * Brew package manager for Mac 2.14.2
   * <tt>brew install jq</tt> 1.5.2 to enable shells to handle JSON
   * CodeBuild
   * CodePipeline
   * CloudFormation (CFN) templates (vs Terraform)
   * CloudWatch and Logs Agents
   * <a target="_blank" href="https://github.com/lightbend/config">Config from Lightbend</a> configuration library for JVM languages using HOCON files
   * Docker for Mac 17.09.0-ce
   * easy_install with <tt>sudo -H easy_install pip</tt>
   * EC2 (Elastic Cloud Compute)
   * Elastic (Docker) Container Services (ECR)
   * <a target="_blank" href="https://flywaydb.org/">Flyway</a> for lightweight version control of database schema migrations
   * Git client
   * GitHub account
   * Gradle (multi-project Java build tool replacing ant and maven)
   * IAM
   * Java (the language )
   * KMS (Key Management Service) in AWS
   * Lambda from AWS
   * MacOS keyboarding - Sierra version
   * MFA (Multi-factor Authentication) in AWS
   * NodeJS 4.x or higher (to install the npm package manager)
   * Python programming language 2.7.14
   * Packer from Hashicorp
   * <a target="_blank" href="https://wilsonmar.github.io/text-editors">Text editor</a> Sublime Text 3 with (darker) Material Theme.
   * iTerm2 for Mac
   * brew install tree - 1.7.0
   * STS (AWS )
   * <a target="_blank" href="https://vertx.io/">Vertx</a> for microservices (https://escoffier.me/vertx-hol) event bus

   PROTIP: Some aspects which need to be updated. But much of the course still applies to harneess Infrastructure as Code (IoC) within Amazon Web Services using Docker and EC2 Container Service (ECS) with EC2 and CloudWatch Logs Agents. It uses EC2 Container Registry (ECR) and Lifecycle Hooks Lambda functions to automate test, build, deploy and operate containerized applications in production using Ansible, CloudFormation, Python Lambda, CodeBuild, CodePipeline, etc. Most other courses do not thoroughly cover secrets management using IAM, MFA, Key Management Service (KMS), auto-scaling, CloudWatch.

Docker has become the modern standard for distributing and running cloud native applications, whilst Amazon Web Services provides the world's most powerful and popular cloud computing platform. Together, these technologies can help you deliver your applications faster, more reliably, and at scale. In this course, Docker in Production Using Amazon Web Services, you'll learn how to master these technologies and create a powerful framework and toolset that you can use in the real world for your own applications. First, you'll discover how to leverage the power of Ansible and CloudFormation to create a generic and reusable tool chain for deploying not just Docker applications, but any cloud service you can think of using a fully automated, infrastructure as code approach. Next, you'll use this tool chain to deploy foundational resources in your AWS account, EC2 container registry repositories, Virtual Private Cloud (VPC) networking resources, and an HTTP proxy service that secures outbound communication for your applications. With this foundation in place, you'll create a production-class environment for a Microservices application that leverages a number of native AWS services including the EC2 container service, Relational Database Service, Autoscaling Groups and Application Load Balancers. Finally, you'll learn how to solve operational challenges including how to extend CloudFormation to perform custom provisioning tasks using AWS Lambda functions, how to securely manage and inject secrets into your Docker applications and supporting resources, and so much more. By the end of this course, you'll have developed an advanced understanding of how you can use Docker and AWS to deploy and run your applications faster, smarter, and more reliably than ever before.

The sample application called "microtrader" simulates a system that provides a simple front-end dashboard for monitoring stock trading activity. The application's features include application-level clustering, distributed messaging, and service discovery.
The app is setup in Docker containers within AWS EC2 container service. 
Infrastructure as Code approach for everything that we do, and you will be introduced to a powerful deployment methodology and toolset that leverages Ansible and the AWS CloudFormation service to build and deploy all of your AWS environments and resources. The primary benefit of this approach will be that you can create, update, and destroy complex AWS environments on demand with the click of a button, or the tap of an Enter key, and you'll be able to directly apply this methodology to your own real-world use cases. An important requirement for any deployment methodology is the ability to extend your toolchain to be able to accommodate custom provisioning tasks, and a significant portion of this course will focus on the use of the AWS Lambda service to extend AWS CloudFormation to perform such tasks. AWS Lambda is a serverless platform that can be used for a variety of use cases. And in this course, we will create functions written in Python that perform various custom provisioning actions. With this foundation in place, we will then focus on managing secrets securely for our environments, creating a simple yet powerful secrets management solution based upon the AWS KMS, or Key Management Service, and EC2 Systems Manager Parameter Store that will inject secrets into your Docker applications at container startup, and also securely provision secrets for other resources in your environments. We will then shift focus to the operational concerns of running your application environments, which will ensure we can manage the lifecycle of the infrastructure that supports our Docker applications without disrupting the application and its end users. For example, we will learn how we can update the machine images of our Docker hosts in a rolling update fashion, which ensures we retain full cluster capacity during rolling updates and does not impact end-user access to the application. We will also implement a powerful Auto Scaling solution that first addresses dynamically managing container and resource capacity at an infrastructure and host level, and will then allow us to dynamically scale up or down the number of containers servicing our Docker applications. Finally, we will wrap all of the various test, build, and deployment actions we perform in this course into a continuous delivery pipeline using the AWS CodePipeline, CodeBuild, and CloudFormation services. This will provide an end-to-end pipeline that automatically and continuously tests, builds, and publishes Docker images on every application source code commit, which then proceeds to continuously deploy your application into a development environment running on AWS, and finally provides a manual approval step before automatically deploying your application changes into production.

Before we can deploy the sample application to AWS, it is important to have a solid understanding of how the application is architected, how to build and test the application, and the expected end-user functionality. In this module, we're going to install and run the sample application in your local development environment, which will provide you with the core foundational understanding of how to build, run, and test the application. The sample application is based upon a microservices architecture, so we will first discuss each of the different microservices and how they interact to provide the end-to-end functionality of the application. With a high-level understanding of the application, we will proceed to fork the application's source code from GitHub and clone the application's source locally. We will briefly examine the structure of the application repository so that you have an understanding of where to find the source code, test specifications, and build specifications for each of the application microservices. We will proceed to build the various application artifacts for each microservice, and then start and run the application. At this point, we will be able to take the application for a test drive, after which we will run acceptance tests that verify the external end-to-end functionality of the application.

Application Architecture

The sample microtrader application is developed using one of my favorite application toolkits called Vert. x. Vert. x is a toolkit for building reactive applications on the Java virtual machine, or JVM, and includes a number of features that allow you to build powerful modern microservice-based applications. The sample application is actually based from the excellent Vert. x microservices workshop tutorial, which you can find at the URL shown. If you are interested in building modern reactive microservices, I highly recommend reading through this tutorial. Note, I have made a number of significant modifications to the application for the purposes of this course, so don't be surprised if you find a number of variations between the course material and the original online tutorial. For this course, I have created four microservices that collectively comprise the microtrader application. A very important requirement in a microservice's architecture is the ability for each microservice to be able to communicate with each other. And a powerful feature of Vert. x is the Vert. x event bus, which allows distributed Vert. x applications to communicate with each other using common messaging patterns. The quote generator service is responsible for periodically generating quotes for stocks associated with three fictitious companies. Quotes are published using a publish/subscribe messaging pattern, allowing for multiple subscribers to receive quotes in real time. The quote generator also publishes an HTTP endpoint that returns the current stock prices. The portfolio service is comprised of a set of trader services, which each buy and sell stocks for a given company on a periodic basis. Each of these traders belongs to a single stock portfolio, which operates as a separate portfolio microservice. Vert. x supports a feature known as an event bus proxy, which is a service that allows for remote procedure call, or RPC style communication, between application components distributed over the event bus. The trader services invoke operations on the portfolio service via the event bus proxy service, ensuring stock trades are recorded and the value of the portfolio is kept up to date. The portfolio service publishes all stock trades that each of the trader services makes on the event bus, which in our fictitious stock market is required for auditing purposes. The audit service subscribes to the stock trade transactions published by the portfolio service and writes each transaction to a MySQL database. The audit service also has an HTTP server endpoint that publishes the last 10 transactions, which will be used by the final service, the trader dashboard. The trader dashboard provides an HTTP server endpoint that serves a client-side JavaScript web application that provides a dashboard view of the fictitious stock market and trading activity. The trader dashboard includes a SockJS event bus bridge, which publishes and extends events from the internal Vert. x event bus to JavaScript client applications using the SockJS WebSocket library. This allows the JavaScript client application to receive quote events in real time over a WebSockets connection, and also communicates with the portfolio event bus proxy service to obtain portfolio information. As you can see, the sample application appears simple from the outside, yet comprises a relatively sophisticated set of microservices that work together using advanced distributed application and messaging patterns to deliver the overall functionality of the application.

Installing the Application

Now that you have a high-level understanding of how the application works, let's actually install, build, run, and test the application. The source code for the application is defined in a single repository located in GitHub. The README file provides a comprehensive overview of the application, and I encourage you to read through the README to gain a detailed understanding of how to build and run the application. To get started, we will fork the microtrader application source code repository published in the docker-production-aws organization on GitHub, and obviously you will need to do this logged in with your own GitHub account. At this point, you can also fork the microtrader-base repository, which defines the base, or parent Docker image, for each of the application Docker images. We will need this repository for the next module when we discuss how to build Docker images for the application. Once you have forked the repositories, next clone the repositories locally to the course root folder, which you created in the first module. After cloning each repository, you should have a microtrader and microtrader-base folder. And for this module, we will be focusing on the microtrader repository. The microtrader application uses Gradle as the build tool to test and build the application. Gradle is a popular tool for building Java applications and is considered by many as a modern replacement for more traditional build tools, such as Ant and Maven. The application is using a Gradle multi-project build, and a number of child projects, or subprojects, are defined in the application repository. There are five subprojects in the application as shown, and notice that each subproject folder includes its own src folder to hold the application's source code associated with the subproject. You can see that each of the services that comprise the application are defined as subprojects, along with a microtrader common subproject, which provides common application components shared across the other subprojects. The Gradle build system is configured through a number of files, and the primary file that configures Gradle is the main build. gradle file located in the root of the repository. This defines common settings that apply for all subprojects, as designated by the allprojects keyword, as well as settings that apply to specific subprojects. For example, the section I'm displaying on screen defines settings that apply for each of the application services, but not the microtrader-common subproject. Notice that Gradle expresses all tasks and settings via a programmatic Groovy domain-specific language, or DSL, meaning you can use standard Groovy programming constructs to create powerful test and build functionality. Another important file in the repository root is the settings. gradle file, which defines the root project name, as well as each of the subprojects as defined in the include statements. By convention, each subproject is located in a folder of the same name, and Gradle will look for a build. gradle file within each subproject folder, allowing for build settings specific to that subproject, which either add additional build logic or override build logic defined in the parent build. gradle file. We can see that for the microtrader-quote subproject, the build. gradle file is very simple and specifies vertx-web as an extra dependency over and above common dependencies defined in the root build. gradle file. The Gradle build system also includes a Gradle wrapper script, which allows you to run the Gradle build system without having to install Gradle or support for its parent language, Groovy. The file named gradlew is for UNIX-based systems, whilst the file named gradlew. bat is for Windows-based systems, and the gradle folder supports these wrapper scripts. The remaining Docker folder, Makefile, and Makefile. settings files provide a Docker-based continuous delivery workflow for the application, and we will cover the specifics of the Docker workflow in the next module.

Building the Application

Now that we understand the basic structure of the application, we can now proceed to build the application by running the command gradlew clean test shadowJar, which will execute the Gradle wrapper script and invoke the clean, test, and shadowJar Gradle tasks. The clean task simple removes the build folder, which is where application artifacts are created, whilst the test task runs unit tests for each of the application services. These tests are defined in the src/test folder within each application service subproject and tests the internal functionality of the specific application service. For example, the microtrader portfolio service includes a number of tests that verify traders can buy stocks. What happens if a trader does not have enough cash to buy stocks? And what happens if the trader attempts to buy more stocks then what are currently available? If we now run our Gradle wrapper script, we will see the clean task complete for each subproject, and then the test task will be executed. At some point during the test stage, you may notice that the build will fail. And in this example, the failure occurs on a task named bower for the microtrader dashboard service. If we take a look in the microtrader-dashboard folder at the build. gradle file, you can see here a task called bower exists, which runs the bower install command in the specified directory. The dashboard module provides a web front end that requires client-side JavaScript, and bower is used to install third-party JavaScript packages. So to fix our build failure, we need to ensure Node. js is installed, which I'll do on my system by running brew install nodejs, and then install the bower dependency globally using the npm package manager. This failure is quite significant in that it illustrates an all too common scenario that plagues software development. The problem of it works on my machine, and often only my machine. In the next module, we will learn how to overcome this issue by using a Docker-based continuous deliver workflow to wrap the build process. With Node. js and Bower installed, we should now be able to run the build successfully. The final shadowJar task creates fat JARs for each of our application services. And if you are not familiar with this term, a fat JAR, or Java Archive File, is a self-contained JAR file that packages the application and all of its dependencies into a single deployable runnable artifact, meaning we can run the application directly using this JAR file. If you have experience developing or deploying Java web applications, you may be used to building web archive, or WAR files, and then loading the WAR file into an application container platform, such as Tomcat, WebSphere, or Jetty. This traditional approach works if you are deploying multiple web applications into a single application container; however, in a Docker container world, you should be running one, and only one application per container. The fat JAR packaging mechanism removes the WAR file, as it is no longer required in a Docker container environment. With the application artifacts built, you may be wondering exactly where our fat JARs are located. An inspection of the main build. gradle file shows us the shadowJar task is configured to output all fat JARs to a build/jars folder in the repository root. If we take a look at the contents of the build/jars folder, notice that a fat JAR has been created for each of the application microservices. You can see that each fat JAR also has a consistent version, which is based upon the git-commit-timestamp and the git-commit-hash. Back in the build. gradle file, notice that the version property can be set explicitly via the environment variable APP_VERSION. And this funny-looking operator, also known as an Elvis operator in Groovy, defines the default versioning scheme if the app version environment variable is not set. Notice that the commitTimestamp and and commitId are generated by simply running Git commands from the shell. And Gradle will also append a build identifier to the application version if the environment variable build_id is defined. This build identifier is useful when you build the application on a continuous delivery system, such as Jenkins, and you want to be able to track a specific build to the job that created the build on your continuous delivery system.

Running the Application

We've built the various application artifacts, or fat JARs for the application, and we can now start the application by running each of the fat JARs. We'll get started by first running the fat JAR for the quote generator service. A benefit of fat JARs is that we can run them via the native Java command line using the -jar flag to reference the fat JAR file. Notice I also add a cluster flag, which is a flag specific to Vert. x that tells Vert. x to run in cluster mode. Clustering is the mechanism Vert. x provides to support distributing a Vert. x application across multiple JVMs, or systems. And as we saw earlier in the application architecture, our application relies on several Vert. x microservices that each need to communicate with each other over the Vert. x event bus. Because this is the first service we have started, the cluster will form with just a single member. And after a few seconds, the quote generator service should start successfully. The quote generator service generates stock quotes periodically every 3 seconds by default, and includes an HTTP endpoint that publishes the current stock prices. By default, the quote generator service listens on port 35000. So if you browse to localhost 35000 in your browser, you should see the current stock prices in a JSON format. If you refresh every few seconds, you should notice that the stock prices are changing periodically. Now let's open a new console window and run the trader dashboard service, which is built as the microtrader-dashboard fat JAR. We run the fat JAR, ensuring we include the cluster flag, and you should see the trader dashboard service joining the cluster originally created by the quote generator service. The trader dashboard web application operates on port 8000 by default. So if you browse to localhost 8000 in your browser, you should see the trader dashboard load successfully. Because we only have the quote generator service running at the moment, the trader dashboard is not fully functional; however, parts of the dashboard that relate to the quote generator should be working. You can see the current stock prices of each company are represented on the chart. Notice that the prices may be fluctuating quite randomly. Remember this is a purely fictitious application with some fairly crazy algorithms for generating sample data. Another area of interest in the dashboard is the Available Services panel. This panel shows currently registered service and message endpoints. You can see the quote generator service has registered an HTTP endpoint on port 35000, and is publishing stock quote data and messages addressed as market-data. This demonstrates another powerful feature of Vert. x, service discovery, which can be plugged into a number of popular back ends including Consul and Kubernetes. In this instance, we are using the default internal service discovery back end, which only will exist for the lifetime of the cluster, but is useful for demonstration purposes. Now it's time to run the portfolio service, which will create three separate trader services that randomly choose a stock to trade in and a portfolio service that manages the stocks traded by each trader. After running the portfolio fat JAR with the cluster flag, the portfolio service will join the cluster, and after a few seconds, you will see console output showing each of the stock trades conducted by each trader. If we switch over to the trader dashboard, you can see new portfolio endpoints appear in the Available Services panel, and the Portfolio pane will show the current status of our portfolio managed by the portfolio service. The final service to run is the audit service. Because the audit service needs to write to a database, we first need to initialize the database before running the audit service. The application is configured to use HSQLDB by default, which is a lightweight embedded database that is packaged inside the audit service itself. So you don't need to install any specific database software. What you do need to do, however, is run database migrations, which will generate the initial schema of the database, and when run locally, write database files to the repository root folder. To run the database migrations, you run the java command referencing the fat JAR file with the cp, or class path flag, and then specifying the fully qualified class name of a class I have created called Migrate. This class is a simple main method that uses the Flyway database migrations library to run database migrations. The migration scripts can be found in the src, main, resources, db folder of the microtrader-audit module. And notice migration scripts exist for hsqldb and mysql. If we run the migrate task, you will see the local default configuration will generate the hsqldb database in the repository root folder. Note that you should only ever have to do this once, or if you update the database schema. But if you run into any issues writing to the database, or running the migrations, you can always remove these files and rerun the migrations as a first troubleshooting step. With the migrations in place, we can now start the audit service in a similar fashion to our previous services. Once the audit service joins the cluster, if you switch back to the trader dashboard, you should see the audit service HTTP endpoint registered in the Available Services pane, and the Last Operations pane should start to show stock trading history that is being written to the audit database. You can see that the audit service is running on port 33000, and if you browse to this port, this is where the trader dashboard is obtaining the information that is populating the Last Operations pane. If you scroll down to the bottom of the dashboard, notice the Circuit Breakers panel, which should show the http-audit-service endpoint with a status of Closed. The circuit breaker pattern is a popular pattern in microservices architectures, providing an ability to detect a service failure and back off trying to connect to the service whilst it has failed, essentially providing a circuit breaker effect. This is an important pattern for avoiding the cascading snowball effect a failure can have in high-volume systems, and again demonstrates another powerful feature of Vert. x. If we stop the audit service and switch back to the trader dashboard, notice that the status of the http-audit-service toggles between Open, where the dashboard is not attempting to connect to the audit service, and Half Open, where the dashboard is checking to see if the audit service has become available. If we restart the audit service, you should see the HTTP audit endpoint status once again has changed to Closed and the trader dashboard is returned to its full functionality.

Testing the Application

The microtrader repository includes acceptance tests, which are a suite of tests that test the external interface of the application. The microtrader application is front-ended by the dashboard service, and hence our acceptance tests need to operate in a similar manner to a web client interacting with the dashboard. The microtrader-specs folder defines these acceptance tests. And you can see that our tests are defined in JavaScript, the native language of browser-based web clients. I'm using the popular Mocha testing framework, along with a number of other tools in the JavaScript test ecosystem. And you can see I have defined tests that verify the functionality of the quote generator REST Endpoint, Audit Service REST Endpoint, Audit Operations Endpoint, Service Discovery Endpoint, Market Events, and Portfolio Operations. To run the tests, we open another terminal window and change to the microtrader-specs folder. Before we can run the tests, we need to ensure the Mocha test runner is installed globally, which we can do by running the npm install mocha command with the global flag. We next run the npm install command, which will install the various JavaScript dependencies required to run the tests. And these dependencies are defined in the package. json file. With all JavaScript dependencies installed, we can now simply run the command mocha, which by convention will look in the tests folder and execute all JavaScript files. You can see that all of our tests pass, meaning our application is fully functional and interacting with external web clients correctly.

Summary

In this module, you were introduced to the sample microtrader application, which although is simple from the outside, operates a sophisticated suite of microservices under the hood, which will provide us with some interesting challenges as we learn how to deploy and operate the application in AWS. We installed the application, and then learned how to build the various microservices that comprise the application using the Gradle build system. The application is structured is a Gradle multi-project build with separate subprojects defined for the quote generator, portfolio, audit, and dashboard services. We built self-contained deployable and runnable application artifacts called fat JARs for each of the application services, and proceeded to start the various application services one by one using the Vert. x clustering system to allow all services to communicate via the Vert. x event bus. Finally, we were introduced to the acceptance tests defined in the microtrader-specs folder, which focused on the front-end dashboard service using tests written in JavaScript and executed using the Mocha framework. We verified that all acceptance tests pass, meaning our application is ready to be packaged in a Docker image for deployment into our AWS environments.

Creating Docker Release Images

Introduction

Hi, my name is Justin Menga, and welcome to Creating Docker Release Images. In the previous module, you were introduced to the sample microtrader application, and you were able to install, build, run, and test the application in your local development environment. We verified the application is functional and operating as expected, but before we are able to deploy and operate the application in AWS, we need to package each of the application microservices into Docker images. In this module, we will learn how to run a Docker-based workflow for building, testing, and publishing Docker release images for the sample application. The workflow is based upon a powerful continuous delivery methodology I describe in my Pluralsight course, Continuous Delivery Using Docker And Ansible. And for this course, I have already created the workflow for you. To ensure you have an understanding of the workflow that has been created, we will first discuss the target end-to-end continuous delivery architecture, and then describe what I refer to as the release pipeline workflow for building, testing, and publishing Docker release images. The release pipeline workflow consists of a number of stages, the first of which is the test stage, which uses Docker to run unit tests and build application artifacts. The next stage is the release stage, which is responsible for building and testing Docker release images for each of the application services. The final stages of the workflow allow you to tag and publish Docker release images into a Docker registry, ready for deployment into the various AWS environments we will create later on in this course.

Continuous Delivery Architecture

The continuous delivery architecture is conceptually constructed of a number of pipelines, which provide a specification of the various stages and test criteria required to take an application from source code right through to production. The first pipeline in this architecture is the release pipeline, whose primary purpose is to test and build application artifacts from source, and then create and publish tested and verified Docker release images that are ready for deployment into preproduction and eventually production environments. These actions are grouped into a number of stages including the test, release, tag, and publish stages. The tooling of the release pipeline workflow is based upon Docker, Docker Compose, and the GNU Make build tool, which provides a completely application and build-tool agnostic approach that can be reused not just for Gradle-based Java applications like our sample application, but also for other languages and application frameworks such as Python, Ruby, and Node. js. The workflow is designed to be completely self-contained and environment agnostic, meaning you can develop and run the workflow locally, and then very easily run the workflow in a production continuous delivery system, such as Jenkins or Bamboo. The workflow includes tag and publish tasks, which publish your tested and verified Docker release images to a Docker registry, which could be Docker Hub or your own private registry, such as the AWS EC2 Container Registry, which is what we will be using in this course. In a production setting, your continuous delivery system would next trigger a deployment pipeline, automatically pulling and deploying the newly published Docker release images into an environment, which would typically be a non-production environment, such as dev or test. In this course, we will be deploying into Amazon Web Services and using Ansible to orchestrate the deployment pipeline.

Release Pipeline Workflow

Now that we have an understanding of the end-to-end continuous delivery architecture, let's take a look at the specifics of the release pipeline. The release pipeline workflow is encoded in the application repository and defined using Docker, Docker Compose, and Make specifications. The workflow is intended to be executed on a per-commit basis, invoking a series of workflow stages. The first two stages of the release pipeline include the test stage and release stage, and it's important to understand that each of these stages is underpinned by a supporting test and release environment. Note that each environment is a lightweight container-based environment defined using a Docker Compose specification. When we execute the test stage, a test environment is created. This environment is very simple and defines a single test service specification that describes the various build tooling required to test and build the application, how to run unit tests, and how to output application artifacts in test results. Assuming all tests pass and the build is successful, the next stage is the release stage. The goal of this stage is to build Docker release images for each application service, using the application artifacts, or fat JARs, created during the test stage. Once these images are built, a release environment is created, which creates a complete running environment using containers, standing up each of the application services based from the newly created release images, as well as other supporting services or resources, such as a database. The idea here is that the application is fully functional in the context of a local Docker environment in that you can run a suite of automated acceptance tests that verify the application is behaving as expected from an external perspective. This concept of a release environment and release stage can be quite new to many development teams, but is incredibly powerful as it encodes into the application source-code repository the minimum definition of how to configure, deploy, and run the application in an environment agnostic manner. After the release stage is complete, the application is ready to be tagged and published to your Docker registry. Once published in the registry, the release pipeline workflow is complete, and at this point, you can trigger your deployment pipeline to deploy your verified Docker release images into your target non-production and production environments.

Workflow Specifications

Now that we have an understanding of the continuous delivery architecture and release pipeline, let's take a look at how we actually define the release pipeline and review the various specifications that describe the workflow. The workflow is defined in two main locations in the application repository. The first location is the docker folder where the test and release environments that support the corresponding test and release stages are defined. Each of these folders includes Dockerfiles defining how to build Docker images relevant to each environment in docker-compose. yml specifications, describing the layout of each Docker environment and Docker Compose terms. The second location is at the root of the application repository, specifically the files named Makefile and Makefile. settings. Collectively, these files orchestrate the various low-level operations for each high-level workflow task and drive the workflow using the Docker test and release environments. You can see that a number of workflow tasks are defined in the Makefile, and you can execute any of these tasks by simply typing the make command followed by the task name. A simple example is the version task, which you can run by typing make version, and this returns the current application version. Remembering this is constructed from the git commit timestamp, commit hash, and an optional build ID. Closer inspection of this task shows that it simply echoes a Makefile variable called APP_VERSION, and you can see the variable is actually defined in the included Makefile. settings file as shown. Notice that APP_VERSION is defined in exactly the same terms as how the application version is determined by Gradle in the build. gradle file. And the reason we need to do this is that both the application build and Docker workflow processes require first-hand knowledge of the application version. Because the Docker workflow is exporting this variable in Makefile. settings, the APP_VERSION value will be injected as an environment variable to every task that Make runs. If you recall from the previous module, Gradle is configured such that if the APP_VERSION environment variable is defined, this overrides the default application version convention, and this approach ensures the Docker workflow is the single source of truth for generating the application version.

Understanding the Test Stage

The test stage is executed by running the command make test, and you can see in the Makefile that the make test task is executing a number of commands required to orchestrate running unit tests, collecting test reports, and creating application artifacts. Don't worry about this if you don't understand fully what is going on here. The specifics here are not the focus of this course. And if you want to understand more about this, refer to my Pluralsight course, Continuous Delivery Using Docker And Ansible. One thing that is worthwhile pointing out is that the variable TEST_ARGS is referenced often in the make test task, and this is because it establishes the context for the various docker-compose commands. Closer inspection of the TEST_ARGS variable reveals it is simply a shortcut for the docker-compose -p and -if command flags, which specify the project name and docker-compose specification respectively. Notice that the TEST_COMPOSE_FILE variable references the path docker/test/docker-compose. yml, which of course is where our test environment specification is defined. If we take a look at that file, we find a very simple docker-compose specification with a single service called test. This services references a Dockerfile located in the same folder. And most importantly, executes exactly the same Gradle tasks we ran in our local environment in the previous module to test and build each of the application services. If we take a look at the Dockerfile, you can see I've based the image from the openjdk Alpine Linux JDK 8 image, which is a much more compact and lightweight image when compared with traditional Linux distributions such as CentOS or Ubuntu. Notice that we install Node. js, Git, and Bower, which we found we needed when we built the application locally, and this is actually something that is very important. We have created a specification for a build environment alongside our source code that can be run in any Docker environment. Not just my machine or your machine, but any machine with a Docker client and Docker Engine. The remainder of the Dockerfile provides a pattern for leveraging Docker's image location mechanisms where we copy specific files that define dependencies as underlying layers, meaning we don't have to recreate these layers if only our source code is changing. Notice that we inject the APP_VERSION environment variable defined in our Make settings, ensuring that the application version is injected into the Gradle build process from our workflow.

Running the Test Stage

We can now get started running the workflow by running make test, and because this is the very first time we have run the workflow, we'll need to create our Docker test environment, which downloads and installs application dependencies, and finally copies the application source code into the container. Once complete, our Docker workflow brings up the test service and executes the clean, test, and shadowJar Gradle tasks. The Gradle tasks actually run very quickly because the Docker test image downloaded all of the dependencies when the Docker image was built. I'm using a custom task called copyDeps in the build. gradle file, which essentially tricks Gradle to download, cache, and copy all application and test dependencies, but importantly excludes the files from actually being copied to the dummy /tmp location. This approach ensures the Docker image has all dependencies cached and ready to go without unnecessarily filling up the dummy /tmp location. And so long as our build. gradle files don't change, any pure application source code changes won't invalidate the cached dependency layers in the image. Let's add a comment to one of the application source code files, run make clean, which cleans up our Docker test environment, and rerun the make test task. Notice that we didn't need to rebuild any of the image layers, except for the final layer, as this is where our application's source code change occurred. Now at this point, you might be wondering exactly where the various fat JARs are output in this Docker test environment. And it's important to understand that the make test task is designed to be very transparent and create the same outputs as if we were running the Gradle tasks locally. If we take a look at the test task in the Makefile, notice that the local build folder is first removed and then the docker cp, or copy command, copies the contents of the /app/build folder in the test container to the local build folder, ensuring all of the build artifacts created in the test environment are persisted locally. This will also include test reports, as they are created in the test-results subfolder of the build folder.

Understanding the Base Image

We have successfully run make test, which tested the application and created application artifacts, and we are now ready to run the release stage. The release stage packages the application artifacts into Docker release images that will eventually be deployed into our AWS environments. Part of the release stage is to create a release environment, which includes the newly built release images and any required supporting resources, such as a database. The release environment is defined in the docker, release, docker-compose. yml file. And notice this environment is much more complex than the test environment. The idea here is to create an environment that is representative of a production environment, and run acceptance tests testing the external functionality of the application. We can see the various microtrader services, which are defined in various Docker files in the local folder. Notice that the release images are based from a microtrader-base image, which is actually defined in the separate microtrader-base repository. I prefer this pattern of separating out the base image, as it allows the specification of how to build the application runtime environment to be reused across multiple applications and repositories. And it's important to now take a quick look to understand the default behavior of the base image. Recall in the last module, you forked and cloned the microtrader-base repository to the course working folder. You can see the base image Dockerfile is very simple. It is based upon the openjdk Java 8-jre-alpine Linux image, as we don't require the JDK to run an already-built fat JAR, and ideally want to keep development packages out of our production environment. The base image copies a file called cluster. xml, which is used to configure how the Vert. x cluster discovery mechanism works. In the local developer environment, the default cluster configuration, which is defined in the microtrader-common module, binds to the local host interface. And although this is effective for a local development machine, this does not work well with containers. In a container environment, each container has its own network stack and can communicate with, and discover, other containers over the default Docker bridge network. So the base image cluster. xml file is configured to allow for multi-cast discovery. If you have experience with AWS, you may be aware that AWS does not support multi-cast network communications. So in later modules, we will modify this file to adapt accordingly. The next piece of the base image is the ENTRYPOINT directive, which references a local entry point shell script. This defines the default launch script of the image, and you can see that this script is very simple and currently does not really do anything. In later modules, we will inject custom initialization logic to deal with a couple of challenges we will hit once we try to run our application in AWS. And this entry point script approach is a good pattern to follow if you need a lifecycle hook to perform custom actions at container startup. The final exec action hands off control from the bash process to the command arguments passed to the entrypoint script, which is another important best practice when you build containers, as it ensures your application process will be launched as the primary process ID, or PID 1, in the container. Back in the base image Dockerfile, the CMD directive provides the default command arguments that are passed to the entrypoint script. You can see we define the command to launch a fat JAR, and that we expect the fat JAR to reside in the location /app/app. jar. We pass in the cluster flag, and also a property flag called vertx. hazelcast. config, which allows us to reference the cluster. xml file that enables multi-cast discovery. So the current default behavior is to launch the entrypoint script, run any custom initialization logic, and then run our fat JAR using the default command arguments. Note that both the default entrypoint and command arguments can be overridden at container launch time if required. So this gives us a convention over configuration approach with the ability to customize as required.

Understanding the Release Stage

If we shift back to the main microtrader repository, let's review the remaining pieces of how we build the release image. Notice we are using a build argument called app_version, which injects the current application version into the Docker build environment. We use the app_version build argument to copy the correct fat JAR from the build/jars folder into the app/app. jar location, which is where the base image command directive expects the fat JAR to be located. If we open the release environment docker-compose specification, you can see that each application service includes a number of environment variables that collectively configure how each service should run. I'm using the typesafe config library to inject these environment variable values. In the application. conf file, located in the resources folder for each application service, links these environment variables to configuration parameters that can be consumed in the application source code. Using the microtrader dashboard service as an example, notice that the HTTP port parameter has a default value of 8000, but will be overwritten by the corresponding environment variable if present. In the application source code, the HTTP port parameter is obtained by calling the typesafe config library, and this is an important aspect of making your application run in Docker containers, as ideally, they should support configuration via our environment variables as a first-class citizen. The remaining services in the release environment include a db MySQL service and a specs service, which runs our acceptance tests against the release environment.

Running the Release Stage

Now that we have an understanding of the release stage, let's run the make release task. The release task in the Makefile orchestrates the building and creating of the release environment, and you can see each of the various Docker release images are being built automatically. Once the release environment has been built, the workflow orchestrates the various deployment tasks that we had to manually do ourselves when we ran the application locally. You can see that the database is started first, and once the database is ready, database migrations are run to create the audit, schema, and tables, after which the audit service can be started. Under the hood, the workflow is starting the database and service containers, and then leveraging the new HEALTHCHECK directive introduced in Docker 1. 12, which defines a process to execute locally in each container to determine container health. The Makefile includes a function called check_service_health, and this function essentially defines a basic shell script that introspects the release environment and waits until each service is marked healthy before continuing to the next service, ensuring the environment comes up correctly without any race conditions or connectivity issues. Once all of the application services are running, the workflow executes the acceptance tests via the specs service, and if all acceptance tests pass, the release workflow is complete, and notice that you can actually browse to the various application service endpoints, which are running in our release environment. This is very useful for local development where you may want to perform some exploratory testing or demonstrate the application. If you run the docker ps command, you can see that each of the application services, along with the database service, are running as separate containers, confirming the application is being served from our Docker release environment.

Tagging and Publishing Release Images

The next stage of the workflow is to tag and publish each of the release images. The workflow includes a make tag command, which allows you to tag each release image with an arbitrary set of tags. The make tag default command tags all of the release images with a default set of tags, which includes the application version, commit hash, the latest tag, and any Git tags associated with the current commit. To demonstrate this, you can run the git tag command as shown, which adds a tag of 0. 1. 0 on the current commit, and then run the make tag default command. If you now run the docker images command, notice that each of the microtrader release images has been tagged as per the default tagging strategy, and also includes the Git tag that we just added to our repository. After tagging the release images, the next step would be to log into our Docker registry and publish the images. And the workflow provides make login and make publish tasks to provide this functionality. At this point, won't publish the image as we'll be doing that in later modules. So we now run the command make clean, which cleans up and tears down our test and release Docker environments.

Summary

In this module, you were introduced to the powerful Docker-based release pipeline workflow, which fast tracks the building, testing, and publishing of Docker release images for the sample application. The release pipeline workflow is part of a wider continuous delivery architecture, where release images published by the release pipeline feed deployment pipelines to one or more environments depending on the specific needs of your application and organization. The workflow is designed to be completely environment, language, and application agnostic, and is based upon a simple toolchain that includes Docker, Docker Compose, and Make. You can use these tools to wrap not just the sample application for this course, but any application designed to run in a Linux environment regardless of language or application framework. The first stage of the workflow is the test stage, which runs application unit tests and builds application artifacts. The application artifacts are then fed into the release stage, which creates Docker release images, stands up a release environment, and then runs acceptance tests that verify the external functionality of the application. The final stage is to tag and publish the tested and verified Docker release images to your Docker registry of choice. The workflow gives us a great head start into learning how to deploy and operate the application on AWS. And in the next module, we'll get started setting up AWS to deploy and run our application.

Setting up AWS Access

Introduction

Hi, my name is Justin Menga and welcome to Setting Up AWS Access. We are almost ready to run our sample application in AWS but before we can do this, we need to ensure our AWS account is configured appropriately to allow authorized users the ability to publish, deploy and run applications and infrastructure in AWS. We will first discuss best practices for configuring the AWS Identity and Access Management or IAM service which includes configuring multi-factor authentication and delegating privileges to IAM roles which users can then assume. We will set up IAM for our account following these core principles, configuring IAM policies, roles, users and groups. We will also set up operational access to our AWS infrastructure creating an EC2 key pair for securely providing SSH access to EC2 hosts and then configuring the AWS command line tool to work with our IAM setup to provide multi-factor authentication and automatic role assumption from the command line.

Setting up AWS Identity and Access Management

This course is not intended to cover AWS Identity Access and Management or IAM in detail. However, to give you a real-world perspective of how you will be interacting with the AWS EC2 container service I have adopted the use of what is considered the best practice in terms of how to establish and operate your Identity Access and Management framework. At a high level this means at a minimum your users should use multi-factor authentication or MFA to authenticate both console and API access to AWS. Multi-factor authentication requires users to authenticate with something they know, for example, the user's password and something they have which is often an application referred to as a virtual MFA device installed on a mobile device that the user will typically have in their position. The AWS console supports MFA authentication and if MFA is enabled for a user will only grant access if both forms of authentication are successful. Another best practice is rather than assign privileges directly to users or groups, you assign privileges to IAM roles that you then allow groups to assume. An IAM role includes policies that permit access to AWS resources and are considered a best practice as they allow users to temporarily assume an elevated level of access only when users require such access and only for a limited time period which is similar to the concept of Sue in Linux. Another advantage of this approach is that you can allow users outside of your resource accounts to assume roles allowing for organizational account structures with a centrally managed identity account easing the administrative overhead of managing users and groups assuming a role also works well with multi-factor authentication. In this example, the user authenticates using an API key and is prompted for an MFA code. This information is passed along with the role the user wants to assume to the AWS STS or Security Token Service which validates the credentials and also verifies the user is allowed to assume the role. If everything is in order, the security token service issues a set of temporary session credentials that grant the user access to AWS resources as defined in the IAM policies attached to the assumed role.

Creating an Account Alias

Let's now step through setting up your AWS IAM access appropriately to meet these high level best practices which will include creating an account alias, creating IAM roles, creating groups, creating policies that enforce the requirement for MFA, creating users and finally, enrolling users for MFA. For this demonstration, I'm assuming that you have a blank account and have logged in with your root credentials. Note that you should never use your root credentials for day-to-day tasks and these credentials should only ever be used for initial account setup or emergency access in the event your day-to-day credentials have been lost or compromised. In the IAM dashboard, we will first customize the IAM user's sign in link and create an account alias. You can set the alias to whatever you like but bear in mind the alias you choose must be globally unique, so you can't for example use the alias I'm showing here as my account has already claimed this alias. Creating an account alias makes it easier for your users to work with the AWS console, so this is one of the first tasks you should complete.

Creating IAM Roles

Next, we will create an IAM role called Admin which is intended to grant full administrative access to the account and we'll configure this as a role intended to support cross-account access. This approach is typically used when you want to grant access to resources in the local account and allow users from a central identity account to assume the role and be granted appropriate access to local resources. In our topology we only have a single account, so we'll grant our own account the ability to assume the role meaning we'll still be able to adopt the same approach of assuming a role even though we don't have a multi-account topology in our AWS environment. As a best practice, we should also require multi-factor authentication or MFA. Next, we attach a policy to the role and we can choose the AWS-managed policy called AdminstratorAccess which is provided out of the box and provides full access to all account resources. After clicking Create Role, the role will be created and if you select the role we need to copy the Amazon resource name or ARN value of the role for the next step.

Creating Groups

We will now create a group called Administrators which will be granted the ability to assume the admin role we have just created. We can create the group by selecting Create New Group under IAM groups, provide a name and we won't be attaching any policies that grant access to resources directly to this group so we just continue to the next step and finish creating the group. We now need to provide the Administrators groups the ability to assume the admin role which we can do by clicking on the group, selecting the Permissions tab and creating an in line policy. We select the Custom Policy option, name the policy AssumeAdminRole and then specify a policy document as shown. This policy grants the ability to assume the admin role we just created which we reference by pasting the ARN of the role. You can click the Validate Policy button to check the policy is valid and then click Apply Policy to finish attaching the policy to the Administrators group. At this point we have set up our Administrators group and now need to set up a Users group. We'll use this group to enforce the requirement for multi-factor authentication, so in general every user and administrator should be assigned to this group. Again we skip adding a managed policy as we are going to add our own in line policy and once the group is created, we can go back into the group, select Permissions and cerate a new custom policy. The policy you need to provide is published at the GitHub just shown and you can learn more about the policy at the blog post shown on screen. In short, the policy provides users enough permissions to manage their password and MFA device but denies all other actions if the user has not been authenticated via MFA. One golden rule of IAM is that deny statements always take precedence over permit statements, so the following deny statement will deny access to all services except for IAM if MFA has not been used for authentication. Let's now copy and paste the policy document and after ensuring you replace the account markers with your local account ID we next validate the policy and then click Apply Policy to attach the policy to the Users group.

Creating Users

Now that our groups are in place, the last step is to create an IAM user. Here I create an account for myself, initially enabling console access only, specifying my own password and disabling the required Reset Password option. We next proceed to assign the user to the Administrators and Users groups meaning this user will be able to assume the admin role and will also be required to use multi-factor authentication. After creating the user, if we navigate back to the main IAM dashboard, copy the IAM user sign in link, we can now paste the link into your browser and log in with the new user credentials. After successfully logging in, notice that we are not able to do much other than browse to the user account and set up multi-factor authentication. Before we set up MFA, note that you will need a virtual MFA device such as the popular Google Authenticator application which is available for both Android and iOS. Assuming you have Google Authenticator installed on a mobile device, after clicking the Edit icon next to Assigned MFA Device, we select the virtual MFA device option after which a quick response or QR code is generated on screen. We now need to switch to our mobile device and use the Google Authenticator application to add a new account. After capturing the generated QR code in the AWS console, we need to enter two token codes to synchronize the virtual MFA device with AWS and once done, MFA setup is complete. At this point we can now attempt to assume the admin role by selecting the switch role option from the user dropdown. In the account box, we enter the account alias you created earlier and we simply refer to the role by its short name, in this case, Admin. If you click the Switch Role button, notice that the operation fails. And this is because we have not yet logged in with our MFA device. If you log out and log back in as the user, notice that you are now prompted for an MFA token. After entering the correct token you can now attempt to switch roles again, and this time we are able to successfully assume the admin role.

Setting up an EC2 Key Pair

The EC2 container service requires you to operate your own EC2 instances and when you want to create your instances, you must specify a new or existing EC2 key pair which is simply an SSH public key paired to an SSH private key running on a system that you want to be able to administer your EC2 instances from. In the example, I will create a new EC2 key pair from the AWS console by selecting the EC2 service and clicking on the Key Pairs link in the Network and Security section. After clicking the Create Key Pair button and providing a suitable name, a private key file will be automatically downloaded to your computer. I typically store all SSH keys in the. ssh folder in my home directory and one thing you will need to do is ensure appropriate permissions are set on the key file by using the chmod command as shown.

Setting up AWS CLI Access

We've set up a basic framework for adding users to our account following the best practice of requiring MFA for all operations. We've seen how to log into the AWS console and assume the admin role and now let's set up the AWS command line tool so that we will be able to assume the admin role and provide MFA token details from the command line. The AWS command line interface is distributed as a Python package so we run the command pip install awscli to install the application and its dependencies. After installation, you can run the AWS configure command to get started with a default configuration but we'll skip this and configure the installation directly ourselves. The AWS CLI configuration is controlled by two files located within the. aws folder under your home directory and if you haven't run the AWS configure command, you will need to create the. aws folder yourself and create a file called config and another file called credentials. The credentials file stores AWS credentials referred to as access keys and we will create a profile called docker-production-aws with an associated access key ID and secret access key which will be used by the AWS CLI program to authenticate with AWS whenever we use this profile. We'll need to generate these credentials in the AWS console so if we switch back to the console, navigate to the IAM dashboard, open our user account and select the Security Credentials tab, we can generate a new access key by clicking the Create Access Key button. We can view the secret access key by clicking Show and we now need to copy both the access key ID and secret access key to our profile in our local credentials file. After updating and saving the credentials file, you can close the Create Access Key window in the AWS console. The other file that configures the AWS CLI is the config file which is where we can create profiles and associated configuration settings for the AWS CLI. For our use case, we want to be able to assume the admin role and a nice feature of the AWS CLI is the ability to do this automatically for you. To do this, we create a profile called docker-production-aws-admin and specify the source profile as the docker-production-aws profile we previously created in the credentials file. We specify the Amazon resource name or ARN of the profile we want to assume using the role_arn setting and notice this is the ARN of the admin role we created earlier in this module. You can also set a default region so that you don't need to explicitly set this in your environment each time you use the CLI. Let's save the config file and switch to the console to test out the CLI. To use the profile we created, we can set an environment variable called AWS_PROFILE to the name of the profile that we want the CLI to use. We will reference the admin profile in the CLI config file because this is the profile that will attempt to assume the admin role in our account. Notice that if we try to test the AWS CLI, by using the command aws ec2 describe-vpcs an authorization failure occurs. And this is because we haven't authenticated using multi-factor authentication. To rectify this, we can specify the ARN of our MFA device in the AWS config file profile which we can obtain from our user account Security Credentials tab in the AWS console. After saving the config file, let's rerun the previous AWS command and notice we are now prompted for an MFA token. After entering the correct token, the AWS command should complete successfully and return information about each VPC in your AWS account. Notice if we rerun the command, we are not prompted again for MFA authentication and this is because we were granted a temporary session token by the AWS security token service that has a lifetime of one hour by default. The AWS CLI stores temporary session tokens in a local cache within the. aws folder allowing you to run AWS commands without having having to re-authenticate for the life of the temporary session token.

Summary

In this module we performed our initial AWS setup establishing the foundations of secure Identity and Access Management using multi-factor authentication and IAM role assumption by creating various IAM roles, policies, users and groups. We created a simple yet effective approach to enforcing multi-factor authentication and attached a virtual MFA device to our IAM user account. We created an EC2 key pair which downloaded a private SSH key to our local computer that is linked or paired to a public SSH key stored in the AWS EC2 service. In future modules, we will use this key pair to ensure we can securely connect and authenticate to AWS EC2 instances using SSH. Finally, we configured the AWS command line tool with API credentials and configured the CLI tool to automatically assume a given role and prompt for MFA authentication ensuring we can use both the AWS console and command line in a secure and effective manner.

Running Docker Applications Using the EC2 Container Service

Introduction

Hi. My name is Justin Menga, and welcome to Running Docker Applications Using the EC2 Container Service. In the previous modules, you were introduced to the sample microtrader application and learned how to test, build, and run the application locally and how to create, tag, and publish docker release images for each of the microtrader application services. We are now ready to run our application in AWS, and in this module, we will learn how to run Docker applications in AWS using the EC2 container service. We will first discuss the EC2 container service and EC2 container registry, learning about the high-level architectural components of these services and how they interact with each other. We will then proceed to publish each of our Docker images to the EC2 container registry, or ECR, which will require us to first create ECR repositories for each image and then publish each image using the release pipeline workflow included in the sample microtrader application. With our Docker images published in ECR, we will establish the various ECS components required to run our applications, including creating ECS clusters, creating ECS task definitions, and defining ECS services and tasks that represent the required runtime state of our application.

EC2 Container Service Overview

The EC2 container service, or ECS, is an AWS-operated service that provides us with the building blocks to construct how we want to deploy and operate Docker-based applications in AWS. As users of the EC2 container service, we configure and manage our applications by defining ECS clusters, ECS task definitions, ECS services, and ECS tasks. Before we can use the EC2 container service, we need a place to publish Docker images for our applications, and the EC2 container registry, or ECR, provides a secure, private Docker registry service where we can create ECR repositories for each of our Docker images and securely manage access to each repository using standard AWS IAM controls. Once an image has been published in an ECR repository, we need Docker infrastructure to run our Docker images. ECS includes the concept of ECS clusters, which are a logical grouping of EC2 instances that can run Docker containers. Each EC2 instance is referred to as an ECS container instance and includes a Docker Engine, as well as an ECS container agent, which communicates with the EC2 container service to manage the deployment and operation of containers. ECS container instances are typically arranged in EC2 autoscaling groups, providing the ability to scale the ECS cluster up and down as required. ECS task definitions provide a blueprint that describes one or more containers that comprise your application. Each container definition includes information specifying the container image, how much CPU and memory should be allocated to the container, environment variables that should be passed to the container, and many other options. You can think of ECS task definitions as similar to a Docker-composed specification. And in our use case, we will create an ECS task definition for each of our microtrader services. An ECS service describes how many instances of an ECS task definition you want to run and the target ECS cluster where you want to run your task definition instances. The number of instances varies depending on the nature of your application. For example, in the microtrader application, we only want to run a single instance of the Quote Generator and Portfolio Services, but can run multiple instances of the Trader Dashboard for high availability and distributing load. ECS always tries to ensure your ECS service is running the desired number of instances. For example, if an instance of your service exits for whatever reason, ECS will attempt to start a new instance of your service and continue to do so until the desired number of instances are running. A very useful feature of ECS services is support for rolling deployments where updates to the ECS task definition associated with a given ECS service will trigger a deployment of new service instances based upon the new ECS task definition in a rolling fashion. This means that ECS will kill the old service instance and deploy new service instances, one instance at a time, until all ECS service instances have been replaced. ECS also includes support for managing the front-end elastic or application load balancers that you may use to front end your ECS service instances. ECS will handle registration of your instances to the load balancer and will coordinate rolling deployments using connection, draining, and health checks to provide uninterrupted service to end users during new deployments. Finally, ECS also supports ECS tasks, which provide the ability to run in an ad hoc fashion one or more instances of a given ECS task definition on a given ECS cluster. Unlike ECS services, ECS tasks are one-shot operations that will either succeed or fail and are useful for running ad hoc deployment, administrative, or scheduled operational tasks.

Publishing Images to EC2 Container Registry

Now that we have an understanding of how the EC2 container service works, let's get started working with ECS by publishing Docker images to the EC2 container registry. Currently the sample application you have forked is configured to publish to Docker Hub. However, if you are using the EC2 container service, you typically will want to use the EC2 container registry to store your images, allowing you to leverage the native integration with the EC2 container service and identity and access management controls. For our application to run, we need to publish each of the microtrader release images we created in the previous module, along with the microtrader-base image. We'll first get started with the microtrader- base image, and before we can publish the image, we need to create a new repository in ECR. If you select Services, ECS Container Service in the AWS Console, you will be presented with a Get started wizard if you have not used ECS previously. We can click on the Get started button and then click Cancel to exit the wizard and open the main ECS dashboard. To create a new repository, we click on Repositories and then click on the Get started button. We will call the Repository name dockerproductionaws/microtrader-base, and after clicking the Next step button, the repository is created. Notice that you are provided with instructions on how to authenticate your Docker client to ECR, and if we switch to the console, we can execute the aws ecr get-login command, which will return the full docker login command you need to run to authenticate to ECR. Note that in the background, our CLI uses a profile we created earlier, which includes a default region. So in this example, you don't need to specify the region. A faster way of logging into ECR on OS X and Linux is to run the eval command with aws ecr get-login specified as a command substitution, which will automatically run the docker login command returned from the AWS CLI in a single step. Now that we have logged into ecr, let's switch to the microtrader base folder and build the microtrader base image by running the docker build command as shown in the AWS Console instructions. After the image is built, we can then tag and push the freshly built image, referencing the fully qualified name of the ECR repository, which includes the registry URL, organization, and repository name, as well as an optional tag. Once you have pushed the image successfully, if we click on Done in the console, you can see the image with the latest tag that we just published, which has a compressed size of approximately 40 MB. The Permissions tab allows you to assign resource-level permissions to ECR repositories, and the typical use case for this type of permission is to grant implicit access to principles without requiring those principles to have explicit permissions to the repository. For example, you may want ECS hosts in another account to be able to pull and run your images, and by granting pull access to the remote account, the ECS hosts in the other account require no explicit permissions to be able to pull your repository. If you click the Add button, notice that you can permit or deny access to principles and define appropriate actions that are either permitted or denied. Note that ECR does not currently support public repositories that can be pulled anonymously. We've established our first ECR.

Publishing Images Using the Release Pipeline

Now that we have created our ECR repositories, we now need to update the microtrader release pipeline to use the newly published base image from ECR and also reconfigure the release pipeline to publish each of the microtrader release images to our new ECR repositories. To base our Docker release images from the new base image published in ECR, we open the Docker release folder in the microtrader repository and modify the from directive in each Dockerfile to reference our fully qualified name of the base image.
