With each Dockerfile updated, we next need to configure our release images to be published to ECR. The Docker workflow is designed to support private registries. However, by default, the Docker registry variable in the MakeFile. settings file is set to Docker. io, which represents Docker Hub. You can override the setting by defining the Docker registry variable in the main Makefile as shown, and you set this to the URL of the EC2 container registry for your account. If you take a look at the tag and publish actions in the Makefile, you can see that the Docker registry value is already referenced, so you don't need to modify these actions. The only other setting that you need to override is the DOCKER_LOGIN_EXPRESSION variable, again defined in Makefile. settings, which, by default, is a standard docker login command that expects a DOCKER_USER and DOCKER_PASSWORD environment variable to be set. Because the AWS CLI generates the docker login command automatically for us, we need to create a new DOCKER_LOGIN_EXPRESSION at the top of our Makefile, which overrides the default setting and executes the aws ecr get-login command. Notice here that I create a variable for the AWS_ACCOUNT_ID and then use an extended form of the aws ecr get-login command, which explicitly references the AWS account of our repository. This is useful for the scenario where you might be publishing an image from a different build account and want to ensure you log in to the correct target registry. With our Makefile now updated, we are ready to test the release pipeline. Because we need to be able to pull the microtrader base image from ECR, we will now need to ensure we run make login first before attempting to run the release pipeline. Once make login completes successfully, we can run make test to build our application artifacts and then run make release. Assuming everything has been configured correctly, make release should complete successfully, and we can now run the make tag:default command to tag each release image. If we run the docker images command, notice that each image is now tagged with the ECR repository. And if we run make publish, you should be able to publish each release image successfully. Now before we continue on, because we have made changes to our local microtrader and microtrader base repositories, now is a good time to commit and push our changes using the git commands as shown.

Creating an ECS Cluster

We have published all the images required to run our application in AWS, so we can now start to create the ECS infrastructure for the application. We'll get started by creating an ECS cluster, which is a collection of ECS container instances that run ECS tasks and services defined in ECS task definitions. An ECS container instance is simply an EC2 instance with an ECS container agent installed that communicates back to the ECS managed service provided by AWS. ECS clusters are the main unit of deployment for ECS. For example, when you create an ECS service, you specify which ECS cluster you want each of the ECS service instances to run on. To get started creating an ECS cluster, we browse to the ECS dashboard and select Clusters from the menu on the left. After clicking the Create Cluster button, we are presented with some parameters of how to configure the cluster, including Cluster name, EC2 instance type, Number of instances, and a Key pair for remote SSH access. To keep things simple, we will just specify a single instance, and notice that the wizard automatically selects an appropriate Amazon machine image for you, which may vary from the ID shown on screen, depending on the region you are located in. This Amazon Machine Image, or AMI, is referred to as the Amazon ECS optimized AMI, which you can find out more about at the URL shown and is the easiest way to get started using ECS. Note that you can create your own machine images from scratch that work with ECS. However, in general and in this course, I typically recommend using the Amazon ECS-optimized AMI as a starting point and performing your own modifications on top of this image, and in later modules, we will learn how and why you would do this. In the Networking section, we'll just select the default VPC and one of the default Subnets, and we'll create a Security Group that allows inbound SSH access from anywhere. Note that you should never open up this level of access to SSH in production. But for now, we are making things simple for demonstration purposes. If you now click on the Create button, a new ECS cluster will be created for you. Notice that the wizard is using CloudFormation under the hood. If we switch over to the CloudFormation dashboard, we can see a new CloudFormation stack is being created for us. The stack is successfully created when it reaches a status of CREATE_COMPLETE. And if we switch back to the ECS dashboard, you can see that we have a single ECS container instance registered to our ECS cluster.

Verifying an ECS Container Instance

Now that our ECS cluster is created with a single ECS container instance, before we attempt to run any containers on our cluster, let's verify our ECS container instance as functioning correctly. The simplest way to check our ECS container instances are functioning correctly is to use the ECS dashboard. And in the Clusters view, you can see that we have a single ECS container instance running on our microtrader cluster. If we select the cluster and then click on the ECS Instances tab, you can see information about the container instance, including the EC2 instance ID, agent status and version, and Docker version. If we click on the container instance link, we can see the private and public IP address of the instance. Now you may be wondering exactly how the ECS container instance knew how to join our microtrader test cluster. And if we click on the EC2 Instance ID link, right-click the instance, and select Instance Settings, View/Change User Data, notice that the EC2 instance runs a very simple user data script on first creation, which simply writes the ECS cluster name to a local configuration file that is used by the ECS agent to determine which cluster it should join. Let's now log in to the instance and take a closer look at what is happening under the hood of an ECS container instance. To log into the instance, we copy the public IP address and then ssh to the instance, referencing the key pair we configured for the stack and specify the default user name of ec2-user. Once you have successfully logged on, we can verify Docker is working correctly by running the docker info command. You can see various pieces of information about the Docker Engine, including the Docker Engine version and storage engine. And notice that there is currently a single running container. If you run the docker ps command, you can see that the container is the amazon-ecs-agent, which is in constant communication with the EC2 container service and whose primary responsibility is running new containers to find in the form of ECS services and ECS tasks. If we run docker inspect against the ECS agent container, we can see a number of local folders emit to the container. This /var/run folder hosts the Docker Engine socket file, which allows the ECS agent to communicate with and manage the Docker Engine. And notice they contain logs amounted to the /var/log/ECS folder on the underlying host file system. These logs are useful for troubleshooting issues, such as the ECS agent not starting or if the ECS agent is unable to pull a Docker image or start a Docker container. The ECS agent also provides a local introspection endpoint, which you can use to obtain basic status information about the ECS agent. To obtain this information, we can curl port 51678 on the local host as demonstrated. You can see that the metadata endpoint lists the ECS cluster that the container instance is registered to, whilst the tasks endpoint shows currently running ECS services or tasks. In later modules when we create our own CloudFormation stacks to stand up complete environments, we'll use this introspection endpoint as a basic health check to ensure ECS container instances have registered successfully to the ECS cluster.

Creating an ECS Task Definition

Now that we have verified our ECS cluster, and ECS instances are up and running, we are ready to create an ECS task definition, which will define how the EC2 container service should configure and run our Docker images. To create an ECS task definition, we shift back to the ECS dashboard, select Task Definitions, and then click the Create new Task Definition button. We will call the task definition microtrader-quote, and we can add a container definition by clicking the Add container button. We will create a single container for the quote generator service, and we specify the full ECR repository name for the quote generator image we published earlier in this module. We need to allocate some memory to the container, and we will configure the quote generator to listen on port 8000 and map this to the same port on the host. In the Environment section, we don't need to specify Entry point or Command arguments as the release image defaults are sufficient, but we'll add an environment variable called HTTP_PORT and set a value of 8000, which will ensure our container binds to port 8000 as we have configured in our port mapping. For now, that's all we need to do to define the quote generator container definition. And after clicking the Add button to finish creating the container definition, we can click the Create button to complete the creation of our first ECS task definition.

Creating an ECS Service

We've established an ECS cluster where we can run our containers and created an ECS task definition that defines a generic container runtime configuration. Now we need to create an ECS service, which will define a service consisting of one or more containers based upon on ECS task definition and target our ECS cluster for running the service. To create an ECS service, we need to select our ECS cluster and then click the Create button on the Services tab. We ensure that the correct microtrader-quote task definition and microtrader test cluster are selected. And we will call the service microtrader-quote and specify just one task as we only have one container instance currently running. For now we don't need to worry about any of the other options, and we are ready to create our service by clicking the Create Service button. We can now check the status of the service by clicking View Service, and notice that we have a single task with a status of PENDING, meaning our ECS agent is attempting to pull the Docker image for the container defined in the task definition and then start the container. After 30 seconds or so, you should see the task change to a RUNNING status, meaning the container successfully started and is running. If we switch back to our SSH session on our ECS instance and run the docker ps command, notice that the quote generator service is running with the container port 8000 mapped to the host port 8000. If we run a curl command locally, we can see that the quote service is running and periodically generating new quotes. Now if you attempt to connect to the public IP address of the ECS instance on port 8000, you won't be able to because recall when we created the ECS cluster, we only permitted access to port 22 from the outside world. Our final production environment will include an application load balancer in front of our ECS instances. So at this point, I won't bother modifying the security group of our ECS instance to allow external access to the quote generator service.

ECS Service Deployments

Our application is up and running as an ECS service, so how do we deploy new versions of our application? The ECS service includes support for automated rolling deployments of new application versions or application configuration changes, so let's see how this works. For now, we are going to make an application configuration change and trigger the new deployment manually, and whilst in later modules this will be completely automated, the same fundamental deployment mechanisms we are about to observe apply. We'll get started by making a change to our ECS task definition. Task definitions are immutable, meaning each time you change a task definition, you create a new revision or version of the task definition configuration. You can create a new revision by selecting an existing task definition and clicking the Create new revision button. In this example, I'm going to modify the port mapping from a fixed port mapping on the host to a dynamic port mapping by clearing the Host port box, meaning Docker will choose a random port on the host to map to our container port. After clicking the Update button and in the Create button, a new revision is created, and notice this has a revision number of 2. With the updated ECS task definition in place, we now need to update our ECS service to use the new task definition revision. Whenever you configure the ECS service to use a new task definition or new revision of a task definition, this will always trigger a new deployment of the ECS service. If we select the service and click on the Update button, notice we can update the task definition to the new revision. If we now click on the Update Service button and then click View Service and select the Deployments tab, notice that we have two deployments, the current active deployment and the new deployment as designated by the primary status. We can see that this has immediately changed to a pending status as ECS is attempting to start a new container based upon the new ECS task definition configuration. Once the new container starts successfully, ECS will terminate the previous container, and we have successfully deployed a new revision of our application. If we switch back to our SSH session and run the docker ps command, notice that a new instance of our application started just a minute or so ago, and notice that we now have a dynamic port mapping on our ECS container instance of port 32768, verifying the configuration change we made has been successfully deployed.

Creating an ECS Task

We've seen how to create an ECS service to create long-running containers that run applications, but sometimes we need to be able to run ad hoc tasks that perform sit up or deployment tasks or perhaps execute a periodic scheduled job that completes within a finite amount of time. We have already seen an example of this with our sample microtrader application where we need to run database migrations for the audit service whenever we deploy the application, and this is something we will configure later on when we create our full production environment. For now though, I want to demonstrate how to create an ECS task using the dashboard and will actually just run another instance of the quote generator service as a simple example. To run a new ECS task, we select the microtrader-test cluster and select the Tasks tab. Notice that we already have a single task running, and this is the task associated with our ECS service. To create a new task, we click the Run new Task button, and you can see that we have similar but different options when compared to creating an ECS service. We can choose a task definition, cluster, and number of tasks, and notice in the Advanced Options section, we can specify container overrides, which allow us to override the command string and/or environment variables passed to the container. Now one fairly amusing environment variable we can configure is the MARKET_PERIOD variable, which defines the frequency at which quotes are generated in milliseconds. The default value is 3000, meaning new quotes are generated every 3 seconds, but we can play a certain amount of havoc on the system by setting this to a much lower value. We'll set this to something really crazy, 1 ms, and then click Run Task to execute the task on our ECS cluster. You can see that the new task is created with a status of PENDING, and this will transition to running. If we switch back to our ECS container instance ssh session, you can see the new container running with a new random host port. If we run the docker stats command, notice that our new task is consuming a significant amount of CPU from our T2 micro instance as we are now generating quotes every single millisecond. If you leave this task running for a few minutes and then switch back to the microtrader-test cluster in the ECS dashboard, notice on the Metrics tab that cluster CPU utilization is now hitting 100%. We'll use this somewhat crazy configuration knob in later modules to invoke and test ECS autoscaling, which will rely on such metrics to raise CloudWatch alarms and trigger autoscaling events. Now that we've wreaked complete havoc with our system, let's return things to normal and just to be sure, we will select the Stop All button, which will kill all tasks in associated containers. If you refresh a few times, notice that the ECS task for our ECS service starts back up automatically, whilst our evil ECS task has disappeared forever. This is because ECS will always attempt to ensure the configured ECS service instance count is always maintained unlike single-shot ECS tasks, which once complete or killed will never be executed again. We can also verify this behavior from the ECS container instance command line by using the docker kill command to kill the container associated with the ECS service. If we run docker ps a few times, notice within a few seconds that ECS will automatically start a new container for the ECS service, ensuring the configured ECS service instance count is maintained. Before we wrap up this module, let's clean up our environment and destroy our ECS cluster by switching to the CloudFormation console, selecting our stack, and clicking on Actions, Delete Stack, which will complete within a few minutes. Note that you will also need to manually delete the ECS cluster from the ECS dashboard as the ECS Create Cluster wizard creates the cluster outside of the CloudFormation stack. To do this, we first need to update the ECS service count to 0 and then delete the ECS service by clicking the Delete button on the View Service screen. After the ECS service has been deleted, we can reselect the ECS cluster, click on the Delete Cluster button, and finally confirm the delete operation.

Summary

In this module, we were introduced to the EC2 container service, discussing the ECS architecture and the various components that comprise ECS. We first created ECR repositories and then published Docker images, integrating the release pipeline workflow included with the sample application. We next created an ECS cluster with a single ECS container instance and defined an ECS task definition for one of our sample application microservices. We created an ECS service, which defines one or more instances of an ECS task definition that should be running in a given ECS cluster and learned how ECS provides automated rolling deployments whenever our ECS service is updated with a new ECS task definition or task definition revision. Finally, we learned how we can schedule ad hoc ECS tasks to our ECS clusters, which provide us with an ability to run deployment, operational, and scheduled tasks as required. Now that we have a basic understanding of ECS fundamentals, in the next module, we will learn how to customize our ECS container instances to add core operational features required for a production- grade environment, including CloudWatch logs integration and proxy support.

Customizing ECS Container Instances

Introduction

Hi, my name is Justin Menga, and welcome to Customizing ECS Container Instances. In the previous module, we were introduced to the EC2 Container Service and built a simple ECS cluster that consisted of a single ECS container instance based from the Amazon ECS-optimized Amazon Machine Image, or AMI, which is published and updated regularly by AWS. Out of the box, the Amazon ECS-optimized AMI provides the basic components needed to run Docker applications using ECS. However, in a production use case, you will typically need to add some additional customizations to support common features, such as login support and working with HTTP Proxies. In this module, we will learn how to customize ECS container instances using the Amazon ECS-optimized AMI as our base runtime environment, and learn how we can use EC2 instance initialization mechanisms to configure and verify our ECS container instances and instance creation time. We will use a popular tool called Packer to add additional features and customizations to our ECS container instances, and install and configure the CloudWatch Logs agent, which forwards operating system and application logs to the AWS CloudWatch Logs service. We will also add support for secure communication to AWS and other internet services using an HTTP Proxy. Finally, we will configure ECS container instance health checks that will verify at instance creation time that our ECS container instances have successfully registered to the configured ECS cluster. We will then proceed to build our Amazon Machine Image and publish it to EC2 for use in future modules.

Custom Amazon Machine Image Design

A strategy I recommend, especially if you are new to ECS, is to start with the Amazon ECS-optimized AMI as your base AMI, which already includes Docker, the ECS agent, and base configuration settings designed to optimally work with the EC2 Container Service. We have already worked with this AMI in the previous module, and at a minimum, all you need to do to use this AMI is configure the ECS agent to join the correct ECS cluster at instance creation time. We saw, in the previous module, that the Create ECS Cluster Wizard does this by using the EC2 UserData mechanism, and this represents the simplest way to get started configuring ECS container instances. In a production real-world use case, however, you will typically need support for additional features, such as installing appropriate login and monitoring agents, modifying base configuration settings, or configuring secure internet communications using an HTTP Proxy. Rather than making all of these changes at instance creation time using UserData, a much better approach is to create or bake your own AMI, and using the Amazon ECS-optimized AMI as a base image allows you to leverage much of the work already done for you of installing and configuring Docker in the ECS agent and just focus on the specific modifications you need to make. To demonstrate this approach, we will create our own custom AMI, installing the CloudWatch Logs agent to support login to the AWS CloudWatch Logs service, and make some static configuration changes to the base configuration of the source AMI, including setting an appropriate timezone, enabling the NTP service in configuring additional Docker configuration settings. We will also create a first run script, which provides the ability to dynamically configure the ECS container instance at instance creation time. This will enable us to configure the correct ECS cluster to join, configure the correct CloudWatch Log groups created for the application, configure an HTTP Proxy for the environment where the ECS container instances will run, and also run health checks that verify the instances created and initialized successfully.

Understanding EC2 Instance Initialization

Before we get started customizing our ECS container instances, it is important to understand the EC2 instance initialization process, which provides hooks for configuring EC2 instances at instance creation time. If you have worked with AWS EC2 instances previously, you will likely be familiar with the concept of UserData, which provides a standard cloud-agnostic mechanism to perform instance configuration at instance creation time. A common use case for UserData is to provide a series of shell commands that configure the instance appropriately for a given application or environment. In addition to UserData, AWS also provides a proprietary mechanism called CloudFormation Init, or cfn-init for short, which provides a richer, more powerful initialization framework over the standard UserData mechanism, and is integrated with CloudFormation, meaning you can integrate with CloudFormation, create, update and delete lifecycle events. For example, you can configure instance creation policies in CloudFormation that will wait until EC2 instances send success signals to CloudFormation using the CloudFormation Init mechanism, ensuring any resources that depend on your EC2 instances are not created or updated until your EC2 instances have initialized successfully. The standard approach to use CloudFormation Init is to use UserData as an entry point to invoke cfn-init, perform all of your instance creation configuration logic using the cfn-init framework, and then call a CloudFormation Init helper tool, called cfn-signal, to signal success or failure to CloudFormation. In later modules, we will use this approach, leveraging the first run script embedded in our custom AMI, and for now, the focus of this module is to build our custom machine image, but it's important to understand the bigger picture of how our first run script will be invoked.

Using Packer to Build Amazon Machine Images

A popular tool for building Amazon Machine Images is the Packer tool, which is an open source tool published by HashiCorp. This module will only provide a basic introduction to Packer and how to use it specifically to build Amazon Machine Images. However, if you want to learn more about Packer, you can check out the Pluralsight course, Designing Immutable Infrastructure with Packer. We first need to install Packer, and you can find installation instructions for your operating system at packer. io. Since I'm using a Mac, I will use Homebrew to install Packer by running the brew install packer command, and once complete, we are ready to start using Packer. Packer provides a modular architecture, and core building blocks include builders, which are responsible for generating the low-level machine image format, and provisioners, which are responsible for performing operating system level provisioning tasks such as installing operating system packages or adding files to the machine image. Each Packer build is defined in a template, which is a JSON file that defines builders, provisioners and other components of the Packer build system. For our use case, we will be leveraging the Amazon EC2 AMI Builder, and to provision the operating system included with our AMI, we will be using shell and file provisioners, which are the most basic and low-level provisioners available. Note that Packer supports configuration management tools, such as Puppet and Ansible, for more advanced and scalable provisioning. However, for our use case, the shell and file provisioners are sufficient.

Creating a Packer Template

Now that we have a basic understanding of the Packer architecture, let's get started creating a Packer template. We will create a new repository called packer-ecs in the course root folder, and at the root of this folder create a file called packer. json. Our template is a JSON document and will consist of four top-level objects, including a variables object, which provides input variables for our build, a builders array, which provides an array of Packer builders, a provisioners array, which provides a list of Packer provisioners, and finally, a post-processors array, which provides an array of Packer post-processor definitions that allow us to perform post-processing tasks after our machine image build has completed. We'll get started by adding a single Packer builder, which we define as the EBS-backed AMI builder by specifying a type of amazon-ebs. Each builder has specific mandatory and optional configuration properties that control how the builder will create your machine image, and you can refer to the Packer documentation for a complete list of all configuration options. For the amazon-ebs builder, we need to specify a number of properties, including AWS access credentials, and note that because we are using an assume role strategy for access to our AWS account, we need to be able to provide temporary STS credentials, including an AWS access key, secret key and session token. Because the EC2 service is regional, we also need to provide an AWS region and the ID of a source AMI available in the region, which we will be using as a starting point for our custom machine image. For our use case, the source AMI will be the Amazon ECS-optimized AMI and you can retrieve the most recent AMI IDs for your region from the URL shown. We next need to specify an instance type that should be used to build and provision the AMI along with the SSH username that Packer will use to access the instance during the build and provisioning process, which must be set to ec2-user when using the Amazon ECS-optimized AMI as our source machine image. If you are working remotely over the internet, you will also need to ensure that the associate_public_ip_address variable is set to true, which ensures the EC2 instance used by Packer can be reached over the internet. Finally, we can configure a name for the AMI using the ami_name variable and can specify tags that will be added to the final AMI that is published to AWS. Now that we've defined the variables required for our build, we need to consider how we will provide inputs for each variable. A simple approach is to hard-code each value directly as I have already demonstrated for most variables. However, for some variables, such as the credentials variables, this approach is not very scalable due to the temporary nature of the AWS credentials we will be using. To make it easier to work with AWS credentials, we will configure credentials using environment variables, and we can configure these using the variables section in our template. Packer is written in Go and uses Go templates, and notice we can use the env action followed by the environment variable name to inject credentials from the environment. In the builders section, we can reference our variables by specifying the user action and referencing the variable name, so the configuration shown will inject AWS credentials from the environment into variables, which will then be injected into the related builder configuration properties.

Adding Packer Provisioning Tasks

Now that we have defined how Packer should build our Amazon Machine Image, we need to add provisioners that will customize our image appropriately. The first customization we will perform will be to set the timezone and enable the Network Time Protocol, or NTP client, for our instance, and to do this, we will use the shell provisioner and reference a script that we will create shortly. We will also pass an environment variable to our script that can be used to set the timezone, allowing us to configure the timezone as a top-level variable in our template. Let's now create the reference script in the path shown, and this needs to be defined as a shell script using the dash again. In general, we should always configure set -e to ensure our scripts will exit with an error code if any command in the script fails, and we will declare a timezone variable based from the timezone environment variable that has been injected by the shell provisioner. It's important to understand that all shell provisioning actions are performed in the context of the SSH user specified in the template. So, in our use case, this is the EC2 user, and consequently, we need to use sudo to perform any administrative actions that require root level privileges. Here we use the sudo tee command to configure the timezone as recommended for AWS Linux, and finally, ensure the NTP client service is set to start on boot.

Configuring the ECS Agent

One mandatory requirement that you must perform at ECS container instance creation time is configuration of the ECS agent to register to the correct ECS cluster. We saw in the previous module that you can simply echo the ECS cluster name to the ecs. config file in the instance UserData. However, since we will be using CloudFormation Init to perform all instance creation tasks, we will take a different approach to performing this configuration by creating a first run script that, along with a number of other configuration tasks, will configure the ECS agent at instance creation time. We will create a new file provisioner that copies a script called firstrun which, as the name suggests, is intended to be executed at instance creation time. It's important that you understand this distinction. We are not running the firstrun script when we build the AMI. Instead we are copying the script to the AMI file system so it can be called at instance creation time. In the firstrun shell script, notice that we echo the value of an environment variable called ECS_CLUSTER to the ecs. config file. So, bear in mind for later modules, in addition to calling the script at instance creation time using CloudFormation Init, we must also set an environment variable of the ECS cluster that we want the ECS container instance to join.

Customizing Docker

In some use cases, you may want to change the default Docker Engine configuration that shifts with the Amazon ECS-optimized AMI. A good example of this is for our sample Micro-Trader Application, which includes an Application Discovery mechanism that can be challenging to get working in a Docker environment due to the various networking constraints that the default Docker network configuration can introduce. The underlying application framework used for the Micro-Trader Application is Vert. x, which uses a component called Hazelcast for discovery of application instances distributed across the network to form Vert. x clusters. The default discovery mechanism is to use multicast networking, which worked fine in earlier modules when we ran the Micro-Trader Application in a local Docker environment as this discovery occurred across an internal network created by Docker Compose that all application containers were attached to on a single Docker host. If we shift this module to a multi-host Docker environment, things start to break. Cluster discovery fundamentally is about dynamically determining the IP address and TCP or UDP endpoints of each cluster member, and the network address translation performed by the default Docker network configuration commonly breaks cluster discovery mechanisms. Here we can see Container A advertises its internal IP address in a cluster discovery message that is sent to the network. However, because Host A and Host B are separate Docker hosts, the network has no knowledge of the internal docker0 IP address on Host A, and the cluster discovery mechanism fails. One solution to address these networking issues is to enable Docker host networking. When we enable host networking, Container A shares the networking stack of host A and, therefore, can be addressed directly using the Docker host IP address. This means that Container A will now advertise the IP address of Host A in the cluster discovery message, allowing Container B to successfully respond back to the Container A cluster endpoint, and the cluster discovery process can now complete successfully. Now, it's important to understand that the majority of applications work perfectly well with the default Docker networking configuration, and using host networking does introduce some constraints on how you can implement Docker. First of all, you cannot use the Docker user namespaces feature with host networking, so your containers should always run non-root users as a security best practice. Secondly, if you are deploying containers for a wide variety of applications on shared Docker hosts, in other words, in more of a platform as a service model, using host networking will not scale well given containers will be competing for shared TCP and UDP port address space. In our use case, we are running our containers with non-root users and using more of a traditional infrastructure as a service model, where our Docker hosts are dedicated to specific applications, meaning host networking is a viable configuration for our scenario. When configuring host networking, you typically will want to completely remove the Docker bridge on your Docker hosts as running containers in host networking mode exposes all host operating network interfaces to the container, meaning unless you remove the default docker0 bridge, you can end up with both the docker0 bridge interface and operating system network interface in your containers, and this can confuse the container as to which interface it should use. To configure host networking rather than hard-code host networking settings into our machine image, ideally we should support the ability to run in host networking mode as a decision that is made when an instance is created as part of the firstrun shell script. We first check for an environment variable called DOCKER_NETWORK_MODE, and if this variable is set to a value of host, then we will configure the Docker Engine to run in host networking mode. In the Amazon ECS-optimized AMI, the /etc/sysconfig/docker file provides an option setting that is used to configure additional flags that will be passed to the Docker Engine. Here we use a sed command to add a number of configuration options that ensure the bridge ip-forward, iptables and ip-masq settings are effectively disabled or removed. We then stop the Docker service and remove the var/lib/docker/network folder which holds the Docker network database. We need to do this to force Docker to recreate the network database when it next starts. And, finally, we remove the docker0 bridge interface from the operating system so that it will not be exposed to our containers.

CloudWatch Logs Integration

CloudWatch Logs is an AWS service designed to aggregate and store logs from your applications and hosts in a central location, with the ability to forward logs to other destinations via log subscriptions using AWS Lambda and other AWS services. You can use the CloudWatch Logs service to collect ECS container instance logs as well as Docker container logs, and at the ECS container instance level, you will typically want to collect operating system, Docker Engine and ECS agent logs. To collect ECS container instance logs, you must install a CloudWatch Logs agent and configure the agent to stream the various types of logs to a log group. Each ECS container instance streams logs to a log stream, commonly named according to the EC2 instance ID, allowing support teams to view logs at an individual host level by filtering by log stream, and also at an aggregate level by filtering by log group. To install the CloudWatch Logs agent, we will create a new provisioner that references a script called install-os-packages, and you can see that the script simply installs a CloudWatch Logs agent using the yum install command. Configuration of the CloudWatch Logs agent is documented at the URL shown, and the nature of the configuration required is to configure the logs agent at instance creation time, hence we will add these configuration tasks to the firstrun script we created earlier in this module. In the script, we write to the referenced awscli. conf configuration file to configure the CloudWatch Logs region, and notice this will expect an environment variable called AWS_DEFAULT_REGION to be configured. We then write out the general AWS Logs configuration to the referenced awslogs. conf file, and here we define a standard suite of log groups that we will post logs to from the local operating system. In total, we define five log groups, one per local operating system log file type, including standard operating system logs as well as Docker logs and ECS agent logs. Notice that we are using a standard convention for defining the log group name, and again, this relies on environment variables to inject the CloudFormation STACK_NAME and AUTOSCALING_GROUP name. For the log_stream_name setting, we reference a special placeholder called instance_id, and the CloudWatch Logs agent will automatically set the stream name to the local EC2 instance ID. After writing the configuration file, we need to actually start the CloudWatch Logs agent as it is not started by default after installation. Again, it is important to remember that the firstrun script is only run at EC2 instance creation time and not actually run during the AMI build process, so we explicitly need to start the CloudWatch Logs agent inside our firstrun script.

HTTP Proxy Support

A security best practice I strongly recommend is, where possible, to deploy your application servers in private subnets that do not have any direct internet connectivity. This approach does not mean your application is not accessible to or from the internet. It simply means that inbound or outbound internet access must be provided via intermediary systems, such as an Application Load Balancer for inbound access and an HTTP Proxy for outbound access. Using a Proxy for outbound access allows you to, at the very least, restrict external services your application can access, protecting your application from malicious content and attempts to control or steal data from your systems by exploiting open outbound internet access. When using a Proxy, we need to ensure that all applications that require internet access support Proxy operation, and we also need to understand how to configure each application to use an HTTP Proxy. In our use case, we need to ensure the ECS agent can communicate with the EC2 Container Service, our Docker Engine can pull images from the EC2 Container Registry, and that the CloudWatch Logs agent can communicate with the CloudWatch Logs service. All of these AWS services are accessible only via public endpoints, so any ECS container instances located on private subnets will need to use an HTTP Proxy to reach the various AWS service API endpoints. When configuring support for an HTTP Proxy, we don't really want to hard-code the Proxy URL into our machine image, so configuring the HTTP Proxy is best configured at instance creation time via the firstrun script we've already created. In the firstrun script, I check for the existence of an environment variable called PROXY_URL and, if present, we first configure the HTTPS_PROXY setting in /etc/sysconfig/docker, which will configure the Docker Engine to use the Proxy URL when pulling images from the EC2 Container Registry. To configure Proxy support for the ECS agent, we first set the HTTPS_PROXY setting to the appropriate Proxy URL in the ECS agent configuration file, and also configure the NO_PROXY option to ensure ECS agent attempts to communicate with the EC2 Metadata Service and Docker Engine bypass the configured Proxy URL. Finally, to configure Proxy support for the CloudWatch Logs agent, we configure the HTTP and HTTPS Proxy settings in the referenced proxy. conf configuration file, and also configure the NO_PROXY option to ensure the CloudWatch Logs agent does not attempt to use the Proxy when communicating with the EC2 Metadata Service to obtain IAM role credentials. Now, there is one more modification we need to make to our machine image and this relates to the default cloud-init configuration that is included with the base Amazon ECS-optimized AMI. Cloud-init is the industry standard mechanism for initializing cloud-based virtual machines, and the UserData mechanism that we have already seen in action is part of the cloud-init framework. In the Amazon ECS-optimized AMI, the default configuration is to run a yum package manager update at instance creation time, and this happens before UserData is invoked, which can cause problems if your instance is located in a private network that requires HTTP Proxy configuration injected via the UserData mechanism. Without any means to communicate with the internet, the yum update process will eventually timeout after several retries, significantly increasing the startup time of your ECS container instances. To address this issue, the simplest approach is to actually disable the yum package manager update in the default cloud-init configuration. To do this, I create a new shell provisioner, which references a script called cloud-init-options, and in this script I use the sed command to modify the default cloud-init configuration and ensure any package manager updates or upgrades are disabled.

ECS Container Instance Health Checks

When creating complete environments using AWS CloudFormation, it is important to be able to verify our ECS container instances have configured and initialized successfully. The CloudFormation Init framework provides the ability to signal CloudFormation when our ECS container instance has successfully completed initial configuration, and all we need to do is add the appropriate health checks to verify our instance has initialized successfully. Because these health checks will be a runtime check at instance creation, we will add these health checks to our firstrun script. You may recall from the previous module, the ECS agent offers a local introspection endpoint that allows us to confirm whether or not the ECS agent has successfully registered to the ECS cluster, and this is a very useful health check to perform as, once the agent has registered to the cluster, it is ready to start running ECS services and tasks. To implement the health check, we add a loop in our script using the until statement, which curls the ECS agent endpoint, extracts the cluster value from the return JSON output, and verify this matches the ECS cluster environment variable which will be passed in from our CloudFormation stack via CloudFormation Init. We repeat this check every five seconds until the ECS agent registers successfully. And in the case something goes wrong and this never happens, the script will continue looping on the health check until CloudFormation times out waiting for instance creation, and at this point, CloudFormation would typically roll back and destroy the ECS container instance. Finally, because we are using the jq command in our health check, we need to ensure the jq package is installed in our machine image, so here we add jq to the packages variable in the install-os-packages script. One other important package we also need to add is the aws-cfn-bootstrap package which adds the CloudFormation Init tools, including cfn-init and cfn-signal, which will be used to download CloudFormation Init Metadata and signal CloudFormation the success or failure of our firstrun script.

Post Build Cleanup

The final task we need to perform in our Packer template is a cleanup task that wipes any transient state so that any instances created from our image will always start with a clean slate. In our Packer template, we create a shell provisioner that references a script called cleanup, and we first stop and disable the Docker service so that we can safely perform initial setup tasks at instance creation. We next remove any Docker and ECS logs that may have been generated during the image build process, and we also remove the Docker socket file which Docker will automatically recreate when the Docker service is next started. Recall earlier in this module, when configuring support for Docker host networking mode, we configured the firstrun script to stop the Docker service and remove the var/lib/docker/network folder to ensure the default docker0 bridge interface is removed. Rather than perform this on instance first run, we can actually move these commands to our post-build cleanup script, meaning the initial state of our ECS image will already have the docker0 bridge interface removed. In the event we don't enable host networking mode at instance startup, Docker will automatically create the docker0 bridge interface, so this approach works regardless of whether or not you enable host networking mode. These post-build cleanup tasks mean we need to ensure we explicitly enable and start the Docker Engine at instance creation time, so we need to add these actions to our firstrun script. Here we re-enable and start the Docker service, and we also need to start the ECS agent after the Docker service has been started.

Building and Publishing the Image

We have to find our Packer template, and are ready to build and publish our custom ECS container instance image. Before we run the build, I am going to add a post-processor action which will write a manifest recording the built AMI artifact IDs to a file called manifest. json. Recall when we first created our Packer template, we defined environment variables for the various AWS credentials that Packer needs to build and publish our image. Packer does not currently support AWS IAN role assumption, so we need to assume the role ourselves and obtain temporary session credentials for the assumed role from the AWS ECS service. To do this, we can run the aws sts command which will generate temporary session credentials that we can then configure in the local environment. After setting these environment variables, we can run our Packer build script using the command packer build and then referencing our Packer template file. Instead of repeating this manual process each time we build the image, we can add a Makefile to our repository that automatically assumes the role and injects the temporary session credentials into the Packer build process. Here I use a Make conditional function to call an assume_role function if the AWS_PROFILE environment is set. This function uses the eval Make function to dynamically inject the temporary session credentials into the environment of the Make build task, using the handy jq utility to extract the various fields of the AWS CLI JSON output to appropriate environment variable values. If we run make build, a set of temporary credentials is returned. These credentials are injected into the call to the Packer build command, ensuring Packer has appropriate access to our AWS account and resources. If you inspect the output of the Packer build process, notice that Packer first creates a new instance from the source AMI, and then attempts to SSH to the instance using the SSH username configured in the Packer template. Once the SSH session is established, Packer executes each of the provisioner tasks one by one, and assuming each task completes without any issues, the Packer build completes successfully, creating a new Amazon Machine Image which is then published to AWS for wider use. Notice that the Packer output includes the new AMI ID, and if we inspect the local manifest. json file, you can see this information is also recorded by the manifest post-processor. If we switch over to the AWS Console, open the EC2 Dashboard, and select AMIs from the Images section, you should be able to see your newly created Amazon Machine Image with the correct AMI ID, confirming we have successfully published our machine image to AWS. We can launch a new EC2 instance from the image by clicking the Launch button, selecting the default options, and finally confirming our EC2 key pair and launching the instance. After a few seconds, a link to your new instance will be presented, and if we click on the instance link, we can see our new EC2 instance that has been created. If you wait for a minute or so, a public IP address will be allocated to the instance, and if we copy the IP address and SSH to the instance, we can inspect our machine image to verify the changes made by the Packer build have been implemented. Our firstrun script should be present in the EC2-user home directory and recall the script checks if the environment variable DOCKER_NETWORK_MODE is configured with a value of host, which enables host networking and removes the default Docker bridge interface. If we set this environment variable and run the firstrun script, we can verify host networking is working as expected by running the docker run command and creating an alpine Linux container in host networking mode. Notice that the ifconfig command output can only see the host if zero interface, and that the default docker0 interface has been successfully removed. Before we wrap up this module, let's terminate our EC2 instance, and now is a good time to create a Git repository for the Packer ECS project. After creating the repository, we commit our changes, and assuming you have created a repository in GitHub, push the changes to your GitHub repository.

Summary

In this module, we learned how to customize ECS container instances using the popular Packer tool to apply a series of operating system changes, and also created a first run script, which is run at instance creation time to configure our ECS container instances appropriately for their specific use case and environment. We learned how to customize Docker Engine options, install and configure the CloudWatch Logs agent, and enable secure communications to AWS services and the internet by supporting configuration of an HTTP Proxy at instance creation time. We introduced a basic health check into our first run script which verifies our ECS container instance has successfully registered to the correct ECS cluster, and finally, we built and published our Amazon Machine Image to AWS. You can use the various techniques discussed in this module to add your own customizations. For example, you could add monitoring agents or perform security hardening of the operating system environment. Our machine image is a core foundational component for enabling application platforms that use the EC2 Container Service and is designed to be generic enough to be used with any stack that includes ECS. In coming modules, we will use our image to not just create the Micro-Trader Application stacks, but also to help create an HTTP Proxy stack that our Micro-Trader ECS container instances can use to communicate securely with AWS services and the internet.

Deploying AWS Infrastructure Using Ansible and CloudFormation

Introduction

Hi, my name is Justin Menga, and welcome to deploying AWS Infrastructure using Ansible and CloudFormation. We have created Docker release images for our sample application, established access to Amazon web services and created a custom Amazon machine image that is ready to support deployment of the sample micro trader application using the EC2 container service. However before we can deploy the sample application we need core foundational infrastructure in place and need to consider our overall AWS deployment strategy. As an example of such infrastructure although we could run our application on the default VPC and subnets provided by AWS, in a production use case you will typically want to structure your VPC and subnets into security zones that help meet the security requirements of your organization and use private subnets to increase the security of your applications. At the very least you ideally will want to define all of your infrastructure configuration using an infrastructure as code approach that is under source code and version control rather than relying on infrastructure manually created and changed through the AWS console. In this module you will learn how to create and deploy shared infrastructure to your AWS account using best practice patterns and templates that you can use in many AWS scenarios to support your applications. We will leverage the power of CloudFormation and Ansible to create a standard approach to creating all AWS infrastructure that is defined in CloudFormation templates and orchestrated by Ansible playbooks. We will create core network infrastructure, supporting resources for the AWS CloudFormation key management service and Lambda services. Create EC2 container registry repositories and deploy an HTTP proxy stack based upon the custom Amazon machine image we created in the last module. The best thing about everything you learn in this module is that we will be creating a consistent and reusable methodology to define and deploy AWS resources for a wide variety of use cases. Basically anything that can be defined in CloudFormation.

AWS Deployment Strategy

AWS offers a large number of infrastructure and application services providing the building blocks of delivering applications in the Cloud. Examples of such services include the EC2 container service for running Docker containers, application load balancers for load balancing incoming requests to your applications, auto scaling groups that allow your applications to scale up and down on demand, the relational database service or RDS for hosting your application database, identity and access management and I've just only really scratched the surface here. Deploying and managing each of these services individually can very quickly become unwieldy and unmanageable and one key service that AWS provides to help manage collections of AWS services and resources is CloudFormation. CloudFormation allows you to organize AWS services and resources into stacks with each resource codified in a CloudFormation template allowing you to declare all resources and services collectively, required to deliver an application and its supporting services, security controls and operational requirements. Using CloudFormation makes it easier to manage and operate each application stack with the ability to centrally create, update and delete each stack on demand and to apply stack wide tags to all stack resources making it easier to identify your applications, resources and services for operational and billing purposes. Another key deployment technology is Ansible which provides the ability to orchestrate complex deployment scenarios. It includes a vast array of modules which can facilitate almost any deployment action you can think of. Now although Ansible can manage many of the AWS services I have listed individually I believe it makes much more sense to leverage native AWS management tools where possible to manage AWS infrastructure, especially in the collective manner provided by CloudFormation and instead use Ansible as your overarching orchestration tool.

AWS Account Infrastructure Architecture

Before we start defining our AWS infrastructure using Ansible and CloudFormation it's important to understand how we will structure the various AWS resources that our AWS account will require. As a best practice principle I recommend encoding as many resources as possible in CloudFormation templates, defined in an Ansible playbook structure located within a Git Repository. And this approach allows us to manage and deploy our AWS resources in a consistent and reusable manner with the powerful version control and code review capabilities of Git. When we consider the resources a given AWS account might require there will always be resources specific to your applications. However we can typically define a set of common shared resources regardless of the type of applications that will be running in that account. And in this module we will define these common resources and Ansible playbooks with an associated CloudFormation template. The first set of common resources is defined in the security playbook which typically consists of a suite of standard IAM roles, policies and other security related resources. For this course we won't be using any additional security resources other than our admin IAM role. So we won't be creating the stack but in a production use case this is something you will typically need to create. The next set of resources is defined in the CloudFormation resources playbook which defines a set of resources used to support CloudFormation operations. At a minimum this consists of a couple of S3 buckets and an AWS key management service or KMS key. The first S3 bucket is used to upload CloudFormation templates which is required when your CloudFormation templates exceed a certain size. Whilst the other S3 bucket is used for storing Lambda function code which is required to support custom CloudFormation resources. I also create a shared KMS key that can be used for encrypting and decrypting sensitive data in your CloudFormation stacks. And we will learn how to leverage this for a simple yet effective secret management strategy in later modules. If you are deploying Docker based applications another set of common resources is defined in the EC2 container registry playbook which defines each of the various ECR repositories and associated permissions for your account. We've already created these repositories manually for our micro trader application however we will migrate each of these repositories to our CloudFormation and playbook structure later on in this module. The next set of resources defines the various networking components that provide connectivity for your applications. This is a critical set of resources that defines VPC's, subnets, internet gateways, route 53 domains and more. The final set of shared resources defines an HTTP proxy stack which delivers a web proxy service that your application instances can leverage for secure outbound internet communications. Later on in this module we will define the stack using a pre-defined template that consists of the popular open-source squid proxy running in Docker containers. And this will be deployed using the EC2 container service and the custom Amazon machine image we built in the previous module. At this point your AWS account has all of the supporting resources and infrastructure to begin defining and deploying applications that are specific to your organization's products. And this often defines the operational boundary between a central Cloud operations group and your product teams that develop and operate product specific applications.

Ansible Playbooks

Ansible provides a flexible framework for orchestrating complex deployment and operational tasks. And a core component of this framework is the Ansible playbook which defines a set of related deployment actions that achieve a higher level outcome. When using Ansible with CloudFormation I recommend an approach where each playbook revolves around a single CloudFormation template located within a single Git Repository that targets one or more environments, with each environment being a CloudFormation stack generated from the CloudFormation template in environment-specific settings located in the playbook. This means the Ansible playbook and its associated CloudFormation template becomes a unit of deployment and version control and it's important to separate your playbooks accordingly. One key requirement for each of these playbooks is the ability to support multiple environments. For example your AWS network playbook might define networking infrastructure for different VPC environments across one or more accounts whilst a playbook for deploying a web application might define development and test environments in a non-production account and staging and production environments in a production account. As we will learn in this module the flexible playbook structure we will adopt allows you to define multiple environments that may exist in a single account or across multiple accounts depending on the specific requirements and account structure of your organization.

Ansible Playbook Structure

When it comes to structuring your playbooks Ansible provides a convention over configuration approach that is described in the playbook best practices document on the Ansible website. And the playbook structure we will adopt in this course very much follows the approaches recommended by Ansible. The example playbook shown on screen is based from a starter repository that we will fork shortly but for now I will talk you through the structure of the playbook. The Ansible. cfg file provides a list of Ansible default settings to apply when the playbook is executed. For example specifying where the inventory file is located and where Ansible roles, which we will talk about shortly, should be enstored in relation to the playbook structure. Each environment is defined in the inventory file as named configuration keys. And the group vars folder stores global and environment specific configuration settings. Each environment is represented as a named sub-folder under the group vars folder with the sub-folder name corresponding to the environments defined in the inventory file whilst the group vars all sub-folder contains global configuration settings that will be applied globally across all environments. If you are familiar with Ansible it's important to understand the inventory does not define hosts or groups of hosts which is traditionally how Ansible has used the inventory for configuration management. And instead each inventory item represents an environment that includes all of the various AWS resources required to support the environment and its applications and services. The templates folder is where we define CloudFormation templates and the standard approach is to define these templates in a generic or base form with appropriate parametrization such that a CloudFormation stack definition for a given environment can be constructed from the base CloudFormation template and environment specific settings defined in the group vars folder. These templates are written in a YAML format but most importantly are Jinja templates. Meaning you can leverage powerful template generation techniques to transform and adapt your base template for each environment. The roles folder includes a file called requirements. yml which defines a list of Ansible roles that the playbook depends on. Ansible roles are a powerful feature that allow you to execute a common set of externally published reusable tasks in your playbooks without having to explicitly write these tasks yourself. Ansible roles also provide a mechanism to support powerful customization and extension of Ansible's functionality. For example later on in this module you'll be introduced to a technique of defining playbook settings using a dot notation style syntax which makes your configuration syntax very intuitive and easy to understand and it's important to mention the syntax is not supported natively by Ansible and is enabled by various customizations that have been added to the referenced Ansible roles. For our use case we will import several Ansible roles throughout this course which will perform common tasks like assuming an IAM role and injecting temporary session credentials and also interact with our playbook to generate a CloudFormation stack definition and upload that definition to the CloudFormation service to create, update, or delete our CloudFormation stacks. The site. yml file stores the primary playbook and consists of a series of plays which each contain a set of deployment tasks. Whenever you run your Ansible playbook you will reference this file and Ansible will execute each of the plays defined in the playbook. Notice that each play is referencing an Ansible role which are each defined in the roles requirements. yml file. However you can define additional plays as required depending on the specific deployment use case. One more important file that is not directly related to Ansible is the. gitignore file which is used to define which files are excluded from source control and your playbook repository. Typically I recommend excluding the Ansible roles as these should be treated as external dependencies that are installed on demand as defined in the requirements. yml file. And we also exclude a local folder called build which is created dynamically to store generated build artifacts when we run our Ansible playbook. Finally the starter repository includes a make file and supporting makefile. settings file which provide a suite of convenient make tasks that simplify executing the various actions you will perform with your playbook. As we progress through this module I will introduce the various native Ansible commands so that you have a solid understanding of how to use the Ansible tooling directly. However we will be using the make tasks throughout this course as they are much easier to use on a day to day basis.

Creating an Ansible Playbook

We've discussed our approach to defining infrastructure as code using CloudFormation and Ansible and described a common pattern for structuring and managing resources in your AWS accounts. Now let's get started implementing this approach by starting with the CloudFormation resources playbook which will define shared resources that support CloudFormation activities including S3 buckets and AWS KMS keys. We've already described the Ansible playbook structure that we will adopt for this course and rather than coding this by hand we can fork a starter playbook repository I have defined at the GitHub URL shown which you can use for all of the playbooks that we will define in this module and course. Once you have forked the starter repository we clone the repository locally to a folder named CloudFormation resources and create a new Git repository by removing the. git folder and running the command git init. When setting up the playbook for the first time the first step I typically perform is defining the target environments that I want this playbook to target. For shared resource playbooks each target environment is typically defined along AWS account boundaries and for our use case, given we only have a single AWS account, we will define a single environment called production in the inventory file. In the real world it is common to have production and non-production accounts and you could support this topology by defining a separate non-production environment that targets your separate non-production account as an example. If you are familiar with Ansible this use of the inventory file might seem a little strange at first. Traditionally the inventory file defines target hosts or systems that you want to provision but in our case we are provisioning complete Cloud environments. Notice that we define a single variable called Ansible connection which tells Ansible that when attempting to run Ansible tasks against this environment it should run these tasks on the local machine rather than attempt to connect to a host that has the name of your environment. To finish our environment definition we also need to create a folder per environment under the group vars folder and create a file called vars. yml in each environment sub-folder which will define configuration settings specific to each environment. Now at this point it's important to point out that the make file includes a convenient make environment task that adds a new environment to the inventory file and creates the corresponding environment in the group vars folder automatically for you. For example, we can run make environment/non-production to add a new environment called non-production and you can see that the environment has been added to both the inventory file and the group vars folder. The next step we need to take is to install the roles defined in the requirements. yml file located in the roles folder. This can be achieved by running the Ansible galaxy command which will install each role into the role's folder. Note that the starter repository includes a make roles task that runs the Ansible galaxy install command with the force flag so this is a much more convenient way to install or refresh your Ansible roles. If you take a look at the Ansible. cfg file in the playbook root you can see that this defines the default location where Ansible roles will be installed. If we take a look at the site. yml file this defines the various plays that will be executed when you run the Ansible playbook command. Notice the two plays are defined. One that calls the aws-sts role and another that calls the aws. cloudformation role which of course are the roles that we installed when we ran the Ansible galaxy install command. An important aspect of the site. yml file is that each play references an env variable in the host's directive. The env variable specifies the environment we want our playbook execution to target and this must reference a valid environment that we have defined in our inventory file and group vars folder. For example, if we want to target the production environment that we have just created for this playbook we must configure the env variable with a value of production when we execute the playbook. And we will see how to do this shortly.

Configuring the AWS STS Role

The AWS STS role provides the ability to assume a given role calling the AWS security token service using the AWS CLI and configures the temporary session credentials and an Ansible variable which we can then inject into the AWS CloudFormation role environment. This approach grants the AWS CloudFormation role the privileges of the assumed role and means the AWS CloudFormation role does not need to concern itself with performing role assumption tasks. An important aspect of the AWS STS role is that it uses the AWS CLI to assume a role. Meaning you can leverage the session credential caching features of the AWS CLI. This is especially useful when you are testing and running your playbooks locally and don't want to have to reenter your MFA code each time you run your playbooks. Note that the AWS STS role requires an input variable called STS. role to be configured which defines the IAM role that the AWS STS Ansible role should assume. The IAM role is very significant in that it not only determines the level of privileges that Ansible will be granted but also determines the target account with the various AWS resources defined in the CloudFormation stack will be created. Note that all of the various roles we will use in this module use a standard prefix to define their input and output variables. For example AWS STS role variables are prefixed with STS whilst the AWS CloudFormation role variables are prefixed with stack. And all other environment settings not related to an Ansible role are prefixed with config which makes it easier to understand the context of each variable in our playbook. To configure the AWS STS role we need to define the STS. role variable in the group vars production vars. yml file which defines our production environment settings. To test out our configuration let's comment out the AWS CloudFormation role in the site. yml file for now and if we run our playbook only the AWS STS role will be invoked. To run our playbook we need to run the Ansible playbook command referencing the site. yml playbook file and at a minimum finding our target environment by using the -e or extra variables flag which allows us to configure or override any variable that our playbook uses. Recall that we must specify the target environment at playbook execution time and we do this by setting the env variable to production in this example. If we execute the command you may be presented with a warning indicating Ansible found a group and host with the same name. This warning was introduced in Ansible 2. 3 and is nothing more than an annoyance but I have opened a pull request with Ansible to optionally suppress this warning so hopefully in Ansible 2. 5 you will be able to suppress the warning by adding a configuration setting to the Ansible. cfg file. Notice that the AWS STS role prompts us for an MFA code. And after entering a valid code the role completes successfully but doesn't provide us with any useful information. Let's run the playbook again but this time add a variable called debug set to true which will configure the AWS STS role to output additional debug information. Notice this time we are not prompted for an MFA code as our AWS STS role is calling the AWS STS assume role command and is leveraging the session credential caching features of the AWS CLI. Because we have set the debug variable to true the results of many of the tasks are displayed along with the variables output by the role. The STS. credentials variable is the primary variable we are interested in which defines a dictionary of environment variables containing the temporary session credentials of our assumed role. This variable is structured in such a way that it can be injected into the environment setting of subsequent plays and this is how the subsequent AWS CloudFormation role is configured in our site. yml file.

Configuring the AWS CloudFormation Role

We've set up our initial playbook structure and learned how the AWS STS role works. So now let's move onto defining our CloudFormation stack and execute the AWS CloudFormation role. If we take a look at the AWS CloudFormation role that we installed into the roles folder you can see this role is much more complex than the AWS STS role we previously looked at. At a minimum this role requires a variable called stack. name to be defined which configures the name of the CloudFormation stack that the role will create. We can configure this variable globally in the group vars or vars. yml file which will apply for all environments unless explicitly overridden by environment settings. Because each environment in our CloudFormation resources playbook will target a different AWS account we can configure a common stack name of CloudFormation resources. Meaning each environment or AWS account we target will have a stack called CloudFormation resources created when we run the playbook. Another important variable is the stack. template variable which defines the CloudFormation template that the AWS CloudFormation role will use to generate the CloudFormation stack. You can see in the AWS CloudFormation role in a. yml file that the internal representation of the stack. template variable is set to a file called stack. yml. j2, located in a templates folder of the playbook repository. So this means by convention the AWS CloudFormation role expects you to provide this file in your playbook and you can see that the start of playbook already includes a template in this location. At a minimum the stack. yml. j2 file can be defined as a pure CloudFormation template in either JSON or preferably the more compact and readable YAML format. One point to note is the. j2 extension of the file which is the standard extension for Jinja2 templates. Ansible uses Jinja2 as its templating engine which provides powerful template generation techniques we can leverage to generate CloudFormation stack definitions. Let's replace the starter sample template with a simple template that defines a single S3 bucket which will be used to store Lambda functions for custom CloudFormation resources. Notice that we use our first piece of Jinja markup which is to include the value of the env variable which defines our target environment in our stack description. We will learn how we can use much more powerful Jinja markup and templating techniques later in this module. But for now we'll keep our stack template simple. At this point we have configured everything we need to run our playbook and create our first CloudFormation stack. So let's try it out. After re-enabling the AWS CloudFormation play in the site. yml playbook file we run the Ansible playbook command specifying production as our target environment. Notice that the AWS CloudFormation role performs a number of tasks and we'll discuss some of the more important tasks shortly. Eventually the playbook will pause on a task called configure CloudFormation stack and at this point Ansible is communication with the AWS CloudFormation service, uploading our CloudFormation stack definition file and waiting for CloudFormation stack creation to complete. If we switch over to the AWS console and open the CloudFormation dashboard we can see our CloudFormation stack being created and the stack should complete creation fairly quickly.
