Once stack creation is complete our playbook completes successfully and if we switch to the S3 dashboard we can see our S3 bucket has been created by CloudFormation.

Understanding the AWS CloudFormation Role

Now that we have seen how easy it is to get started with using the AWS CloudFormation role let's take a deeper look at how the role works. At a high level the AWS CloudFormation role consists of two main actions. The first is to generate a CloudFormation stack definition and the second is to upload and deploy the generated stack definition to CloudFormation. A nice feature of the role is that we can pass a tag called generate to only run the template generation action. So if we rerun the playbook, this time passing the generate tag, notice that the playbook completes very quickly and skips the tasks related to the deployment of the CloudFormation stack. Note that the make file included in the repository comes with a convenient generate task. Meaning you can run the generation stage by simply running make generate slash environment name which under the hood runs the Ansible playbook command, passing in the generate tag automatically for you. If we take a look in our playbook folder structure notice that a folder called build has been created. And within the build folder separate sub-folders named according to timestamps are present. Each folder represents an invocation of your playbook. The first sub-folder is the timestamp when we ran the playbook for the first time and the second sub-folder is for our most recent playbook execution. These are essentially build folders that contain various artifacts used by the AWS CloudFormation role and let's take a look at each of these artifacts. The stack. yml file represents the generated CloudFormation stack definition in a human readable YAML format. This doesn't look too much different from our CloudFormation stack template however on closer inspection notice that the env variable has been replaced with production in the stack description, demonstrating that the stack definition here represents the output of the Jinja2 template with the various environment specific settings injected into the generated stack definition file. The stack. json file is a compact version of the stack. yml file and ultimately is what is uploaded to the CloudFormation servers. The policy. json file represents the CloudFormation stack policy applied when the stack was created or modified and the config. json file stores both stack policy and stack input parameters and this file is required if you are using the AWS code pipeline CloudFormation deployment action which we will learn about in later modules. The AWS CloudFormation role also provides a number of outputs which we can demonstrate by rerunning the playbook, this time removing the generate tag and setting the debug variable to true. Now before we examine the outputs notice that our playbook execution is idempotent. Because we did not change our stack template or environment settings the same CloudFormation template was generated and no change was required to the CloudFormation stack. In the debug output you can see that a variable called stack. facts is returned which includes information about the generated stack, including a list of all stack resources created. You can leverage these stack facts and subsequent provisioning tasks allowing for more advanced deployment scenarios as required.

Using AWS CloudFormation Role Templates

We've learned the basics of the AWS CloudFormation role and one powerful feature we haven't covered yet is the ability to leverage predefined templates published in the role. If we take a look at the AWS CloudFormation role notice a folder called templates which includes a number of predefined template files including one called cfn. yml. j2 which is designed to simplify the creation of shared CloudFormation resource stacks. Let's learn how we can leverage this template but before we can do this we need to delete the CloudFormation stack we have already created. The AWS CloudFormation role supports deletion of stacks by setting a variable called stack. delete to true. However instead of running the Ansible playbook command and explicitly setting this variable to true we can instead run the make delete environment command which will execute the playbook and automatically set the stack. delete variable to true for you. If we now actually execute the make delete command targeting the production environment our playbook will instruct CloudFormation to delete the stack and after a minute or two you should see the stack disappear from the AWS CloudFormation dashboard. To use the templates embedded in the AWS CloudFormation role in our playbook global settings we simply configure the stack. template variable to reference the relative path of the template file. Here we specify the path templates/cfn. yml. j2 which the role will resolve relative to the root path of the role. Now that we've referenced the role template let's take a closer look at the template to learn how we can use it to meet our specific requirements. You can see that the template creates two S3 buckets. One for storing Cloud formation templates and one for storing Lambda functions for CloudFormation custom resources. These resources don't leverage any environment specific settings and because S3 buckets must have globally unique names we reference the AWS account ID sudo parameter to create buckets named according to your AWS account ID. Below the Cfn lambda bucket resource notice we have some Jinja markup that conditionally adds an S3 bucket policy for the CFN lambda bucket resource. But only if the referenced variable is defined. The idea here is this variable defines a list of remote accounts that have read access to the bucket and you can see that we use a Jinja for loop to configure each remote account as a principle in the IAM policy statement. In our use case we are only using a single account however in a multi-account environment you may want to store and share your lambda function centrally with other accounts. The next resource in the template is the CFN master key resource which creates a KMS key intended to be used for managing secrets in CloudFormation stacks. We'll leverage this approach in later modules but for now notice that we can use the default Jinja2 filter to set default values for variables that are undefined. In this example we can configure an explicit role name to administer the KMS key but by default this will be set to a role named admin if the explicit role name is not defined. In addition the for loop shown will only be executed if the referenced variable is defined which allows you to add additional IAM roles to administer the KMS key as required. If we scroll down to the output section notice that the template includes a number of CloudFormation outputs and also includes support for a relatively recent feature called CloudFormation exports. CloudFormation exports allow you to export variables for use in other CloudFormation stacks as long as they are located within the same AWS account and region. This means you can create a standard naming convention for shared resource outputs and reference these standard names and other stacks without having to hard code the specific physical resource identifiers.

Creating the CloudFormation Resources Stack

Now that we've learned about the CloudFormation resources template and configured our playbook to user template let's actually run our playbook. Because we are just using a single AWS account we don't actually need to configure any of the configuration variables available in the template. So we are simply defining our stack name and setting the correct IAM role to assume for each environment. Note that the playbook structure provides the flexibility to configure these settings globally but at any time we can override these settings at an environment specific level if required. For example we could create an environment that uses a completely different approach to creating CloudFormation shared resources yet use the default approach for all other environments and this is the power and flexibility the playbook structure provides. Now at this point rather than running the Ansible playbook command directly we can introduce the make deploy command which is a task included in the local make file and just as we have seen with the other make tasks we can simply reference the target environment by running make deploy slash environment name which in this example is make deploy / production. After executing this command a CloudFormation stack definition based upon the role template is generated and the stack is then created in CloudFormation. Once the stack is created we can use the S3 dashboard to verify our two S3 buckets have been created and use the IAM dashboard to verify the KMS key has been created. And if we open the KMS key configuration settings we can verify the correct IAM resource policy has been applied.

Creating the EC2 Container Registry Stack

We've taken an in-depth look at how our Ansible playbooks are structured, how the AWS STS and CloudFormation roles work and how we can leverage role templates to create common types of resources with minimal configuration. One great feature of this approach is that you can reuse the approach for any set of AWS resources that can be defined in CloudFormation. We'll demonstrate this now by creating a playbook that defines our EC2 container registry repositories and to do this we'll first clone the CloudFormation startup playbook to a folder called ECR resources and create a new Git Repository in the ECR resources folder. We first define a single environment called production and then run make roles to install the required Ansible roles. After removing the default non-prod environment from the inventory file and group vars folders if we take a look at the AWS CloudFormation role templates folder we can see a template called ecr. yml. j2. Closer inspection of this template shows that we need to define a dictionary called config. repositories with each key in the dictionary defining the repository name. At a minimum we can just define an empty dictionary for each named repository however the template does provide the ability to define a list of read only accounts that can pull images as well as a list of read write accounts that can push or pull images from a given repository. In our use case we don't need to share our repositories with any other accounts so in our playbook production settings file, after configuring the correct IAM role, we define a top level variable called config. repositories and then specify each repository as a named dictionary with no additional settings. We also need to configure our stack name in the global settings file using the stack. name variable and ensure we reference the ECR template file by defining the stack. template variable. We are now ready to execute our playbook however before we can do this we first need to remove the ECR repositories we created by hand in previous modules. Chances are you will already have each tagged image for each repository in your local Docker repository however if you have removed them for any reason you can pull each image locally first before deleting each ECR repository. After ensuring we have all images in our local Docker repository we navigate to the EC2 container service dashboard, select repositories and delete all of the existing repositories. With all of our old ECR repositories deleted we can now proceed to run the Ansible playbook for our ECR resources project. Notice that we execute the playbook in exactly the same manner as we did for the CloudFormation shared resources stack. If we shift over to the CloudFormation dashboard we can see that our new stack is being created and once complete our ECR repositories should be present once again in the ECS dashboard. The final task is to push each image we published previously back to the newly created ECR repositories. And we can do this by using the Docker push command against our local Docker repository. Once complete all of the micro trader release images should be restored to their previous state.

Creating the Network Stack

We have created two simple CloudFormation stacks using the AWS CloudFormation role and now it's time to create a somewhat more complex stack which will define all of the AWS networking resources for our account. Let's get started by cloning the CloudFormation starter playbook to a folder called network resources and create a new Git Repository in the network resources folder. We first define an environment called production and then run the make roles command to install the required roles. The AWS CloudFormation role includes a template called network. yml. j2 so we can forget our global settings with an appropriate stack name and reference a network template in the AWS CloudFormation role. On the surface the network template appears quite small in size and demonstrates the use of a Jinja feature called macros. Macros are similar to the concept of functions in programming languages allowing you to define reusable pieces of template generation code and here you can see the network template imports a macro defined in the macro's network. j2 file referencing the macro via an object called network. You can see that the template calls two macro functions. A resources function, designed to generate CloudFormation network resources, and an outputs function which is designed to generate CloudFormation outputs. You can see both the resources and outputs functions. Each expect a dictionary called config vpcs to be defined which configures one or more named virtual private Clouds or VPCs in related settings. At a minimum each named VPC requires you to specify two mandatory variables, defining the VPC CIDR block which configures the range of IP address assigned to the VPC and a private route 53 domain name. Each VPC also accepts a number of optional variables that control the makeup of the VPC network resources that will be created. For example the template allows you to define public and private subnets where public subnets will include default routes to the internet whilst private subnets do not contain any default routes. Meaning hosts located in private subnets cannot communicate directly with the internet. We can specify an availability zone count which defaults to two. Meaning by default each of the public and private subnets will be created in availability zone A and B for your region although you can increase this count if your region supports more availability zones. The subnet size variable defines the subnet mask length for each subnet that is created which by default is set to 24 bits and is also commonly referred to as the 255. 255. 255. 0 subnet mask. One thing to note is that you must ensure your VPC CIDR block has enough IP address space to create public and private subnets in each of the availability zones you have defined. The domain name, DNS servers and NTP servers variables are used to configure VPC, DHCP options hits and the private domains and public domains variables define private and public route 53 zones respectively. If we take a look at the network. j2 macro notice that this defines a variety of network resources including internet gateways, route tables, VPC endpoints, DHCP option sets, route 53 zones and VPC subnets. You can see that the macro makes extensive use of Jinja loops and filters and the IP address and IP subnet filters that ship with Ansible are especially useful for generating subnetting and IP addressing configurations. Now that we understand the network template let's configure and run our playbook to create network resources. For our use case, and to keep things simple, we will only be creating a single VPC which we will call default and we must specify a suitable CIDR block for the VPC. Here we configure a CIDR block of 192. 168. 0. 0/20 which will support up to 16/24 subnets. By default the network template will create 8/24 subnets in total across two availability zones. So the CIDR block is adequately sized and leaves some room for additional subnets in the future. We also configure a private route 53 zone of dockerproductionaws. org which we will use for resolving internal services. We can now execute the playbook by running the make deploy command referencing the production environment and over in the CloudFormation console we can see our stack being created. After a couple of minutes the stack should finish creation and if we select CloudFormation exports notice that the network template created a whole bunch of exports. You can see that each export is prefixed with the VPC name of default and these exports will make it much more easier to reference your network resources in other CloudFormation stacks. If you open the VPC dashboard and browse to subnets notice that the public, medium, high and management subnets have been created in each availability zone. And if you select route tables you can see that the public route table has a default route to the internet and is only associated with the public subnets whilst the private route table has no default route and is associated with each of our private subnets.

Publishing the Docker Squid Image

We have one last act to create in this module and this is the HTTP proxy stack. Our previous network stack established a network topology that includes public subnets which have direct connectivity to the internet and a number of private subnets which do not have direct internet connectivity. In later modules, when we deploy our micro trader application, we will deploy our application instances to the private subnets. Meaning our instances will not be able to communicate directly with the internet and as we have already learned our ECS container instances require access to the AWS EC2 container service, EC2 container registry and CloudWatch log services which all have internet facing service API endpoints. To support AWS service and other public communications from private subnets we will deploy a proxy stack in our public subnets. In true Docker spirit we will run our proxies in Docker containers using the EC2 container service and in keeping with the stacks we've already deployed so far we will once again be leveraging a template from the AWS CloudFormation role. Before we can deploy the proxy stack we need to publish a Docker image for our proxy application and the Docker production AWS organization on GitHub already has a publish Docker squid repository that includes a workflow for building, testing and publishing Docker images that include the popular open-source squid proxy application. We also need to create an ECR repository to host the Docker squid image. So let's get started by first adding a repository called dockerproductionaws squid to our ECR resources playbook and then apply the change by running the ECR resources playbook targeting our production environment. If we open the AWS console, navigate to the ECS dashboard and browse to repositories notice that a new repository has been created. And we need to copy the value of the repository URI so that we can publish the Docker squid image to this repository. We can now fork the Docker squid repository in the Docker production AWS organization to your local GitHub account and proceed to clone the repository to your local environment. The workflow included in the repository is fully functional and just requires you to modify a couple of make file settings to get the Docker squid image published to the ECR repository we just created. Here we modify the Docker registry setting, copying the registry portion of the repository URI we copied when we created the ECR repository for the Docker squid image. We also need to set the AWS account ID which should match the account number in the Docker registry setting. The org name and repo name variables are already configured correctly and at this point we can run the workflow to build and publish the Docker image. After ensuring our local AWS environment is configured correctly we first run the make log-in command which will obtain an authorization token from the AWS ECR service. You can now run make release to build the Docker squid image and then run make tag and make publish to tag and publish the squid image to the squid ECR repository. After publishing is complete, if we check the ECR repository in the AWS console you should be able to see the image you just published and we can now create a playbook to define our proxy stack.

Creating the HTTP Proxy Stack

We create our proxy playbook by first cloning the AWS starter repository to a folder called proxy resources and create a new Git Repository in the proxy resources folder. We define an environment called production and then run make roles to install the required Ansible roles. The AWS CloudFormation role includes a template called proxy. yml. j2. So we can forget our global settings with an appropriate stack name and reference the proxy YAML j2 template in the AWS CloudFormation role. Inspection of this template introduces some differences we haven't seen so far in the other templates we've used. For example the proxy template includes a number of input parameters which the CloudFormation service will expect to be provided at stack creation time. One other interesting point is that the proxy template makes extensive use of the Fn import value intrinsic function which is used to import the value of CloudFormation exports. Notice that the proxy template requires an input parameter called VPCName to be configured and expects the network resources CloudFormation exports to be prefixed with a title-cased version of the configured VPCName which, in our case, is the default VPC we configured in our network resources playbook. We can also see that the application desired count parameter is used to control the number of proxy instances in which AZ subnets the proxy elastic load balancer and auto-scaling group will be deployed to. And by default this is set to a desired count of two which matches the AZ count of the network resources stack we created earlier in this module. Don't worry too much about how the various resources and the template are constructed for now. We'll cover off creating CloudFormation stacks for ECS based applications in much more detail in later modules. If we focus back to the stack parameters these define inputs to the stack template that must be provided at stack creation time. You can see that many of the stack inputs will vary depending on the environment. For example we might use a small EC2 instance type for non-production environments whilst in production environments we may need to use larger instance types. The AWS CloudFormation role includes support for stack inputs via a dictionary variable called stack. inputs. And a standard pattern is to define global defaults for this dictionary in the global settings file. Here we configure some of the stack inputs as key value peers in the stack. inputs dictionary and notice that the inputs simple reference the names of each of the stack parameters directly. Note that any stack parameters that we don't reference here globally must be defined as environment specific settings unless the stack parameter defines a default value in the CloudFormation template. If we now open the production environment settings file you might be tempted to take a similar approach and define a stack. inputs dictionary. However I don't recommend this approach as doing so will override all settings defined in the global stack. inputs dictionary. A much better approach is to override individual parameters by using an explicit syntax of stack. inputs. parameter name which ensures we only add or override individual parameters in the stack. inputs dictionary and will inherit any global defaults in our environment settings. We first configure the application image ID referencing the AMI we created in the last module when we built our ECS AMI using Packer. We specified t2. micro as our application instance type, setting the EC2 key peer name to the admin key peer we created when we set up our accounts and then override the application desired count value we defined in the global settings so that our stack will deploy two instances in a highly available configuration. We configure the proxy image setting to our ECS repository URI for the Docker production AWS squid repository and because we have globally defined the proxy image tag as latest we don't need to specify the image tag if we are happy with the setting. By default the squid proxy image is configured to only allow access to AWS API service endpoints. So we add the Docker production aws org private domain from our network resources playbook to the proxy white list. Finally we configure the name of the VPC our proxy stack will be created in which we named default when we created the network resources stack.

Deploying the HTTP Proxy Stack

At this point we are ready to create our HTTP proxy stack by running the make deploy command, targeting our production environment. The CloudFormation stack will take approximately 10 to 15 minutes to create and once complete, if you browse to the ECS dashboard, you should be able to see an ECS cluster with two ECS container instances. If you select the cluster notice the proxy service has two running tasks. And if you click on the ECS instances tab then click on one of the EC2 instance ID's we will now temporarily open SSH access to test out the proxy operation. We need to first copy the public IP address of the instance, click on the assigned security group and add a temporary inbound security rule permitting inbound SSH access from your public IP address. If we switch to the local terminal we can now SSH to the instance using the SSH key we created in earlier modules. If we run the docker ps command, notice that the squid container does not list a port mapping and this is because our proxy template is configured to run Docker in host networking mode. If you run the command sudo netstat-plnt notice that the squid process is listening on port 3128 and we can configure our local shell environment to use the proxy by exporting the displayed environment variables to the URL of the local squid instance. We can now test proxy operation by first running the curl command for Pluralsight. com, ensuring we connect from the IP address of the local eth0 interface. Notice that we get an access denied error from the proxy as somewhat surprisingly Pluralsight. com is not on the default proxy white list. If we now try and reach an AWS service API endpoint, which in this example is the web application firewall or waf service endpoint, we now get a response from the waf service as AWS service API endpoints are white listed by the proxy. Now that we've verified proxy operation we can remove the temporary security rule from the proxy security group. And our proxy stat deployment is complete.

Summary

In this module we introduced a powerful methodology and tool chain for deploying AWS services and resources using CloudFormation and Ansible. We were able to quickly create CloudFormation resources including S3 buckets and KMS keys, ECR repositories, a complete set of network resources and finally a fully functional HTTP proxy stack using a simple yet powerful playbook structure that allows you to create multiple environments across one or more AWS accounts. We learned how we can use best practice Ansible patterns to structure our playbooks not just around servers or groups of servers but around complete environments that can encompass any AWS service or resource that can be defined in CloudFormation. Common tasks such as IAM role assumption, CloudFormation template generation and CloudFormation stack deployment are all taken care of via generic reusable Ansible roles allowing you to focus on just defining your resources and how you want to configure each environment. We learned how the Ansible AWS CloudFormation role includes support for the powerful Jinja templating engine which allows you to overcome many of the limitations when you are working with native CloudFormation templates. We also learned how we can publish common templates for reuse and about powerful template generation techniques include Jinja filters and macros. Everything we created in this module was created using a fully automated infrastructure as code approach and on that note now would be a good time to commit all of your changes to the various playbooks we've created. Now that we have established all of the foundational AWS infrastructure and resources in our account we are now ready to focus on deploying the micro trade application to AWS. And as you might expect we'll be using exactly the same approach to create our micro trader application stacks.

Architecting and Preparing Applications for ECS

Introduction

Hi, my name is Justin Menga, and welcome to Architecting and Preparing Applications for ECS. In the previous module we established core foundational infrastructure required to support the deployment of our microtrader application to AWS, and learned how we can use Ansible and CloudFormation to define infrastructure as code, and very quickly create, update, and destroy complete environments. We are now ready to focus on the task of running the microtrader application in AWS. And in this module we will first discuss how we will architect the CloudFormation stack that will run the microtrader application in its supporting resources and then discuss specific challenges we will face running the microtrader application in AWS, including how microtrader cluster discovery works in AWS environments where traditional application discovery techniques do not work. Another challenge we need to overcome is the ability to generate configuration files on the fly to configure our application to run appropriately or for a local Docker environment, as well as an AWS ECS environment. And we will learn how to configure our containers to use a tool called ConfD that can generate environment-specific configuration files on the fly in a manageable and scalable manner.

Microtrader AWS Architecture

Back in module 2 you were introduced to the sample microtrader application, and learned about the application architecture as illustrated onscreen. The microtrader application consists of four micro services that each work together to deliver the overall functionality of the application. We have already built and published Docker images for each of the microtrader services and our goal is to create a complete, highly-available environment for deploying the microtrader application and its various supporting resources using the Ansible and CloudFormation methodology we introduced in the last module. Before we create a playbook and define CloudFormation templates in target environments we need to understand what we need to build in terms of AWS resources and services to ensure our application is production ready, highly available, and can scale as required. The first component in our application stack will be an ECS cluster, which contains ECS container instances operating in an EC2 auto scaling group. This is where we will run containers for each of the microtrader application services, and we will use EC2 auto scaling groups so that we can easily scale up or scale down compute resources for the application as required. To provide high availability and load balancing for our application services we will mix configure load balances using the recently-introduced AWS application load balancers, which include support for content-based routing, as well as WebSocket support, which our microtrader dashboard service uses to communicate events in real time to web clients. We will actually create two application load balancers in our stack. The first load balancer will be a public internet facing load balancer, which is used to service front-end requests to the microtrader dashboard, whilst the other load balancer will be an internal load balancer, which is used to facilitate internal REST API communications between the various microtrader application services. We will also set up an internal DNS record for the internal load balancer, which will make it easy to bypass the HTTP proxy we established in the previous module for internal HTTP communications between the microtrader application services. The microtrader audit services needs to persist soack trading history to a database, and we will use the AWS relational database service, or RDS to create an RDS instance running a MySQL database for storing audit data. Centralized access to application and system logs are a critical requirement for running real-world applications, and we will create CloudWatch log groups that will store various types of ECS container instance system logs, as well as our microtrader application logs. Finally, we will create ECS task definitions that define the runtime Docker configuration for each of our microtrader application services, and also create ECS services that will schedule one or more instances of each of our microtrader application services to the ECS cluster and coordinate rolling deployments in conjunction with the application load balancers that service fronting connections to each application service.

Docker and AWS Challenges

Before we get started defining out microtrader application stack, it's important to understand the distributed nature of the application components we are working with and the impact of using Docker and AWS to run the microtrader application. In earlier modules, we learned the microtrader application is based upon an application toolkit called Vert. x, which includes built in support for distributing components of your application across multiple systems using a feature known as the EventBus. When you deploy Vert. x applications in this manner, they establish what is referred to as a cluster, using an underlying cluster auto discovery in management component called Hazelcast. In earlier modules we configured Hazelcast to use multicast network communications for cluster auto discovery in our local Docker environment. However, in AWS, multicast network communications are not supported. So we require an alternative cluster auto discovery mechanism. Hazelcast supports a feature called EC2 auto discovery, which if enabled attempts to discover other cluster members by querying the EC2 API service for other EC2 instances that are tagged with an application-specific key and value. For example, for our microtrader application we can tag each ECS container instance in the ECS cluster with a key called Hazelcast:group and a value of microtrader and then configure each application service to query for other cluster members based upon this key value pair, and this is something that we will learn how to configure in this module. Another challenge in Docker environments is the default behavior of Docker, which is to use network address translation to enable communications between containers distributed across multiple Docker hosts. Network address translation breaks cluster discovery mechanisms as each cluster member attempts to advertise its local IP address to other cluster members, however, network reachability is via a secret external IP address unknown to the advertising cluster member, which in the case of Docker is the Docker host IP address. One way to overcome this is to enable Docker host networking mode, where containers share the Docker host network stack and therefore are directly reachable via the Docker host IP address; and recall in earlier modules, we enable the ability to configure Docker host networking mode in our ECS machine images for this exact purpose. The final challenge relates to the way Vert. x establishes event bus communications between cluster members once the Vert. x cluster has formed. By default, Vert. x uses a dynamic ephemeral TCP port for EventBus communications, and exchanges this information with other cluster members using Hazelcast. Each cluster member then establishes connections with other members in the cluster allowing all custom members to be able to communicate with each other over the EventBus. This dynamic behavior can be particularly problematic in environments that use network address translation. So enabling Docker host networking mode is one approach to making this issue simpler to deal with. The other problem is that you often need to lock down security groups in your production environments, and allowing any-to-any communication between your cluster members is just not an option for and security-conscience organization. Thankfully Vert. x does provide the ability to configure a static port for EventBus communications, and in later modules we will learn how to configure our microtrader application services to use static EventBus ports.

Controlling Cluster Auto Discovery

We've discussed challenges with the Hazelcast auto-discovery mechanism in AWS, so let's now discuss how we can configure the EC2 auto-discovery feature to enable the microtrader application services to auto discover each other in our AWS environments. The Hazelcast configuration is controlled via a file called cluster. xml, which we discussed briefly in earlier modules when we enabled multicast discovery for our local Docker environment. The document at the URL shown onscreen describes how to configure EC2 auto discovery, and as you can see we need to disable multicast discovery and define an AWS configuration section with various properties that control the EC2 auto discovery behavior. This looks straightforward enough, but one problem we need to consider is that we need to support running the microtrader application in a local Docker environment, for example, when we create and test our microtrader application release images, as well as supporting running the microtrader application in our production AWS environments. So we need the ability to load our Hazelcast configuration that enables multicast discovery in a local Docker environment and then load a different configuration for AWS environments, which enables EC2 auto discovery. Ideally all environment-based configuration for Docker containers should be defined in environment-variable terms. However, Hazelcast does not support environment variables, instead relying on the file-based approach of defining a cluster configuration file. As a general principle you should never bake environment-specific settings into your Docker images, and although Docker supports bind mounts that could mount a configuration file on the underlying host into your containers at container startup, this does not solve the problem of how you deploy and update these configuration files to your underlying host. One approach to solve this issue is to generate configuration files on the fly at container startup, where you have a script or utility that examines environment variables passed to the container and generates configuration files based upon those environment variable inputs. Although you can do this quite easily with Bash scripts, these scripts quickly become complex, especially if you introduce a lot of conditional logic, and the script you create is typically a custom script that can't easily be reused for other applications that face the same problem. An alternative to Bash scripts that I highly recommend is using a tool called ConfD, which is an open-source tool written in Go and published at the GitHub URL shown. ConfD provides a simple and reusable approach that relies on Go templates to define templates for your configuration files, and then runs a Go template generator to render an environment-specific version of your configuration file template to a configurable location in your file system where the application expects the configuration file to reside. ConfD supports obtaining environment-specific configuration from environment variables that also supports a number of other back-ends, including Consul, etcd, and DynamoDB. This makes ConfD a very powerful and reusable solution for generating configuration files on the fly, and is a much better long-term approach than using Bash scripts. So let's now learn how we can introduce this capability into our microtrader Docker images.

Configuring Cluster Auto Discovery Using Confd

To use ConfD to generate configuration files on the fly, we will need to install the ConfD utility and then create template files for rendering the Hazelcast cluster. xml configuration file at container startup. Because we need this capability for all of our microtrader application services, we will add the capability to the microtrader base image, which will then be inherited by each of the microtrader release images. Let's now open the microtrader base project, navigating to the Docker file at the root of the repository, and we will first install ConfD, which is already published as an Alpine package in the alpine/edge/testing repository. We add a label called @testing, referencing the alpine/edge/testing repository to the local Alpine repository configuration file, and then install ConfD by referencing the name of the package followed by the repository label. We create the /app/conf directory explicitly and ensure the Vert. x user has full access to the /app folder and contained subfolders. We next add a copy directive that will copy a local folder, which we will create shortly to etc/confd in the image. And this is where our ConfD configuration and template files will be stored. We also need to remove the existing directive that copies the current cluster. xml file as confd will now be responsible for generating this file dynamically at container startup. Let's now create the etc/confd folder locally, and we need to add two subfolders called conf. d and templates. The conf. d folder stored ConfD configuration for each template that is generated, which is an any-style TOML file created for each template. Here we define a file called cluster. xml. toml, and specify a top level key of template. We define the source of the template, which is the name of the template file as stored in the ETC confd templates folder and a destination, which is where ConfD will output the render template file. Notice the destination as the location our Docker file command directive expects the Hazelcast cluster. xml file to be located. We also add a list of keys, which defines the allowed configuration variables that will be used to render the template file. The keys are defined in a generic path-based syntax to allow for different configuration back-ends to be used. And when using environment variables as the back-end, ConfD will ignore the leading slash and convert each subsequent slash to an underscore, and then look for environment variables that match each configuration key. The templates folder stores template files that ConfD will render. Here we create a file called cluster. xml. tmpl as defined in the conf. d configuration file and initially paste the existing cluster. xml file contents into this template. Each template is defined as a Go template, which uses handlebar-style double curly braces to reference configuration variables or inbuilt functions that control the template generation output. We first address the hardcoded group name and password settings by adding a template function called getv that will inject the value of the cluster group name and cluster group password environment variables defaulting to the previous hard-coded values if these environment variables are not defined. Next we tackle the join configuration section, which controls the Hazelcast auto discovery configuration. To determine if we want to generate a configuration for an AWS environment we will add an if function that checks if an environment variable-named cluster/aws/enabled is set to true, which will evaluate to false by default if not defined. If cluster/aws/enabled is true, we then configure auto discover appropriately for an AWS environment, disabling multicast and TCP IP-based discovery and enabling the EC2 auto discovery feature. Notice that we use environment variables to control the settings required for EC2 auto discovery, settings saying DEFAULT if these environment variables are not supplied. The IAM role property defines the IAM role that Hazelcast should attempt to assume to query the EC2 service, and this role must have The EC2 describe instances permission. If you set this to the default value of default, Hazelcast will use the EC2 IAM instance role, which is typically the best-practice approach you should take. And, in the next module we will configure the IAM instance roles of our ECS container instances to include the required EC2 describe instances permission. Notice if CLUSTER_AWS_ENABLED is set to false or not configured then multicast discovery will be enabled, as was the case previously. So, this approach allows us to configure Hazelcast auto discovery appropriately for both local Docker environments, as well as AWS environments, by simply configuring the CLUSTER_AWS_ENABLED environment variable appropriately for the given environment. The final modification we need to make is to run ConfD at container startup, so that the Hazelcast configuration file is rendered appropriately before starting the Vert. x application in subsequent Hazelcast auto discovery process. The entrypoint script currently defined for the container is designed specifically for this purpose and allows us to run ConfD prior to handing off control to the main application process. Here we run ConfD passing in the one-time flag, which ensures ConfD will execute only once and not run in the background as a daemon process, and specify a back-end of env, which means ConfD will look in the environment for various template configuration variables when rendering each template. Let's now remove the original cluster. xml file and build the microtrader base image, tagging the image with the fully-qualified name of your ECR microtrader base repository. After building the image we can quickly test the image using the docker run command as shown. Here we kept the output of the rendered cluster. xml file, and notice this has a default group name and password values, and also has the default multicast auto discovery mechanism enabled. If we now create another container from the image, this time setting a custom cluster group password, and setting the CLUSTER_AWS_ENABLED variable to true, notice that our rendered cluster. xml file includes the custom group password and is also now configured for the EC2 auto discovery using the template default settings for the various AWS configuration properties. Now that we've tested the changes to our base image, let's publish the base image to your ECR repository, and once complete we can test that our microtrader release images still run correctly by running make test and make release in the microtrader repository. As you can see, all acceptance tests pass, meaning the local Docker environment cluster discovery mechanism is still working as before. We now need to tag and publish the updated microtrader release images, and once complete, you should run make clean to clean up your local Docker environment. As a final task, you should also commit and push the changes you have made to the microtrader base repository.

Summary

In this module we defined the target architecture we will deploy to AWS for the microtrader applications, which includes an EC2 auto-scaling group, a public and internal application load balancer, an RDS instance, and various ECS resources including an ECS cluster, ECS task definitions, and ECS services. We discussed challenges with deploying the microtrader application to AWS, which relate to how Vert. x cluster members discover each other. We learned how to configure Hazelcast for EC2 auto discovery, and discussed how we can use Docker host networking mode to simplify the Hazelcast EC2 auto discovery mechanism and how EventBus communications can be established. Finally, we configured our microtrader base image to use the ConfD tool, which generates the Hazelcast cluster. xml configuration file using Go templates, allowing us to support operation in both a local Docker environment, as well as an AWS ECS environment, by simply modifying the environment variable configuration, passed to our microtrader containers at startup. In the next module we will start defining the CloudFormation template for the microtrader application, defining all of the supporting resources required to run the microtrader application in AWS.

Defining ECS Applications Using Ansible and CloudFormation

Introduction

Hi, my name is Justin Menga and welcome to Defining AWS Infrastructure using Ansible and CloudFormation. In the previous module we discussed the architecture of the CloudFormation stack we will create to run the Microtrader application in AWS and in this module we will cerate our initial stack and define all of the supporting resources. We will get started by establishing an Ansible playbook for the stack, leveraging the same AWS CloudFormation role we introduced in earlier modules and define a development environment for the Microtrader application. We will then proceed to define the various components of the stack in our playbook creating an EC2 autoscaling group defining a public and internal application load balancer and associated DNS record, configuring an RDS instance that will run the audit database, create CloudWatch logs for storing system and container logs, and configure security groups and IAM roles that link our resources and allow them to communicate with each other. By the end of this module you will have a firm understanding of how to build a complex set of AWS resources and combine them to from a strong architectural foundation for ECS-based applications.

Creating the Microtrader Deployment Playbook

We have added support for our Microtrader application to run in an AWS environment and we are now ready to create a Microtrader deployment playbook that defines our Microtrader application stack. Let's get started by cloning the AWS starter repository to a folder called microtrader-deploy and create a new Git repository in the microtrader-deploy folder. We will create two environments called development and production by running the make environment command and then execute the make roles command to install the required Ansible roles. If we now open the global settings file in group_vars, all, vars. yml because we will be creating multiple environments within the same AWS account, we won't configure the stack name at a global level and instead we'll define the stack name for each environment later on in this module. In earlier modules we used predefined templates included with the AWS CloudFormation role. However, for our Microtrader application stack, we will create our own CloudFormation template which by default the AWS CloudFormation role expects to be located in a file called templates/stack. yml. j2 and notice this file is already defined with a sample CloudFormation template from the AWS starter responsory.

Configuring EC2 Autoscaling Groups

In the previous module we discussed the various AWS resources required for the Microtrader application and we will get started by defining an EC2 autoscaling group that will run a group of ECS container instances to which we will deploy our Docker containers. Before we get started, I want to point out a great resource that you should always refer to when defining CloudFormation stacks. The online AWS CloudFormation User Guide provides very comprehensive coverage of all facets of CloudFormation including a template reference that defines all AWS resource types. Here you will find detailed information about each resource supported by CloudFormation and it always pays to refer to this document as it provides valuable information about how each resource needs to be configured and also outlines caveats or considerations you need to be aware of when you create, modify or delete your resources. As I step through this module I highly recommend you supplement your learning by referring to the relevant resource definitions in the CloudFormation documentation. Let's get started defining our CloudFormation template by configuring an appropriate stack description and rather than referencing the end variable to dynamically add the name of the environment to the description, notice that the AWS CloudFormation role allows you to override the stack description on a per environment basis using the stack. description variable. Now, is also a good time to set the stack name in our development settings which we will call Microtrader development to clearly and uniquely identify our development environment. We can now focus on the resources section of the template by first creating a resource called ApplicationAutoscaling and one thing you will notice as we step through creating our CloudFormation resources is that I always recommend using generic resource names where possible to help make your templates reusable across multiple stacks. We specify a type of AWS autoscaling AutoscalingGroup and first define a CreationPolicy attribute which defines how CloudFormation will behave when creating this resource. The ResourceSignal property defines the number or count of EC2 instances that must be successfully created in the AutoscalingGroup. The CloudFormation Init framework which we will discuss shortly provides the ability for an EC2 instance to signal CloudFormation it has successfully bootstrapped and in previous modules we installed CloudFormation Init in our custom ECS Amazon machine image. I typically recommend that you specify a count of the desired number of instances in your autoscaling group meaning all instances must create successfully otherwise the AutoscalingGroup resource creation will fail and your CloudFormation stack deployment will fail and roll back. Here we define a reference to an input parameter using the ref intrinsic function which CloudFormation will populate with the value of the reference parameter at stack creation time. We reference a parameter called ApplicationDesiredCount and then define a timeout of 15 minutes in ISO 8601 duration format meaning we must receive the application desired count number of success signals within 15 minutes of creating the AutoscalingGroup. Let's now create the application desired count input parameter which represents the desired number of ECS container instances in our AutoscalingGroup. This parameter will be a number parameter and we specify a default value of one instance if this input parameter is not provided at stack creation time. We next define the various properties of the AutoscalingGroup first defining the VPCZoneIdentifier property which is a list of VPC subnet IDs that AutoscalingGroup instances will be created in. Rather than specify actual subnet ID values, notice that we use the Fn::ImportValue intrinsic function which is used to import the value of CloudFormation exports within the local region of your AWS account. In earlier modules when we created the network resources stack, a large number of CloudFormation exports were created including exports for each of the subnet IDs created as part of the stack. Here we use the Fn::Sub intrinsic function to concatenate the value of an input parameter called VPC name with the name of the target subnets. The network resources stack creates CloudFormation exports that are prefixed with the name of a target VPC and here our approach means we can reuse our template in environments that target different VPCs by simply supplying the appropriate value for the VPC name input parameter. We configure the VPC name input parameter with a default value of default meaning our template will target a VPC name's default if we don't provide this input parameter. So, the VPCZoneIdentifier property will target default MediumSubnetA and B as our target subnets. We next reference an ApplicationAutoscalingLaunchConfiguration resource that we will create shortly which controls how each instance in the AutoscalingGroup will be configured at instance creation time. We specify a minimum and maximum size for the AutoscalingGroup and reference the ApplicationDesiredCount parameter we created earlier for the desired count or capacity of the AutoscalingGroup. Finally we create two tags. The first tag will provide a friendly name for each instance in the AWS console and we use the Fn::Sub intrinsic function along with the AWS stack name pseudo parameter which CloudFormation will resolve and substitute at stack creation time with the name of the CloudFormation stack. The PropagateAtLaunch property means that this tag will be propagated to each instance that is created as part of the AutoscalingGroup. The second tag is required for the Hazelcast EC2 autodiscovery mechanism and we reference thee ApplicationCluster resource which is a resource we will create later on that represents the ECS cluster that the ECS container instances in our AutoscalingGroup will belong to. CloudFormation resources are typically defined using logical resource identifiers such as application cluster in this example with unique physical resource identifiers created for each logical resource at stack creation time meaning we can guarantee this value will be unique within our AWS account and region and will therefore uniquely identify members of our ECS application cluster. Later on we will configure the tag key and tag value as environment variable values passed to our Microtrader application services allowing each Microtrader service to discover other Microtrader services within the same autoscaling group and ECS cluster.

Configuring Autoscaling Launch Configurations

We now need to create an EC2 AutoScalingLaunchConfiguration resource which provides the configuration for each instance in our EC2 AutoscalingGroup. The image ID property defines the Amazon machine image or AMI to launch our instances from referencing an input parameter called ApplicationImageId and we define the EC2 instance type and EC2 key pair properties in a similar fashion. We next define each of these input parameters and notice that along with default values, we can also add input parameter constraints using the AllowedValues property. We continue configuring our AutoscalingLaunchConfiguration resource by next defining the IAMInstanceProfile property which references an IAMInstanceProfile resource that we will create shortly. The SecurityGroups property defines a list of security groups that will be attached to each instance and here we reference a single security group that we will create later on. The final property we configure is the UserData property which provides the ability to run custom initialization actions at instance creation time. Although you can use UserData for simple initialization tasks, AWS provides a proprietary mechanism called CloudFormation Init for more complex scenarios which we discussed briefly in earlier modules when we created our custom ECS machine image. In this course, we will use CloudFormation Init as our primary tool to perform custom initialization actions. However, CloudFormation Init must be invoked using UserData and a standard pattern to do this is to provide a Bash script to UserData that calls the various CloudFormation Init tools. The script must be Base64 encoded which CloudFormation will automatically do for us by using the Fn::Base64 intrinsic function and we then use another intrinsic function called Fn::Join which simply joins a list of values together using a configurable delimiter. Notice that we specify the basher bang in inserting line breaks we required and call the cfn-init tool passing the name of the stack and the name of the logical resource that contains CloudFormation Init metadata which in this case is the ApplicationAutoscalingLaunchConfiguration resource. We also need to specify the AWS region and because our instances will be located on the private MediumSubnet with no direct internet connectivity, we must also specify proxy settings referencing the default ProxyUrl CloudFormation export created by the proxy stack in earlier modules. The call to cfn-init will execute all actions in CloudFormation Init metadata which we will create shortly and after cfn-init completes, we must signal success or failure to CloudFormation using the cfn-signal command. Notice that we refer to the exit code of the previously run cfn-init command using a special Bash variable which in this case will be zero or success if cfn-init completed successfully or a non-zero value or failure if cfn-init failed for any reason. We provide a similar set of configuration flags to the cfn-signal command and one gotcha here is that the resource flag refers to the resource we need to signal which is the ApplicationAutoscaling resource that defines the creation policy for the AutoscalingGroup and recall when we configured the AutoscalingGroup creation policy that we expect to receive signals from each instance before marking the AutoscalingGroup resource as being successfully created.

Configuring CloudFormation Init Metadata

The ApplicationAutoScalingLaunchConfiguration resource also stores stores CloudFormation Init metadata that defines customer initialization tasks that should be performed by each AutoscalingGroup instance at instance creation or launch configuration time. Here we define a single config set using the commands property to define a dictionary of named commands that will be executed. Each named command is executed in alphabetical order so a common pattern is to prefix each command with a numeric value to ensure the execution order of each command. In our case, we only run a single command which is to call the firstrun Shell script in the EC2-user home directory that is baked into our ECS machine image. And recall from pervious modules that this script expects a number of environment variables to be present in order to configure proxy, ECS agent, Docker and CloudWatch log settings appropriate to the environment the instance is operating in. The stack name AutoscalingGroup and AWS default default region settings are all used to configure CloudWatch log settings ensuring we config the correct log group names for ECS container instance logs and connect to the correct regional service endpoint for CloudWatch logs. The ECS_CLUSTER setting is used to configure the ECS agent to join the reference ECS_CLUSTER and is also used to check the ECS container instance has successfully joined the correct cluster before signaling success to CloudFormation. Finally, we configure the ProxyUrl environment variable importing the value of the CloudFormation export published by the proxy stack which will configure the CloudWatch log's agent Docker in the ECS agent to use the web proxy.

Configuring EC2 Autoscaling Security Groups

We now need to define the ApplicationAutoScalingSecurityGroup resource we referenced in our ApplicationAutoScalingLaunchConfiguration. We first create the SecurityGroup resource configuring an appropriate description and importing the value of the default VpcId CloudFormation export to configure the VPC ID the security group belongs to. We permit ingress SSH access from any host within the default VpcCidr address block and then allow outbound access to the AWS VpcDnsServer as well as HTTP proxy access to proxy instances within the proxy stack. Notice that we make heavy use of CloudFormation exports which avoids you having to determine the specific physical values for each referenced resource and instead let CloudFormation evaluate each export value at stack creation time. We also need to configure our security group such that our Microtrader services can form a vertex cluster using Hazelcast and then can then communicate with each other using the vertex event bus. By default, Hazelcast uses TCP port 5701 for cluster communications and because our Docker containers will operate in host networking mode sharing the operating system network stack, multiple vertex containers running on the same host will all attempt to use port 5701. Hazelcast deals with this by auto incrementing the port it attempts to use if the port it first attempts to use is being used by another Hazelcast instance. This means we need to configure a range of TCP ports and if you review the cluster. xml template in the Microtrader-based repository, the starting port and auto incrementing behavior are defined in the port configuration property of the network section and you can see that the configuration will start at port 5701 and auto increment up to 5710. So, this means we need to allow instances in the AutoscalingGroup to be able to communicate with each other using the TCP port range of 5701 to 5710. One challenge with configuring this rule in our ApplicationAutoscalingSecurityGroup is that we need to reference the security group itself as both the source and destination security groups. This is not permitted in CloudFormation as it creates a circular reference, i. e. the ApplicationAutoscalingSecurityGroup resource depends on itself. CloudFormation offers special SecurtiyGroupIngress and SecurityGroupEgress resources that CloudFormation will evaluate once all security groups have been created and will add security rules to a target security group that specify a source or destination security group ID in the ingress or egress security rule. We configure a resource called MicrotraderClusterDiscoveryIngress which will add the first four properties as an ingress security rule to the security group targeted by the group ID property. Because this is a separate CloudFormation resource, we can now safely reference the ApplicationAutoscalingSecurityGroup which CloudFormation will ensure has been created before attempting to add the ingress rule to the target ApplicationAutoscalingSecurityGroup. We also configure a reciprocal MicrotradeClusterDiscoveryEgress rule which likewise adds the first four properties as an egress security rule to the security group targeted by the group ID property. And the end result is that the ApplicationAutoscalingSecurityGroup will end up with two rules permitting ingress and egress access from any instance to any instance in our ApplicationAutoscalingGroup using the Hazelcast cluster port range of 5701 to 5710. In the previous module we also discussed that vertex uses separate ports for event bus communications and in the next module we will configure our Microtrader application services to use fixed TCP ports of 15701 through 15704, so we add MicrotraderEventBusIngress and egress resources which will allow our Microtrader services to communicate with each other over the vertex event bus. Note that in general whenever you need to configure security rules that reference other security groups in CloudFormation templates, the best practice is to use Security group ingress and security group egress resources to avoid the possibility of circular dependencies in your template.

Configuring Autoscaling EC2 Instance Profiles

We next need to define the ApplicationAutoscalingInstanceProfile resource we referenced in our ApplicationAutoscalingLaunchConfiguration and this resource is simply a pointer to an IAM role resource that we name ApplicationAutoscalingInstanceRole. Because we are defining a role for an EC2 instance profile, we must specify the principle as the EC2 service in the assume role policy document and we next attach several in line IAM policies to the role. The first policy we name ECSContainerInstancePolicy and here we configure the various permissions required for ECS and ECR-related actions. Notice that we create the ability to register and de-register container instances from only the application ECS cluster and the various ECR permissions allow our ECS container instances to pull Docker images from our ECR repositories. We next create a policy called HazelcastClusterDiscoveryPolicy which grants the EC2 described instances permission that is required for the Hazelcast EC2 auto discovery mechanism to work and finally create a CloudwatchLogsPolicy which will enable ApplicationAutoscaling instances to create log streams and write log events to any log group that is prefixed with the name of our CloudFormation stack. The approach implemented uses the principle of least privileged where possible to ensure your ECS container instances can only perform potentially dangerous actions such as de-registering container instances on resources specific to the CloudFormation stack.

Configuring a Public Load Balancer

We've created our ApplicationAutoscaling group and related resources and now it's time to create application load balancers for our stack. As we discussed earlier, we will define two application load balancers, a public load balancer that services front end requests from the internet to our Microtrader dashboard and an internal load balancer that services internal HTTP REST communications between our Microtrader services. In this course, we will use the new layer seven application load balancers introduces by AWS in mid-2016 which can make load balancing decisions based upon HTTP information such as the host header or path of incoming HTTP requests. If you have previously configured classic elastic load balancers, which operate at a layer four or TCP level you will find application load balancers are more complex and require more configuration to achieve a working state. The extra effort is generally worth it though as you get many additional features such as content-based routing, dynamic port mapping for ECS-based applications as well as support for web sockets which our Microtrader dashboard uses to communicate with web clients. In this course, we won't be discussing dynamic port mapping as the nature of the Microtrader application is such that it operates much better using Docker host networking mode but you can read more about dynamic port mapping and ECS integration with application load balancers at the URL shown. When creating application load balancers, you first create an application load balancer resource which defines the physical location of the load balancer and assigns security groups permitting ingress and egress access. Linux creates one or more listeners which you can think of as service endpoints or ports that provide client-side or front-end connectivity to the load balancer. For example, you might define an HTTP listener operating on port 80 as well as an HTTPS listener configured with an appropriate certificate that listens on port 443. Each listener must define a default target group which is a separate resource that represents backend instances you want to route and load balance incoming requests to. A powerful feature of the new application load balancers is the ability to optionally create listener rules which allow you to configure rules that route incoming HTTP requests to different target groups based upon the host header or path of the incoming request. We will first create the public load balancer and define this as an ElasticLoadBalancingV2 or ApplicationLoadBalancer resource. The public load balancer is internet facing and hints as configured with an internet-facing scheme and must be located on the public subnets in our VPC as these are the only subnets with direct internet access. We also set a couple of LoadBalancerAttributes disabling delete detection and configuring a connection idle timeout of 30 seconds and finally, attach a security group to the load balancer that we will define shortly. We next need to define a listener resource for the load balancer which defines a service endpoint that the load balancer will accept incoming requests to. Here we reference the PublicLoadBalancer resource in the LoadBalancerArn property and define the protocol and port of the listener. Because we will be using plain old HTTP to access the Microtrader dashboard, we configure a protocol of HTTP and hard code the port to port 80. Finally, we configure the DefaultActions property which defines the default target group resource that the listener will forward all requests to by default. Here we reference a resource called DashboardServiceTargetGroup which means by default all requests to this listener will be forwarded to our Microtrader dashboard and we define the protocol and port used to communicate to each backend instance. We configure HTTP as the protocol and reference an input parameter called MicrotraderDashboardHttpPort which is used to configure the HTTP port the Microtrader dashboard service will operate on. After creating this parameter we set the correct VPC and configure a target group attribute that sets the de-registration delay timeout to 30 seconds meaning the load balancer will allow connections to drain for up to 30 seconds when an instance is placed into a connection draining state. The final resources we need to create for the public load balancer are security related and we first create the PublicLoadBalancerSecurityGroup which permits ingress access from the internet to our front end HTTP listener on port 80. Our load balancer also needs an egress security rule that permits egress access from the PublicLoadBalancer to the MicrotraderDashboard instances in the ApplicationAutoscalingGroup, so we create a SecurityGroupEgress rule to avoid circular dependencies between the public load balancer and ApplicationAutoscalingSecurityGroups. Here our egress resource specifies a single egress security rule that will be added to the PublicLoadBalancerSecurityGroup resource at stack creation time and allows egress access from the PublicLoadBalancer to the MicrotraderDashboardHttpPort operating on all ApplicationAutoscaling instances. We also create a reciprocal SecurityGroupIngress resource which allows the same access on the ingress side to the ApplicationAutoscalingSecurityGroup. One final task we need to perform is to define a stack output which will be visible in the outputs of the CloudFormation stack. Here we define an output that returns the value of the PublicLoadBalancer. DNSName so that we can easily refer to this value when we test our stack.

Configuring an Internal Load Balancer

We now need to create an internal application load balancer to enable internal HTTP REST communications between our Microtrader services. The Microtrader quote service publishes an HTTP REST endpoint that can be used to query the current stock market quote prices whilst the audit service publishes an endpoint that can be used to obtain the last 10 stock trades. These endpoints are used internally by the dashboard and portfolio services and are published to an in-memory service location register available to all Microtrader cluster members. We first create an ApplicationLoadBalancer resource and specify the scheme as internal given we are defining a load balancer for internal use only. We locate the ApplicationLoadBalancer on the same private subnets as our ECS container instances attaching a SecurityGroup called ApplicationLoadBalancerSecurityGroup disabling delete protection and configuring a connection idle timeout of 30 seconds. We next create a listener that will service incoming requests to the load balancer binding the listener to the ApplicationLoadBalancer specifying a protocol of HTTP and configuring the listener to listen on port 80. For the listener DefaultAction, we reference a target group called QuoteServiceTargetGroup meaning by default incoming requests will be routed to the Microtrader quote service. The ApplicationLoadBalancer also needs to service requests to the audit service and to enable this we add a listener or resource called AuditServiceListenerRule which attaches a content routing rule to the ApplicationLoadBalancerListener resource. The conditions property defines a condition that specifies any HTTP request that matches a path pattern of audit/* will matched to this rule and then in the actions property we specify that any requests matching this rule should be forwarded to the AuditServiceTargetGroup resource. So, this means any request to audit/* will be routed to the Microtrader audit service while requests to any other path including quote/* will be routed to the Microtrader quote service. We now need to define the quote service and audit service target groups and notice that we specify a HealthCheckPath of /quote and /audit respectively which is the default HTTP root path to each of these services. We also need to ensure we route requests to the correct port for each service and the reference input parameters that define these port values. After creating the various referenced input parameters, the final resources we need to configure are the ApplicationLoadBalancerSecurityGroup which defines an empty set of ingress and egress rules and SecurityGroupIngress and SecurityGroupEgress resources that permit outbound access from the ApplicationLoadBalancer to the Microtrader quote service and also permit outbound access to the Microtrader audit service by referencing appropriate input parameters and security group resources in each rule. We also need to allow the ApplicationAutoscalingGroup to access the front end ApplicationLoadBalancer HTTP port which ensures the Microtrader dashboard service will be able to communicate with the quote and audit endpoints serviced by the application load balancer.

Configuring DNS Records

Our Microtrader services are located in a private subnet and use an HTTP proxy to access the various AWS EC2 container service CloudWatch logs and EC2 API service endpoints. Because our Microtrader services perform internal communications with each other via the internal ApplicationLoadBalancer, ideally we don't want this traffic traversing the HTTP proxy, so we need to configure proxy bypass rules on our Microtrader ECS container instances and ECS services. All AWS load balancers are assigned a DNS name from the elb. amazonaws. com DNS namespace. In this external namespace is something we don't really want to have to bypass the proxy, so to overcome this we will create a DNS record in the private route 53 VPC domain that we created as part of our network resources stack. We will configure the internal application load balancer endpoint with a friendly DNS name which will simplify configuration of proxy bypass rules for our Microtrader services and avoid having to bypass the external AWS ELB DNS namespace. We can do this in CloudFormation by creating a Route53::RecordSet resource and configure the name or value of the record by joining an input parameter called ApplicationDnsName with the default VpcDomain CloudFormation export value to form a fully qualified domain name record. We need to reference the name of the Route53 hosted zone which always must end with a dot and we configure the record as a canonical name or CNAME record as the record will be a friendly name for the AWS-generated DNS name of the load balancer. We obtain this generated name by using the Fn::Sub intrinsic function to obtain the DNS name attribute of the ApplicationLoadBalancer and attach this as a resource record to the DNS record set. Finally, to wrap up our DNS configuration we add the ApplicationDnsName input parameter we reverenced in our DNS record.

Configuring the Relational Database Service

The Microtrader Audit service keeps a history of all stock trading transactions and needs to persist this to a relational database. As we discussed earlier, we will use the AWS relational database service or RDS to provide a managed MySQL database instance which we will create in a resource called ApplicationDatabase.
