After specifying the resource type, we define an attribute called DeletionPolicy which specifies what should happen to the RDS instance in the event the stack is deleted. For production environments you will typically choose a policy of either retain which will not delete the instance, or snapshot which will create an RDS snapshot before deleting the instance. Whilst in non-production environments you may want to specify a policy of delete to ensure your RDS instance resources are always deleted along with the stack. One current limitation with CloudFormation is that you cannot reference input parameters for deletion policies. However, a useful feature of the AWS CloudFormation role is the ability to override all deletion policies for a given type of resource by specifying a special parameter syntax that allows you to reference all resources for a given resource type. Here we first set the deletion policy to snapshot in the template and then reference the deletion policy property of the resource type in our development settings configuring a value of delete meaning our development environment will permit the RDS instance to be destroyed when deleting the stack whilst other environments such as our production environment will first take an RDS snapshot as per the global setting in the template. We define an RDS engine and version of MySQL 5. 7 referencing an input parameter called DatabaseInstanceType to define the database instance class or type and allocate 10 gigabytes of GP2 or SSD storage to the instance. We configure a master username, master password and database name referencing appropriate input parameters and attach a security group to the instance called ApplicationDatabaseSecurityGroup which we will define shortly. The DBSubnetGroupName references a DBSubnetGroup resource that we will create shortly and the MultiAZ property allows you to enable or disable a backup RDS instance in another availability zone which will replicate data from your primary RDS instance and automatically fail over in the event of a primary RDS instance failure. The AvailabilityZone property only needs to be configured if you disable MultiAZ mode and to deal with this we reference a CloudFormation condition which states if the DatabaseSingelAZCondition is true, then run the single AZ RDS instance in availability zone A otherwise we omit the AvailabilityZone property by referencing the special pseudo parameter AWS::NoValue. Let's now define the referenced condition which as you can see is a Boolean expression using the Fn::Equals intrinsic function which will evaluate to true if the DatabaseMultiAZ input parameter is set to false. Let's now create the DatabaseMultiAZ input parameter along with other input parameters we referenced in the RDS instance resource and once complete, we can continue configuring RDS by creating the ApplicationDatabaseDBSubnetGroup resource. Even if you configure a single AZ RDS instance, DBSubnetGroups must include at least two subnets from different availability zones and here we specify the private HighSubnets in AZA and AZB. We can now configure the ApplicationDatabaseSecurityGroup and we configure an empty ingress security policy as we will be configuring separate SecurityGroupIngress and SecurityGroupEgress resources to enable access from the ApplicationAutoscalingGroup to the RDS instance. We also configure a dummy egress security group as by default AWS will permit all outbound access if an egress security group is not defined and here we permit ICMP access to the IP address 192. 0. 2. 0/32 which is a reserved address space which that is un-routable on the public internet effectively blocking any communications from being initiated from our RDS instance. Finally, to prevent circular references within our template, we add SecurityGroupIngress and SecurityGroupEgress resources that permit access from the ApplicationAutoscalingGroup instances to MySQL port 3306 on the RDS instance.

Configuring CloudWatch Log Groups

Central storage of system and container logs is critical to running Docker applications in production and the Microtrader stack uses CloudWatch logs to store all system and container logs. Our stack requires a number CloudWatch log groups including groups for the various types of ECS container instance container logs, for example, Docker logs and ECS agent logs as well as groups that collect log output from each ECS service and associated containers. At an ECS container level the CloudWatch log groups configuration is created at instance creation time via the firstrun Shell script embedded in our custom ECS Amazon machine image. The configuration is based upon the AWS documentation for using CloudWatch logs with ECS container instances and creates a number of log groups as shown on screen. This requires us to create corresponding CloudWatch log groups in our stack which we create using the AWS logs LogGroup resource. We first create log groups for the de-messaged messages in Docker logs as shown on screen and also create log groups for the ECS agent and ECS init logs. The deletion policy attribute allows you to specify whether to retain or delete each log group in the event the log resource is removed from the CloudFormation stack or the stack is deleted in full. You will typically set this attribute to retain for production environments with a setting of delete in non-production environments and because the deletion policy attribute cannot reference an input parameter, we create a special resource type parameter in our development settings that overrides the configure retain deletion policy for each log group in our development environment. When configuring the log group name, we need to ensure the name matches the log group names that will be configured in our ECS container instances and we reference an input parameter of LogRetention to specify the time to retain logs in each log group. Here we configure a default retention of seven days and also set a list of currently allowed values for log retention of CloudWatch logs. We also create four additional log groups, one for each of our Microtrader services and later on in this module we will reference each log group and ECS task definitions for each Microtrader service. Notice that we use a naming convention of stack name, ECS, service name, container name for each log group which clearly identifies the context and purpose of the log group. One other best practice you should follow is to set explicit dependencies for each of the log groups on our AutoscalingGroup meaning our AutoscalingGroup will not be created or updated until after all log groups have been created or updated. Setting explicit dependencies helps avoid race conditions and unexpected behavior which often doesn't occur on stack creation but can happen for a number of CloudFormation stack update edge cases.

Summary

In this module we established an Ansible playbook for the Microtrader stack, created an environment called development and defined all of the supporting resources in the Microtrader stack. We learned how to create EC2 autoscaling groups using the CloudFormation Init framework to perform environment-specific configuration of the custom ECS machine image we built in earlier modules and to also signal to CloudFormation the success or failure of the creation in initial configuration of each instance in our autoscaling group. We created a public application load balancer that exposures the Microtrader dashboard to the public endpoint as well as an internal load balancer that is used for internal communications between the various Microtrader services. We used the new AWS application load balancer and learned how we can create content routing rules to route HTTP requests to different target groups based upon the host head of value or path of the request. We next created an RDS instance and learned how we can leverage CloudFormation conditions to selectively enable or disable multi-AZ failover. And finally, configured CloudWatch log groups which capture system logs from our EC2 autoscaling groups as well as Docker container logs from our ECS services. Throughout this module, we also defined security groups and learned how to avoid circular dependencies between security group resources by using special AWS EC2 security group ingress and egress resources and created IAM policies that work on the principle of least privilege. In the next module we will complete our stack definition by configuring the various ECS resources that comprise the Microtrader application and then deploy, test and troubleshoot our Microtrader stack.

Deploying ECS Applications Using Ansible and CloudFormation

Introduction

Hi! My name is Justin Menga and welcome to deploying ECS applications using Ansible and CloudFormation. We have created an Ansible playbook and CloudFormation template for our Microtrader application stack, and defined all of the supporting resources in our stack, including auto scaling groups, application load balancers, an RDS instance, and CloudWatch log groups. After an introduction to how ECS allocates system resources, including CPU, memory and network ports, we will focus on the ECS CloudFormation resources required in our CloudFormation stack. We will focus on the EC2 container service components of our application, and we'll define an ECS cluster resource, which ECS container instances and the application auto scaling group will join, along with ECS task definitions and ECS services for each of our Microtrader application services. We will then deploy our stack to AWS, which for the most part will be in a working state. However, as we will see, there will be an issue with the application and we will learn how we can troubleshoot and remediate the issue.

ECS System Resources

We have defined the supporting infrastructure in our stack and can now focus on defining ECS resources, including an ECS cluster, ECS task definitions, and ECS services. Before we get started configuring ECS, I want to discuss how ECS allocates system resources, which is an important aspect of understanding how ECS places tasks on ECS container instances and optimizes resource usage for your containers. ECS has three key resources that you need to be aware of, which are CPU, memory and port mappings. We will first discuss CPU and port mappings and then spend some time focusing on memory allocation, which is the most complex resource to manage on ECS. ECS allows you to control CPU resource allocation via CPU reservations, expressed in terms of CPU units, which describe the relative amount of CPU that should be reserved for a container. ECS provides 1024 CPU units per core on each ECS container instance, and these reservations are only applied in the event CPU resources are constrained. For example, if you have two containers on a singe core instance configured with a reservation of 512 units, each container can 100% CPU if the other container is idle. However, if both containers are active, the underlying Docker engine will allocate 50% CPU time to each container, given each container holds equal shares of the total CPU units available. Note that by default ECS will assume two CPU units if you don't configure a CPU reservation for a given container. So, as a best practice you should always configure a CPU reservation appropriate for your application and environment. It is also important to understand that CPU reservations are deducted from the CPU unit count of your ECS container instances. So you can't, for example, place a new container on an ECS container instance that has already allocated all of its CPU units to other containers. ECS also tracks TCP and UDP port mappings on each ECS container instance. For example, if container X registers a host port of TCP port 8000, no other container on that ECS container instance will be able to register port 8000 whilst container X is active. In this scenario, you are limited to only running a single application X container per ECS container instance because the application X container requires a host port of 8000. If you are using application load balancers you can configure dynamic port mapping where your container port is mapped to an available dynamic host port at container startup. The ECS agent is able to determine the dynamic host port that was allocated at container creation, and then registers that port in the application load balancer target group associated with the ECS service. And this approach allows you to run multiple instances of a given container on the same ECS container instance. In our scenario, because we are using Docker host networking mode, there is no concept of static or dynamic port mappings, as our containers directly expose their ports via the ECS container instance host network stack, so we are limited to a single instance of each Microtrader service per ECS container instance.

Understanding ECS Memory Allocation

When configuring memory for your ECS containers you can configure memory reservations, which specify the minimum memory guarantee that you want Docker to provide to your applications, along with a memory limit, which defines the maximum memory available to the container. The AWS CloudFormation reference provides detailed information on the various ways you can configure these settings. However a typical approach is to define both of these settings together and we will adopt this approach in this course. One other important aspect to note is that the EC2 container service always reserves a small amount of memory for the EC2 container instance operating system and ECS agent. For example, on a t2. micro instance which has one gigabyte or 1024 megabytes of memory, ECS reserves 29 megabytes providing 995 megabytes of memory that can be allocated to containers, and you can see the maximum amount of memory available varies depending on the instance type. When configuring a memory reservation value, ECS will deduct the configured memory reservation from the available resources of the ECS container instance. So, you typically should avoid being too generous when configuring the memory reservation value, particularly if you want to run multiple containers on each ECS container instance. In our use case, we may run up to four containers at any one time on a single ECS container instance, in other words one for each of our Microtrader services, and as we will learn later in this module, we also need to run a single adhoc task each time we deploy a new application release, meaning we potentially need to support five containers running at the same time. Hence we configure a memory reservation of 195 megabytes per container for a total of 975 megabytes across five containers, which just fits under the one gigabyte memory capacity of our t2. micro instances. Obviously in the real world, you should size the memory allocated to your containers based upon your application requirements, and for JVM-based applications it is important to also match your container memory configuration to your Java memory settings. By default, assuming you are using Java 8, the JVM allocates a maximum of one quarter, or 25%, of the system memory to the Java heap, which is the primary memory storage area for your application. In our scenario, our instances are t2. micros with one gigabyte of system memory, so by default the JVM will only permit a maximum of 256 megabytes for the Java heap. The other memory consideration is metaspace memory which is used to store Java class metadata. By default this memory space is only constrained by the available memory of the system, so it is possible to exhaust system memory with the default metaspace memory settings. This means we have a default scenario where we can't leverage the available resources of our system with a default Java heap memory allocation whilst our system could potentially become unstable if the JVM attempts to allocate all available system memory to the metaspace memory area. One other issue in container scenarios is that the JVM has no awareness of container memory limits that may have been configured. For example, if we configure a memory limit of 800 megabytes for each container, the JVM has no visibility of this at a system level, therefore assumes that it is working in an environment with one gigabyte of available system memory assuming a t2. micro instance. This can lead to a scenario where the JVM exceeds the container memory limit, which causes Docker to kill the container immediately with an out of memory error. You should always avoid this situation where possible as Docker does not attempt to stop the container gracefully and this forceful termination of the container could have undesirable effects on your application and data. Luckily, the JVM offers extension flags that allow you to override memory defaults and you should always your memory settings to your Docker environment appropriately. In our scenario, we will configure each container with a hard memory limit of 800 megabytes. So ideally you want to ensure our configuration will allow our applications to consume all of this memory if required, and also ensure we do not exceed this memory limit to avoid the unpredictable termination of our application. To achieve this, we will configure our task definitions with a maximum heap size of 672 megabytes and a maximum metaspace memory allocation of 128 megabytes, which increases the heap memory available to our applications and also ensures we do not exceed the container memory hard limit.

Configuring ECS Task Definitions Part 1

Now that we understand how ECS system resources and memory allocation works, let's get started configuring our ECS CloudFormation resources. We will first define an ECS cluster called application cluster, and as you can see, this is a very simple resource with no configuration properties, and it's simply used to logically group a set of ECS container instances together. We next create ECS task definitions, which are a configuration artifact that defines a set of one or more related container definitions and optional volume definitions. Our application requires four ECS task definitions, one for each Microtrader service, and we'll get started by creating a resource called quote task definition for the Microtrader quote service. Because our containers need to run in Docker host networking mode, we first specify a network mode of host, and then define a single container definition including a container called quote. The image property defines the image of the container and we use the Fn Sub intrinsic function to join the base registry and organization values of the Docker image URI with the container repository name and image tag. Here we use the Microtrader image parameter to express the base portion of the URI, and the Microtrader image tag parameter expresses the tag or version of the image to deploy. Let's now create the Microtrader image and Microtrader image tag input parameters, configuring suitable default values for your account. We next define memory settings for the container configuring a reservation of 195 megabytes using the memory reservation property, and a memory limit of 800 megabytes using the memory property. As we discussed earlier, this means that we are configuring Docker to enforce a hard memory limit of 800 megabytes and if system memory is constrained, Docker will attempt to guarantee a minimum of 195 megabytes for the container. We also configure a CPU reservation of 200, which will ensure each container gets at least 20% CPU time if our ECS container instances are under load. In order to match our JVM memory settings to our container memory configuration, we next configure the various environment settings for the container, first configuring the Java tool options environment variable, which provides the ability to pass Java system properties to the JVM process at container startup. Here we configure appropriate memory settings configuring maximum heap size and max metaspace size using the appropriate extension flags we discussed previously. And we also define the various system properties that configure proxy settings for Java applications, which we need to support the Hazelcast EC2 auto-discovery mechanism that communicates with the AWS EC2 API. Notice that we disable the proxy for all internal communications within the internal Route 53 domain, and importantly also disable proxy communications to the EC2 metadata and ECS metadata endpoints, which are accessed via the 169. 254 and localhost address spaces. We continue configuring our container environment settings by specifying a non-default cluster group name and password, and notice that our super secret password is not really that secret. This approach of using plaintext passwords is a big security no-no, and in later modules we will learn how to deal with securely configuring sensitive data, such as credentials. But for now, to keep things simple, we will just specify passwords in plaintext. We next set the cluster AWS enabled environment variable to true, which will enable Hazelcast EC2 auto-discovery in our Microtrader applications. Notice that we configure the various EC2 auto-discovery settings and we configure the same tag key and tag value that we previously associated with the application auto scaling group, allowing Microtrader services deployed to any instance within the auto scaling group to discover other Microtrader service instances. We next configure the http port the quote service rest endpoint will listen on, and configure the http root of the quote service to be /quote, given we are using content routing roles on the internal application load balancer that routes requests to the quote and audit services based upon the http root path. Notice we also configure the http host and http public port environment variables, which configure the host name and port that the quote service will publish in the service discovery location register shared with the cluster. This is configured as the friendly DNS name and port the internal load balancer listens on as we want all traffic to the quote service rest endpoint to traverse the internal load balancer. We can now configure logging for our container enabling the AWS logs or CloudWatch Logs driver and then pass a number of configuration options to the AWS logs driver. Notice that we configure the log group to match the name of the log group we created earlier for the quote service, and configure the correct region that our stack is running in. The AWS logs stream prefix is useful in that it changes the default naming of log streams from the container id to a format that includes the prefix, container name and, most importantly, the ECS task id assigned to the container. This task id is useful for correlating log streams to the various events the ECS service generates as each event is associated with an ECS task id rather than the Docker container id. The next property we configure is the port mapping setting where we specify that our container is listening on the http port, as configured by the Microtrader quote http port input parameter, which we have already defined in our stack. This is required for registering the quote service http endpoint and the internal application load balancer. And because we are using host networking mode, this port will be exposed directly to the outside world. The port mapping also ensures the ECS scheduler is aware of which port resources are in use on a given ECS container instance, which ensures ECS does not attempt to deploy another container that operates on the same port to the same ECS container instance. The final setting we configure for the quote task definition are custom command arguments that will be passed to our container entry point script at container startup. Recall that we previously discussed event bus communications and that we were going to configure static ports for these communications, and the mechanism Vert. x provides us to do this is by specifying a command-line flag called cluster port. Here the hard code 15701 as the quote service event bus port, and recall, in the previous module, we configured security rules that expect our Microtrader services to use the TCP port range of 15701 to 15704 for event bus communications. We also need to configure the event bus port as a container port mapping so that ECS is aware of this port mapping and won't attempt to deploy multiple containers that attempt to buy into the configured event bus port.

Configuring ECS Task Definitions Part 2

We can now configure the dashboard task definition, which is very similar to the quote task definition, first configuring a network mode of host, and then creating a single container definition, the appropriate image, memory and CPU settings, command arguments, port mappings, and log configuration settings. Notice that we configure the dashboard service to use port 15702 for event bus communications, and when configuring environment variable settings we define the same settings we defined for the quote task definition and the Java tool options environment variable, as well as the various cluster-related environment variable settings, and, finally, ensure the dashboard service will listen on the correct http port. We next configure the portfolio task definition, again configuring a network mode of host, specifying the correct Microtrader portfolio image, and defining appropriate memory and CPU settings, command arguments, port mappings, and log configuration settings. We configure the portfolio service to use port 15703 for event bus communications, and because the portfolio service does not expose an http rest endpoint, we only need to configure Java tool options and cluster discovery environment variable settings. The final task we configure is the audit task definition, once again configuring a network mode of host, and defining the various system resource parameters, command arguments, and log configuration settings. And we configure the audit service to use port 15704 for event bus communications. After configuring the Java tool options and cluster discovery environment variables, we configure the http port and http root variables specifying a root path of /audit, which is required given our application load balancer will route all requests matching this path to the audit service. And finally configure the http host and http public port values, which reference the published http endpoint on the internal application load balancer. Because the audit service needs to communicate with the application database RDS instance, we also configure various JDBC settings, including configuring an appropriate JDBC URL, using the Fn Sub intrinsic function, referencing the application database endpoint address attribute, which returns the internal DNS name of the application database resource.

Understanding ECS Service Deployment

We have configured an ECS cluster and ECS task definitions for our Microtrader services. And the final resources we need to create are ECS service resources, which will schedule one or more instances of our ECS task definitions to our ECS cluster and manage rolling deployments using both the public and internal application load balancers. Before we configure these resources I will briefly explain the ECS service deployment mechanism which provides features including connection draining and rolling deployments out of the box. A key trait of ECS services that you need to understand is that whenever you update the task definition associated with an ECS service, a rolling deployment of the ECS service will be automatically triggered, where the ECS scheduler will attempt to replace all of the existing ECS service instances or containers with new containers that are configured according to the new ECS task definition. ECS also integrates with AWS elastic and application load balancers and will automatically orchestrate connection draining for containers that need to be replaced deregistering the container service endpoint from the load balancer or load balancer target group. For example, let's assume we have version 1. 4 of an ECS service that is frontended by an application load balancer, and want to deploy version 1. 5 of the application. After creating a new task definition that references the new application version, ECS will start a rolling deployment, first instructing the load balancer to drain connections to one of the existing service instances. Once connection draining is complete ECS will next deregister the container from the load balancer, and then deploy a new container based upon the new ECS task definition. After the container has started, ECS will register the instance and the load balancer, and assuming load balancer health checks are successful the deployment of the first new service instance completes successfully. The process of connection draining, deregistering service instances, deploying a new container, and waiting for the service instance to pass health checks will repeat until all existing service instances have been replaced, at which point the rolling deployment process is complete. If at any point this process fails, for example a container fails to start, or fails a load balancer health check, ECS will roll back to the previous version. If a rollback is initiated, the same deployment process is followed, except each service instance is replaced with the previous task definition that was in place before the failed deployment. This is an important consideration for your applications as they must be designed such that they are both backwards and forwards compatible by at least one version.

Configuring ECS Services

We have configured an ECS cluster and ECS task definitions for our Microtrader services, and the final resources we need to create are ECS service resources that will schedule one or more instances of our ECS task definitions to our ECS cluster and manage rolling deployments using both the public and internal application load balancers. We first define the quote service, and notice that we define a dependency on the application auto scaling group along with the quote service log group and application load balancer listener resources. We configure the service to deploy the quote task definition to our application ECS cluster and configure a desired count of one as we only ever want a single stream of quotes being generated in our application. We set the minimum health percent in the deployment configuration to zero, which means the ECS scheduler is permitted to take the quote service out of service whenever a new version of a quote service application or task definition needs to be deployed. We also set the maximum percent to 100%, meaning ECS will never attempt to deploy a single instance of our quote service during a rolling update. And this approach ensures our application will never have more than one active quote service instance in service. In the load balancers property, we configure the name of the container and the container port and the quote task definition, which ECS will register with our internal load balancer quote service target group. And we configure the role property to reference an IAM role defined in the ECS service role resource, which grants the EC2 container service the ability to perform the actions defined in the Amazon EC2 container service role managed policy, including the ability to register, deregister, and drain instances from our application load balancer. We next define the audit service, which has dependencies on the application auto scaling, audit service log group, and application load balancer listener resources, as well as the application database resource, given our audit service needs to persist stock transactions to the audit database. After configuring the appropriate references for the cluster and task definition properties, because the audit service can scale to multiple instances, we scale the audit service desired count property based upon the application desired count or desired number of instances in the application auto scaling group. We also configure a maximum percent of 200 in the deployment configuration, meaning the ECS scheduler is permitted to temporarily increase the desired count by up to 200% during a deployment, assuming the ECS cluster has sufficient resources. For the minimum healthy percent we create a condition called application single instance condition, which will evaluate to true, if the application desired count is set to one, and then reference this condition if our stack only has a single ECS container instance, or 50% if we have more than one container instance. This approach means that the audit service can be taken out of service if we only have a single container instance, otherwise we permit ECS to reduce the desired count by up to 50%, if our ECS cluster has more than one container instance, ensuring the audit service remains available in a high availability configuration scenario. Finally, we associate the audit container and port with the internal application load balancer audit service target group, and ensure the ECS service role is attached to the audit service. We can now move on to the portfolio service, which should only start once the audit service has started, so that there is no chance of stock transactions being performed without being audited, and we can achieve this by configuring the portfolio service to depend on the audit service along with the application auto scaling and portfolio service log group resources. After configuring the cluster and task definition properties, because we only want to operate a single instance of the portfolio service, we hard code the desired count to one and configure the deployment configuration such that the ECS scheduler can take the portfolio service out of service and will never deploy more than one instance during a deployment. The final service is the dashboard service, which depends on each of the other Microtrader services along with the application auto scaling, dashboard service log group, and public load balancer listener resources. And we also configure the cluster and task definition properties appropriately. The dashboard service can operate across multiple instances, so we define a configuration that is very similar to the audit service, configuring a conditional minimum healthy percent deployment setting, and finally associate the dashboard container and port with the public load balancer dashboard service target group, and ensure the ECS service role is attached to the dashboard service.

Deploying the Microtrader Stack

We have defined our CloudFormation stack and we are almost ready to deploy our stack to AWS. Before we can do this, we need to define environment settings for the stack in the group_vars development vars. yml file we created earlier. We first configure the STS role ARN variable with the of the ARN of the IAM admin role in our target account, which grants us the necessary privileges to create the CloudFormation stack and associated resources. We next need to define settings that we can apply to the various stack input parameters that we referenced when we created our stack template, and recall that we can configure inputs for our stack parameters using the syntax stack. inputs. parameter name. We first define application environment settings, and notice that I only configure settings for parameters that do not specify default values, as the default values for the other parameters are suitable for our development environment. We next define the database name, username and password, which are mandatory parameters that we must specify per environment. And again notice that we are not following security best practices by specifying a password in cleartext, but we will fix this in later modules. Finally, we configure required Microtrader settings, which include the http ports that are used for the quote, audit, and dashboard services. Now that we have defined environment settings for our stack, we can deploy our stack to AWS by running the make deploy command referencing our development environment. One thing I can tell you about defining large CloudFormation stacks is that you invariably will make mistakes in your template. And as you can see, we are no different with an error occurring during the template generation process. This error is somewhat cryptic although given the reference to details, about configuring YAML syntax, it's likely this is related to a YAML formatting error. The Ansible playbook command supports very very verbose output by specifying the -vvv flag, and the make workflow includes a verbose flag that sets the -vvv flag. So if we rerun the playbook with the verbose flag, notice we get extremely verbose output including full stack traces, which is very useful for debugging and troubleshooting. Here you can see we have made an error and the values applied to the Fn Sub intrinsic function related to our public load balancer resources. And the problem here is that I have missed a leading dollar symbol. Let's run the playbook again, and whoops, we have another error. This time the error occurs when we submit the generated CloudFormation template to the CloudFormation service. And you can see that we have incorrectly named the Fn import value intrinsic function as if Fn import somewhere in our template. Let's now find and fix this error, which is located on the vpc id property of the application auto scaling security group, and rerun the Ansible playbook this time omitting the verbose flag as we should now have fixed all issues in our template. Note that if you encounter any further errors, you can browse to the Microtrader First Deploy link in the Repository Timeline section of the Microtrader Deploy Repository published at the URL shown, which contains the stack template and settings that we have worked through in the last two modules. The will take between 15 to 20 minutes to deploy, and if we open the CloudFormation dashboard in the AWS console and select the Microtrader development stack, we can see the various resources that are created by the Resources and Events tabs. Notice that our auto scaling group creates successfully with success signal from our single instance, and the quote service is the first ECS service that is created. At this point, because the audit service depends on the application database and the remaining Microtrader services depend on the audit service, ECS service creation pauses until the application database has been successfully created, and then proceeds to deploy the audit service, followed by the portfolio service, and finally the dashboard service is deployed. Once stack creation is complete, we can browse to the Outputs tab and copy the URL of the public load balancer to our browser, and you can see that for the most part our Microtrader application has been successfully deployed to AWS.

Troubleshooting the Microtrader Application

If you take a close look at the Microtrader dashboard notice that the Last Operations pane is not displaying the last 10 audit operations and that our http audit service circuit breaker is flapping between a closed, half open and open state. If we navigate to the ECS dashboard, select the Microtrader development cluster and open the audit service, if you refresh the tasks pane a few times over the course of a minute, you should notice that the audit service is flapping up and down. We can confirm this by clicking on the Stopped filter under Task status, and you can see that we have a large number of stopped tasks that were stopped because the task failed load balancer health checks. To determine why these health checks are failing, we need to check the audit service container logs, which we can access directly via the CloudWatch logs link in the task details. If we take a look at the parent log group of the log stream, notice that we have a large number of log streams, one for each task, and that each log stream is named according to our AWS logs prefix. If we open a log stream, notice that we are receiving JDBC errors indicating the audit table does not exist in our application database. The reason for this is that we have not run database migrations as part of our stack deployment, which you may recall are run as part of our local Docker workflow by executing a database migrations class included as part of our audit service. We can take the same approach in AWS by creating a task definition for running database migrations, and then schedule an ECS task from this task definition to our application ECS cluster. We first create a resource called migrate task definition and although the migration task will not listen on any ports, we still need to configure the network mode as host, as our ECS image does not create a default Docker network bridge when operating in host networking mode, and our migration task needs the host networking stack to communicate over the network to our application database. We specify the Microtrader audit image as the Docker image, configuring CPU and memory settings identical to the other task definitions, and create a log configuration that configures the AWS logs driver to log to a log group based upon the stack name and purpose of the task. Let's quickly create this log group resource ensuring we configure a matching name, and we next configure environment settings and a migrate task definition. Although the migrate task does not currently require http proxy access, we will need it in later modules. So we configure the Java tool options to use our http proxy. We specify the various JDBC environment variables required to run the database migrations, and also configure the DB migrations location to use MySQL database migrations instead of the default HSQL migrations. Finally we configure custom command arguments that will run the migrate class in the Microtrader audit service application, and after saving these changes we next need to deploy the updated playbook, which adds the new ECS task definition to our stack. Once the stack deployment completes successfully, if we navigate to the ECS dashboard, we can now run a task that will execute our new migration task definition. We first open the Tasks tab and select the Run New Task button. In the Task Definition dropdown, we select the Migrate Task Definition, and if we expand the Advanced Options pane and migrate task in the Container Overrides section, notice we can specify command and environment overrides providing some flexibility, if you need to run different tasks from the same Docker image and associated task definition. If we click the Run Task button, we should now see a pending task in the Running task list, which should complete within 10 to 20 seconds. If you click the Stopped task status option and then type migrate in the filter box, this will display the task that was just executed. If you click on the task and expand the migrate container, notice that the container completed with an exit code of zero, meaning our migrations ran successfully without error. You should now see the Last Operations pane is populated with stock trading history verifying running the database migrations has resolved the issue. At this point, we have successfully deployed our Microtrader application. But for now it required manual intervention to run database migrations as an ECS task. CloudFormation does not support the ability to create or run ECS tasks natively, and in the next module we will learn how to create a custom CloudFormation resource that will enable us to run database migrations as part of our automated stack deployment process.

Summary

In this module, we successfully deployed our Microtrader application to AWS, using Ansible, CloudFormation, and the EC2 container service. We first discussed how ECS manages system resources, including CPU, memory, and port mapping resources. We learned that ECS offers CPU reservations that allow you to configure a CPU guarantee for your containers and both memory reservations and memory limits, which provide fine-grained control over how you allocate memory to your containers. Correct memory sizing is important for any application and in the case of our Java-based applications, it is important to also tune JVM memory settings to match your container memory configuration. We then defined various ECS resources in the Macrotrader CloudFormation template, which included a single ECS cluster, as well as ECS task definitions and ECS services for each of our Microtrader services. ECS task definitions define the runtime configuration of your containers, whilst ECS services manage the number of container instances you want to deploy and also manage rolling updates whenever you update your ECS task definitions. Finally, after deploying and testing our Microtrader application, we found we had to manually run a critical deployment action, which is to run database migrations, to create or update the audit database schema for our Microtrader audit service. In the next module, we will create a custom CloudFormation resource that allows you to run any ECS task from a given ECS task definition on a given ECS cluster, and we will use this custom resource to automatically run our database migrations as part of our stack deployment process.

Creating CloudFormation Custom Resources Using AWS Lambda

Introduction

Hi My name is Justin Menga and welcome to creating cloud formation customer resources using AWS Lambda. Cloud formation is perhaps my favorite AWS service and one of the reasons for this is the ability to extend cloud formation using custom resources. Custom resources provide the ability to create your own custom provisioning actions which you can use to configure AWS services, resources or features that cloud formation does not support out of the box. as well as perform custom provisioning of external services or resources outside of AWS. This powerful capability allows you to extend cloud formation providing the flexibility to deal with almost any provisioning scenario that you deployments may require. As we learned in the previous module cloud formation does not provide the ability to create and run ECS tasks and in this module we will create a custom resource using AWS Lambda to provide such a capability. This customer resource is very powerful in that you can use it to run any task that you can define in a docker container which could include our use case of running database migrations, as well as other use cases including generating static web content or running a backup job and the list goes on and on. With the addition of this powerful custom resource the end result is cloud formation is transformed into a deployment tool that can orchestrate complex deployment scenarios. We will learn how to program lambda functions using Python. Including how to interact with AWS services in your code and how to build and publish your lambda functions. We will then define our lambda function and the micro trader application stack and create a custom resource that will run database migrations. Enabling fully automated deployment of the micro trader application to AWS.

CloudFormation Custom Resources Overview

Before we get started creating our cloud formation custom resource, it is important to understand how cloud formation custom resources work and how they can be used to implement custom provisioning actions as part of your stack deployment when a custom resource is defined in a cloud formation template and the resource needs to be created, updated or deleted. The cloud formation service will invoke a lambda function defined by a property called Service Token which is a mandatory property that must be defined for all custom resources. Cloud formation will pass in and put request or event to the lambda function which includes the request type, resource properties and a response URL. Providing the lambda function with enough information to perform custom provisioning logic and then respond with the result of the provisioning operation to the cloud formation service. The requests type is either create, update or delete. in midst of the lifecycle of the resource for example the request type would be create if this is the first time the resource is being created or update if the resource already exists but its configuration has been modified in the cloud formation template. Resource properties are custom configuration data that is passed to your lambda function which allow you to specify the necessary information required to perform your custom provisioning actions. For example in our use case of running an ECS task at a minimum we need to provide the ECS task definition we want to run as well as the ECS cluster where we want our task to be executed. The response URL is a pre-signed S3 URL where you are expected to provide a response once your custom resource provisioning has completed. The response is a Jason object it must indicate success or failure of the provisioning action. Which determines where the cloud formation should continue provisioning the stack will roll back to its previous state due to a customer resource provisioning failure. The response can also include information about the provisioned customer resource. Which is expressed in the form of resource data which can be referenced by other cloud formation resources in your stack using the (mumbling) or (mumbling) intrinsic functions. You can find out more about custom resources by reading the AWS cloud formation documentation. Which provides extensive coverage of custom resources and I encourage you to refer to this document to supplement your learning as we progress through this module. In terms of how our specific custom resource will work we will be creating an ECS task runner that communicates with the EC2 container service and attempts to run an ECS task on our ECS cluster which executes database migrations against the order database. It is important to understand that running ECS task is an asynchronous operation. In other words the call to the EC2 container service will return immediately. Meaning your lambda function must pole the status of the ECS task. To determine whether or not it has completed successfully here the ESC task starts up a short time after the request to run the task and proceeds to execute database migrations. The lambda function needs to check the status of the task until the database migrations complete successfully. At which point the ECS Service will respond with the result of the ECS task execution.

Creating a Lambda Function

Now that we understand at a high level how cloud formation custom resources work let's get started creating a simple lambda function in the AWS console to gain through the understanding into cloud formation custom resources and AWS lambda functions. After opening the AWS console we never get to the lambda dashboard. Click on the Get started now button and in the select blueprint screen we select the blank function and click Next. We will call this function my first custom resource and first need to change the run time to Python 2. 7. And the lambda function code section we can define our code and we'll get started by importing the python logging module creating a log object so that we can perform logging in our function. The lambda handle a function is intended to be the entry point that is invoked by the AWS lambda service and notice that an event in context object is supplied to your function. The event object is provided by cloud formation when it invokes your function. While as the context object provides runtime information about the lambda execution environment. Let's first add a logging statement to capture the details of the event objects and we'll create a function that will pretty print the object in a JSON format. Which also deals with any date time values that may be present in the objects. And this custom resource we aren't actually going to do anything other than return a success response to cloud formation and we can achieve this by creating a response object which we convert to a serialized value using the json dumps method. We specify a number of fields that cloud formation expects in the response object. Including the status or results which must be either success or failure, stack ID, request ID and logical resource ID which all must match the ID's provided in the original request from cloud formation and finally the physical resource ID which should be a value that uniquely identifies the resource being created. In the simple example we will just use the logical resource ID provided in the original request from cloud formation which is the name of the custom resource object in our cloud formation stack and is at least guaranteed to be unique in the context of our stack resources. We next create an HTTP request object using the URL lib2 library supplying the S3 response URL from the cloud formation request. The serialized response object is request data and include headers required in the HTTP request when posting a response to an S3 pre-signed URL the HTTP method needs to be set as put and finally we create a try except block that posts the response for cloud formation and also deals with any errors that may occur. In the lambda function handler and role we select a custom role and a new tap should open in the IAM management console asking you to create a new role. If you click on Show policy documents notice that the role includes the minimum permissions required for the lambda service. Which is to be able to perform logging on behalf of your lambda function. After clicking the allow button we are return to the Lambda console and we can now continue and click the create function button to finish creation of our lambda function.

Creating a Custom Resource

We can now create a custom resource in our micro trader cloud formation stack but before we do this we need to copy the ARN value of the lambda function we just created. Which will be required for the service token property of our custom resource. After opening the stack. yml. j2 template in our micro trader deploy project we never get to the resources section and create a new custom resource called my first custom resource specifying a type of AWS cloud formation custom resource. The first property we need to define is the service token property. Where we paste the ARN value of our lambda function which provides the necessary information for cloud formation to invoke our lambda function when it attempts to create, update or delete a custom resource. Given our custom resource will ultimately run ECS tasks we will provide two additional properties that provide the name of our micro trader ECS cluster as well as the of the ARN of the migrate task definition which is the task definition we need to execute to run database migrations. Let's now save our updated stack template and run our playbook to deploy our new custom resource via cloud formation. In the cloud formation dashboard you should see the micro trader stack being updated and after our new custom resource is created successfully if we navigate back to the lambda console and select the monitoring tab we can see that our lambda function has been invoked once with no indication errors. We can view our lambda function logs by clicking the view logs and cloud watch link which opens the cloud watch logs dashboard and noticed that a log group prefixed with AWS lambda is automatically created for our function. If we open the log stream we can see the event that was passed to our function from cloud formation and notice that the request type is create given cloud formation is attempting to create a new resource and the resource properties dictionary includes the cluster in task definition properties we defined in our custom resource. Let's now update the custom resource to include a property of count which could be used to control the number of ECS tasks that we want to run. After running our playbook we should see the custom resource updated successfully in cloud formation and in the logs for our lambda function we can see that the cloud formation requests includes a request type of update and also includes our newly defined count property. One of a new piece of data in the cloud formation request is the old resource properties dictionary which provides the previous configuration of the resource prior to the update. Which is useful for determining what changes have been made to your resource. As a final demonstration let's comment the custom resource in our stack, rerun our playbook to deploy the changes and this time we should see our custom resource is successfully deleted by cloud formation. In the lambda function logs notice that the request type is delete demonstrating cloud formation provides us with the appropriate lifecycle information to create, update and delete our custom resources.

Creating a Custom Resources Project

Now that you have been introduced to the basic concepts of cloud formation custom resources it's time to create a fully fledged custom resources lambda function. We'll get started by creating a new project called lambda ECS tasks in the course root folder and establish the skeleton structure of our lambda project. The structure we will create works well for Python based lambda functions. Supporting all facets of function development including building testing and publishing L functions. We create a makefile which will be used to automate tests build and publish tasks. And create a. getignored file that excludes a local build folder where built out of fix will be stored along with a folder called src/vendor which is where any third party Python libraries our function requires will be located. We next create the SRC folder and create a python file called ecs_task. py which is the location where we define our lambda function code. We also create a file called setup. cfg. With settings required for (mumbling) environments that ensure third party libraries are installed into the correct folder. Finally we create a file called requirements. txt which will be used to specify third party dependencies for our lambda function.

Handling CloudFormation Requests

We can now get started coding our ECS tasks lambda function. And we first add some boilerplate import and configuration settings. Which adds the vendor sub folder into the system path ensuring our function can import python modules installed in the vendor sub folder. This is an approach I use for all of my Python lambda functions, that allows you to cleanly separate third party libraries from your source code. We also add logging configuration and notice that I allow the logging level to be set from the environment. As we can pass environment variables to lambda functions which can be useful for quickly enabling debug logging. Early in this module we created a simple lambda function that dealt with posting an appropriate response to the S3 URL provided in the cloud formation request. This is a common low level task all of your cloud formation custom resources will need to deal with. And rather than deal with us directly in each of your lambda functions. We can import a third party library that deals with this for you. Along with other common low level custom resource handling tasks. Such as ensuring the correct stack ID and request ID are included in the response. The library called cfn lambda handler was created by yours truly and is published as a pre-package. So assuming we have installed this package we can import a couple of classes from this library into our lambda function. The handler class implements the decorator patten passing a custom handler function based upon the incoming requests type from cloud formation to a decorator function in the cfn lambda handler library which performs common pre-processing logic such as appearing a response object executes the custom logic defined in your custom handler function and finally performs common post processing logic before posting a final result to the S3 response URL. Let's now create a handler object from the handler class and later on we will specify this handler object as the point for the lambda function we will create in AWS. Can use the handler object to decorate our own functions. Which will deal with the different types of cloud formation custom resource requests. Notice that we define a function called handle create which we configure to only deal with cloud formation create requests by decorating the function with the @handler. create decorator from the handler object. Note that by default the handler decorator constructs a success response automatically for us. So by simply returning the original event a success response will be generated. In a similar fashion, we can also deal with cloud formation update and delete requests by decorating each function with an appropriate decorator which ensures the handler object will delegate incoming cloud formation requests to the appropriate handler function based upon the request type.

Validating Input Data

Now that we've sketched out the basic code structure let's focus on implementing the logic for the create function. Which will handle the creation of custom resources. The first thing we need to do is inspect the incoming event and validate the event has the information required to successfully create the custom resource. Rather than defining this in line in the function I use a pattern where I separate the validation logic into a separate file. Which I place in a sub folder called lib where I prefer to store common components in sheet code. Here I create a file called validation. py and introduce a third party validation library called voluptuous. Which provides an easy to use domain specific language or DSL. For defining validation schemas the validate function is what we will call from our main lambda function. Which passes and put resource properties to the get validate a function. and returns a schema object that specify the various properties we expect to be passed with our custom resource. In our use case of running an ECS task we require the (mumbling) properties to be provided. And specify these must be defined as a string we also configure a count property making configuration of this property optional by specifying a default value of one and because cloud formation resource property values are always passed as strings we create a custom validator that returns an integer from the count property and specify this value must be between zero and 10. Sometimes we may not want to run any ECS task if our custom resource has been updated. So we add in a run on update property which defaults to true and again uses a custom validator to ensure the supplied string value can be converted to a bullion value. We also add in instances list property which can optionally specify the list of the ECS container instances to run the ECS task and finally define the overrides dictionary property which allows you to override command arguments and environment settings in the configured task definition. The DictToString validator demonstrates the power of _____, which converts any nonstring values in the overrides dictionary to string values which is required when you pass overrides to the EC2 container service API. Finally, we set the extra parameters are true which means extra properties that are not defined in our schema will be permitted but ignored. Before we can import this validation logic into our lambda function we need to create a file called init. py at the root of the lib folder. and here we import the validate function from the validation file which we can then export to our main lambda function back in our lambda function we can now import the validate function from the lib module. In our create handler we create an object called task from the result of the validate function and pass on the resource properties dictionary from the cloud formation request. The task object will be derived from the resource properties dictionary which conforms to the schema we defined in our validation code. If the resource properties do not conform to our schema an exception will be thrown. So we deal with this by importing the appropriate exceptions and then ____ try exit block around our validate call which will return a failure result to cloud formation if a validation exception is raised.

Integrating with the ECS Service

Now that we have elevated the input to our lambda function we can focus on scheduling an ECS task according to the resource properties configured in the custom resource. Again in the interests of separation of concerns in a clean model of code I will take the approach of defining an ECS Task Manager class in a file called ecs. py located in the lib folder. We first need to import the boto3 library which provides the official AWS (mumbling) Python and then create a class called ECS task manager which will handle all ECS task operations. In the ECS task manager constructor we attach a class variable called client which is a boto3 client object. That will communicate with the EC2 container service. We next add method called start task, which is responsible for starting or running an ECS task. You can see we specific cluster tasks definition and started by as required premises. In the start of by parameter is important as it allows you to take ECS tasks with an identifier that you can use to check the status of those tasks in subsequent calls to the EC2 container service. The Boto3 ECS client has two distinct methods for running ECS tasks. A start task method, which starts a task on a list of specified ECS container instances. In a run task method, which schedule a task on ECS cluster but is not concerned with which ECS container instance the task actually runs on. We use the instances parameter to control this behavior meaning if we pass an A list of instances then we will call the boto3 start task method otherwise we will call the run task method. Finally the overrides parameter is an optional dictionary that can be used to pass command argument in an environment variable settings that will override the settings in the specified task definition. We also add a describe tasks, stop tasks, endless tasks method. Which simply call the corresponding Boto3 ECS client methods, as we need to be able to call these methods from our mainland to function to deal with various scenarios. The list tasks method includes a call to a reclusive partial function. They cause the boto3 ECS client list tasks method recursively which ensures paginated results from multiple calls to this method can be returned in a single result. Finally we create custom exception classes which will be used to deal with ECS task failures in our main lamda function. Let's now update the init. py file in the lib folder to export our ECS Task Manager class and shifts back to our main function code where we import the ECS task manager in custom exception classes and proceed to create a new instance of the ECS Task Manager class. Notice that we create this instance outside of our handle create method which brings us to an important aspect of the AWS lambda execution. Any code that is executed outside of your handler may be cached across multiple invocations of your function which means the task manager can be potentially re used across multiple custom resource requests. This improves the performance and efficiency of our ECS task runner. As the underlying Boto3 clients can reuse _____ credentials across multiple function calls without having to reauthenticate on each function invacation. We next deal with creating an appropriate value for the started by parameter which we pass to the ECS task manager start task method. As we discussed earlier this parameter can be used as an identifier to track the status of an ECS task. And if we refer to the boto3 documentation at the URL shown, navigating to the available services ECS start task method we can see the started by value can be a maximum of 36 characters. To ensure our started by parameter can uniquely track the status of our ECS task and fits within the constraint of 36 characters we will concatenate the received stake ID and logical resource ID and then provide the md5 hash of this value as our started by parameter. We achieve this by importing the md5 from the hash lib Python library. Creating a function called Get task ID there returns the md5 of the concatenated values and then attach this to our task dictionary in a property name started by. We can now call the ECS task manager service and to improve readability we create a function called start, that simply returns the result of calling the start task method on our ECS task manager, which we then assign to a task result property in our task dictionary.

Polling ECS Task Status

We then instruct the ECS Service to run a task it is important to understand the school is asynchronous meaning ESC will attempt to a task and return a response immediately once a task has been schedule. This means that our code needs to subsequently poll the ECS service, to determine the status of the task. As we can't return a success or failure result to cloud formation, until we know if the task has finished running and whether or not it completed successfully. To deal with this, we create a function called poll that periodically polls the status of the ECS task in returns once a task is completed. We first specify a poll interval of 10 seconds, controlling how often we check status and next create an infinite loop using a wall true statement all AWS lambda functions have an execution time limit that is currently five minutes. Which means if we are approaching this time limit we should exit with a timeout error. So that cloud formation can detect this failure immediately rather than never receive back a result from our custom resource function. We can detect this by passing in a parameter we refer to as remaining time. Which references the get remaining time in Milli's method that is provided by the context object passed to our handler function. Here we check if the remaining time in milli seconds is least in our poll interval plus a few seconds and if this is the case, we raise a CFN lambda execution timeout which is an exception imported from the cfn lambda handle library that we will discuss in more detail later on. For now we need to catch the exception in our handler function. Ensuring the result prospect cloud formation is a failure with an appropriate reason. Back in our polling function we next check if our task has completed which we delegate to a function called check complete along with the result object. The task result is originally returned from the call to the start method on the ECS task manager. Which returns a dictionary object with a tasks property that includes a list of created tasks in the current status. As well as any failures that may have a occurred. When polling the task status we are inspecting the last status property and waiting for this to change to a value of stopped at which point we are able to inspect the exit code of any containers associated with the task to determine if the task is completed successfully. The check complete function first checks of there have been any failures, returned from the call to start task. Which for example could happen if you specified an invalid cluster or task definition and if any failures are detected we raise an ECS task failure error exception which is defined alongside of our ECS Task Manager class and again we need to deal with this exception in our main handler function. We next obtain the tasks property from the task result and to check if the task is completed we use a simple list comprehension that checks if all tasks have a last status of stopped. In the event the call to check complete returns false our poll function needs to sleep for the poll interval. so we import the python time library and call the time. sleep function to polls execution. After sleeping 14 seconds we next need to update the status of our ECS tasks, which we delegate to a function call describe tasks. This function extracts the task ARN property from the initial start task call to the ECS task manager and then calls the described tasks method on our ECS task manager passing the ECS cluster in a list of tasks ARN to check the status of. At this point we invoke a new iteration of our loop which will check if the updated status of the tasks is now completed and we finally need to add logic for when our tasks actually complete. Once our ECS tasks have stopped we need to check each of the containers associated with that task verifying the exit code of the container is zero. Meaning the operation defined in the container completed successfully without error. We do this by creating a check exit codes function which extracts the list of tasks from the task result and uses a list comprehension to catch any containers that exit with a non-zero exit code or error result. If this is the case we raise an ECS task exit code error passing the list of tasks in non-zero containers which we need to handle appropriately in our main handler function. Finally assuming the call to check exit code determines all containers completed successfully we return the task result from our poll function which we update in the task object in our main handler function. At this point we log the result and specify the task ARN as a physical resource ID that is returned to cloud formation. Is this includes the ECS task identifier which we can use to locate through the information about the task from the ECS console in cloud watch logs.

Building Lambda Functions

At this point we have completed the initial version of our ECS task Custom resource function and in order to test our custom resource with our cloud formation stack. We need to now build and publish our lambda function. We first will define the build task in our Makefile and to ensure a clean build, we first call the clean task, which removes any compiled python files along with the src/vendor in build folders. We next change into the src folder and run (mumbling) install command using the -t flag to instruct pip to install third party libraries into the vendor sub folder and to also reference the requirements. txt file which specific third party dependencies. Let's now quickly add these dependencies and requirements. txt. Which includes the cfn lambda handler library that decorates our handler functions in the voluptuous library for all validation logic. You may notice that we do not include the AWS boto3 Python stk as the python lambda execution environment already includes boto3 libraries. We next create a local build folder to house our lambda function distribution and then create a zip file called ecsTasks. zip. That includes all files in our src folder excluding compile python files that we may have created to configure our make file more generically we extract the name of our zip file to a parameter called function name which we define with a default value of ecs tasks at the top of our Make file. We can now build our function by running make build and notice this and stools all third party dependencies into the src/vendor folder and finally create a zip file called esc tasks in the local build folder.

Publishing Lambda Functions

The zip file created by a build process is ready for consumption by AWS lambda. And although we can upload this file manually through the AWS lambda console, ultimately we want this to be fully automated and the best approach is to store distribution in an S3 bucket which AWS supports as its recommended mechanism to store your lambda function code. In earlier modules the call we created a cloud formation resource stack and that this stack included an S3 bucket intended for storing lambda functions. The bucket is named according to a standard naming convention of your account ID-cfn-lambda and back in our make file we define a variable called S3 buckets that defines the S3 bucket where we want to store our lambda function distribution. To copy out distribution to the S3 buckets we use the AWS S3 cp or Copy command specifying the quiet flag and copying our local zip file to the S3 bucket URL. One important aspect of the cfn lambda bucket is that it has S3 visioning enabled, meaning each time we overwrite an existing object rather than destroying the previous object S3 will create a new version of the object meaning we can access both the current version along with the older versions of the objects. This is useful for storing lambda distributions and provides a basic form of version control. As we can specify the S3 version of our lambda function code distribution when we create a lambda function. To determine the S3 objects version when we upload a new distribution we can use the AWS S3 API list object visions command. Referencing the S3 bucket and distribution file name and use a James path query expression to extract the version of the latest object. James path expressions are very powerful as can be demonstrated by running a few examples from the command line. If we copy the lambda distribution to the S3 bucket a few times to create multiple S3 object versions We can then query the json output of the list object versions command and specifically determine the latest version of the objects by adding a query where as latest (mumbling) We can then extract the version ID parameter of the latest S3 object and use the text output option to print the string value of the version ID to the console. This is exactly what we do back in our make file. Capturing the vision and a variable called S3 object version which we can then eco back to the end user and now make publish task. Let's now test out our publish task by simply running the make publish command and the publish operation should complete in a few seconds with the S3 object version published to your console. We can now open the AWS console navigate to the S3 dash board and open the cfn lambda bucket for your account. If we select the ecstask. zip file and click on the latest version dropdown you can see the two versions we copied manually along with the most recent version that we published using our make publish task.

Creating Lambda Functions in CloudFormation

Now that we have published our lambda function code we are ready to create a lambda function in our cloud formation stack. Rather than creating lambda functions manually we can make out stacks completely self-contained by creating the lambda functions that perform resource provisioning as part of our cloud formations stack. If we switch over to the micro trader deploy project and open the stack. yml. j2 file. Let's create a new resource called ECS task runner, which we configure as a lambda function resource and specify this resource depends on another resource called ECS task runner log group that will store our lambda function logs. After quickly creating the ECS task runner log group resource. We can figure our lambda function with a suitable name and description derive from our stack name. And then define the handler that lambda should call when invoking our function which is the handler decorator object from the cfn lambda handler library. We specify the minimum memory size of 128 megabytes configure the run time as Python 2. 7 and specify the maximum execution timeout which is currently 300 seconds in the code section we define the S3 bucket based upon our bucket naming conventions and then specify the name of our lambda function distribution is the S3 key property.
