We also specify the S3 object version in reference and input parameter called ECS task runner version which we add to the parameters section and also define the corresponding setting in our development vars. yml setting file. Using the object version that was output when we published our ECS task distribution. Finally we attach the ARN of a Iam resource called ECS task runner role. Which grants the required ECS and cloudwatch log privileges required by our lambda function. Let's now create the ECS task runner role resource which specifies the lambda service as the principal in the Assume role policy document In the attach _____ policy. That grants the ECS task to run a function the required privileges to create ECS tasks. We first add a statement that allows the function to write log events to the ECS task run a log group and then grant the lambda function the various permissions to start, stop and describe ECS tasks. Notice that we use a constraint to restrict these actions to adjust the ECS cluster defined in our stack which follows the principle of least privilege and increases the security of your implementation.

Deploying CloudFormation Custom Resources

Now that we have added our lambda function and associated resources to our stack. Let's now rename the my first custom resource to migrate task and update the service token property to reference the ARN of our lambda function resource. We can leave the other settings as is. Although, we can remove the count property as our lambda function will default the count to one. We also need to add some dependencies into our stack. To control the order in which resources related to our custom resource are deployed. We want to ensure that our migrate task is only ever executed once the application order scaling group and application database resources have been created. And we also want to ensure migrations are executed before ordered service is deployed which we can achieve by adding a dependency on the migrate task to the ordered service. Let's now save our stack and deploy our changes by running the ansible playbook command. If we never get to the cloud formation dashboard in the AWS console we can see the various resources for our lambda function are created. And once these resources have been provisioned our new migrate task custom resource is created we can see this complete successfully and notice that the physical resource ID of the resource represents the task ID of the executed ECS task. We can now verify our migrate task was actually run by first browsing to the lambda console where we can see our newly created function. If we open the function and select the monitoring tab we can see a single invocation of our function with no eras and in the clear watch logs for the function. Our function responded to the cloud formation create request with a status of success. Notice that the logs task result shows the migrate container exited with an exit code of zero indicating that the migrate task did indeed complete successfully. If we navigate to the microtrader development ECS tasks migrate low group which is where logs associated with the migrate task definition are stored we can see the log stream associated with the custom resource physical resource identifier and open the log string to verify our database migrations executed successfully.

Handling Update and Delete Requests

We have successfully implemented the logic for dealing with cloud formation create requests and now need to add support for handling update and delete requests. An update request will be sent by cloud formation we need any of the properties defined for our custom resource change. Given an ECS task is not a persistent or long running resource such as a database or order scaling group. The concept of update is a little bit different here and really what it means is whether or not we want to run a new ECS task. Any time the customer resource is updated in our use case the typical scenario when an update would occur to the migrate task custom resource is due to the migrate task definition resource being updated for example, a new version of the application being deployed an application change could result in a database schema modification so for our use case we want to ensure database migrations are always executed on any update to the migrate task custom resource. Given an update to our ECS task custom resource will result in a new ECS task being created. The logic for the update handler will be similar to the Create handler. Rather than copy and paste the same logic for running a new ECS task we can refactor out the common logic to a couple of functions called create task and start in pole which collectively create a task object and then start pole and finally re-tune. The task ARN of the executed task once it completes successfully. We can also refactor out the extensive exception handling to a separate file called errors. py in the lib folder. implementing the decorator pattern to create error handler decorator function that we can use to implement standard error handling logic here we take the decorated function as an input in return a handler function that will rap execution of the decorated function with the exception handling logic we originally defined for the create handler. This is implemented by invoking the decorated function inside the try block catching any exceptions that may be raised and finally returning the success or failure event. Depending on whether or not the decorated function completed successfully. We need to export our error handler decorator and the error. py file in the lib folder. Import the error handler into our main lambda function and then decorate our handle create. Handle update and handle delete functions with the new decorator. We can now remove the try block from our handle create method and the resulting code is much more concise and cleaner to read. Now that we've refected our handle create code to be reusable the implementation of the handle update function is almost identical. Except we only run a new task if the run on update property is set to true one other improvement we should also implement is to consider the count property in both the handle create and handle update functions. As we should only run a task if the count property is greater than zero. The final cloud formation request type we need to handle is the delete requests in the context of running an ECS task delete can mean one of two things. Firstly, in the event a previously created ECS task is still running in a delete request is received we should stop the running ECS task and return success. Otherwise, if the previously created ECS task has already completed then we should not do anything and just return success. To determine if the ECS task associated with our custom resource is still running. We need to calculate the started by Tag value that was applied to the original create ECS task request and then call the ECS Task Manager list tasks method to list all tasks running on the ECS cluster they match the started by tag. We then loop through each task and coolly stop task method on the ECS task manager. This method will stop the ECS task if it is running it'll return immediately without doing anything if the task is already stopped. Let's now build and publish our updated lambda function and update the development settings in our microtrader deploy project with the new object version of the updated function. Let's also see if the run on Update property to true on the migrate task resource and proceed to execute the updated playbook and the cloud formation console we see that our ECS task run a function is updated and in the migrate task resource is subsequently updated successfully. If we check the logs of our ECS task run a function We can see the cloud formation event with a request type of update. That an ECS Task was created and that the migrate task completed successfully with an exit code of zero.

Handling Long Running ECS Tasks

One limitation of AWS Lambda is that your functions can only currently execute for a maximum of five minutes. Although, this is not an issue in our scenario you may have provisioning tasks that take longer than five minutes to complete. So being able to overcome this limitation certainly would be useful. CFN lambda handle library includes support for a fourth operation called poll which is invoked automatically CFN lambda handler decorator function detects a CFN lambda execution timeout exception at the moment, we are dealing with this exception ourselves and our code. However if you let this exception be handled by the CFN lambda handler decorator it will attempt to automatically reinvoke your lambda function. And signal to the invoke function a poll event by adding a property called event status with a value of poll. The newly invoked CFN lambda handler will automatically detect the poll event for us and invoke a handler in your lambda function that is decorated with a pole handler allowing you to continue polling the status of your provisioning actions up to a configurable absolute timeout period Let's now this handler to our lambda function. And you can see this follows the same pattern as our create update and delete handlers rather than attempting to recreate a task object from the original cloud formation requests you can store the task result from the previous function invocation in a property called Event State. So that on a poll event you can retrieve the previous state of your function from this property. If we cannot locate the previous state we return a failure response in the event we retrieve the previous task state and the task completes successfully we return success response. Note that the event state property is set by the CFN lambda handler library on the previous invocation of the lambda function by passing the task object to the CFN lambda execution timeout exception which is raised when the remaining execution time of the lambda function is about to expire. We also need to modify our error handling so that we don't trip and handle the CFN lambda execution time out exception and see if the underlying CFN lambda handler decorator handled this exception which will automatically invoke the desired polling behavior to avoid our lambda function from running forever we also need to see a timeout property on our custom resource which we add to our validation schema. (mumbling) which is the current amount of time cloud formation will wait for a custom resource to complete before failing resource creation and rolling back stack deployment. We also need to set the timeout property on the cloud formation custom resource event which the CFN lambda handler decorator will use as an absolute timeout when determining whether to continue polling each time your function reaches the execution time out. Let's now build and publish our updated function and once complete update the output is three object vision and now microtrader deploy development settings. We can now test out a long running task which we can simulate by creating a new custom resource that calls our migrate task definition but includes an overrides property that allows you to override command arguments in environment variables passed to the container. Here we create a resource called long running task specifying a time out of ten minutes and overriding the command arguments such that the task will simply sleep for six minutes which exceeds the maximum lambda execution time out of five minutes ensuring ECS task run a function will timeout. invoke our polling behavior. One final configuration task we need to perform is to update the IAM role attached to the lambda function to grab the function the ability to invoke itself which is what the CFN lambda handler library will do when a CFN lambda execution time out error is raised. After updating the IAM role, we can now run our updated deployment playbook and in the cloud formation console we should see the long running task custom resource being created which then sits in a create in progress state for some time. If we never get to the CloudWatch logs for the ECS task from a lamda function we can see the command override specified in our custom resource and notice that the function is periodically polling the ECS task status. After approximately five minutes or so you should see a new invocation of the function with an event type of poll. Notice that the state from the original execution of the function is for provided in the event state property allowing our function to continue polling the status of the long running ECS task. After about a minute or so the sleep command in the long running task should complete and the function returns a success response to cloud formation. Back in our cloud formation console we can see that the long running task resource took approximately six minutes to create and that the reason was created successfully confirming long running task support for our stack is working correctly. We can now remove the long running task resource from our cloud formation stack and deploy the changes to the microtrader deploy playbook which also conveniently will test the delete function of our custom resource. If we open the lambda log groups for our function we can see that the delete event is handled by function successfully and over in the cloud formation console the delete action on the long running custom resource should complete successfully. At this point, we have successfully completed implementation and testing of our ECS Task runner custom resource. A final test for you to complete is to destroy your current microtrader environment and then redeploy a new instance of the microtrader stack if everything goes to plan the stack should now run database migrations automatically before starting the ordered service ensuring the ordered service starts successfully without any errors and our microtrader application is now being deployed successfully in a fully automated manner.

Summary

In this module we learned how to create cloud formation custom resources using the AWS Lambda service to define our custom provisioning logic in a python lambda function. This powerful capability enables us to perform almost any provisioning tasks you can think of within the deployment lifecycle of your cloud formation stacks. Transforming cloud formation into an extremely powerful and flexible deployment tool. When creating a custom resource you must be able to handle the lifecycle of cloud formation resources which includes create update and delete lifecycle actions. A reasonable amount of low level boilerplate code is required to support cloud formation custom resources However, you can leverage the CFN lambda handler library introduced in this module to eliminate much of the spoiler plight which ultimately makes your lambda functions easier to read and manage. CFN lambda handler library also makes it very easy to support long running provisioning actions that exceed the current lambda execution time out of five minutes. We learned how to create a very powerful custom resource that allows you to execute any arbitrary ECS task as a deployment action which in the case of our microtrader stack allows us to run database migrations before attempting to start our microtrader application. We also learned how to create a project for the customer resource lambda function how to structure and develop your code and interact with AWS services and then build and deploy your lambda function distribution to an S3 bucket. Finally, we learned how to define the lambda function and associated custom resources within our microtrader stack allowing you to include your custom resources in the same self-contained infrastructurrized code manner as all of your other stake resources.

Managing Secrets in AWS

Introduction

Hi, my name is Justin Menga, and welcome to Managing Secrets in AWS. Secrets management is a challenging, yet critical problem to solve for any application or service that you will operate in production, and in this course we have already introduced a couple of secrets management problems in our current Microtrader stack. One of these problems is that we are using insecure passwords that are visible in plain text for the world to see. If your code is checked into a public repository visible to the outside world, this is obviously extremely insecure and a recipe for disaster, and even if you are using a private repository, you should never, ever store passwords or credentials in a plain text format. The other problem is even if we address the problem of securely storing our credentials, we next need to deal with providing these credentials securely to our Docker containers. One obvious approach to this would be to inject the plain text credentials as environment variables, which is what we are currently doing, however, this is also not considered good security practice, as it is possible to leak your credentials via your docker configurations, docker logs, and the docker inspect command. This, of course, raises the question of just how do you ensure security of your credentials, yet still leverage the power of Infrastructure as Code, fully automated deployments, and twelve factor applications where configuration is provided via the environment. In this module, we will answer all of these questions, and implement a solution that leverages native AWS services with minimal cost and effort. We will learn how to leverage the EC2 Systems Manager service to securely store secrets in an encrypted format using AWS Key Management Service, or KMS Keys. We will also create a CloudFormation custom resources that provisions our secrets, and finally learn how we can consume these secrets in a secure manner inside our Docker containers, and in other CloudFormation resources.

Introducing the EC2 Systems Manager

There are many different approaches and solutions to secrets management, and rather than discuss the various options and pros and cons of each, I will instead just provide you with an approach that works well for ECS-based applications. Before we explain this approach, we need to introduce the EC2 System Manager, which is a service provided by AWS that is designed to support configuration management and provisioning of EC2 instances. The EC2 System Manager has a number of useful features, and one feature that we will use is the Parameter Store, which is a key value store private to your AWS account and region that can be used to store configuration parameters. The Parameter Store supports a parameter type called Secure String, which allows you to store string values in an encrypted format with encryption provided by the KMS, or Key Management Service, Another important feature of the Parameter Store is that you can control access to each parameter using IAM policies, providing a very effective means to control access to encrypted parameters or secrets stored in the Parameter Store.

Secrets Management Solution Overview

Now that we've been introduced to the EC2 Systems Manager, let's walk through how we will leverage this service to provide an end-to-end secrets management solution. First, we will define CloudFormation custom resources, where each resource represents a single simple systems manager or SSM parameter. Note that the EC2 Systems Manager is referred to as Simple Systems Manager, or SSM, when interacting with the AWS SDKs in CLI, so I will refer to all of these terms interchangeably throughout this module. Although CloudFormation includes native support for provisioning SSM parameters, it does not currently support secure string parameters, which means we need to create our own custom resource in order to provision our secrets securely. The custom resource will be configured in terms of a Name, Key, KmsKeyId, and optional secret value properties. With each of these properties passed to an SSM SecretsProvisioner Lambda function, which will be invoked whenever CloudFormation needs to create, update, or delete the custom resource. The Name property specifies the name of the SSM parameter that will be created, which must be unique within your AWS account and region. So we will incorporate the name of the stack to set the appropriate scope or context of the SSM parameter. In our implementation, the parameter value will be expressed in the form of a key value pair, with the key property specifying the key portion, whilst the value property specifies the actual secret value. Note that the secret value can be provided to the custom resource in an encrypted format. Or in the case this value is our method, a random secret will be automatically generated for you, and the KmsKeyId, which should not be confused with the Key property, specifies the ID of the KMSKey that will be used to encrypt the secret value in the EC2 Systems Manager. As a security best practice, this KMSKey ideally should b generated as part of your stack, however, to simplify this module, we will just leverage the KMSKey that you created as part of the CloudFormation resources stack, whose key-id is published as a CloudFormation export. Once the SSM parameter has been provisioned, other resources in the stack can access the SSM parameter and associate its secret value. For example, we will configure our Microtrader Docker images to obtain a configurable set of SSM parameters on container startup, inject the parameter key value parts into the container environment, and then start the application. This process happens internally within the container at container startup, ensuring we do not leak secrets via our ECS task definitions, or by accessing Docker logs or using the docker inspect command. We will also configure our custom resource to expose the SSM secret credential value as a plain text value to other stack resources that do not support EC2 systems manager. For example, RDS does not currently support EC2 Systems Manager, so we can expose the secret value to RDS by referencing the value attribute of our custom resource. Note that CloudFormation does not log or expose resources attributes via the CloudFormation API or CloudFormation logs, so this approach ensures your credentials are not leaked externally, and only resources that you specify inside your stack have visibility of the secret. The end result is that our applications and services can securely access credentials and other secrets as required for their needs, and in this example, ECS-based container applications can securely inject a database secret into the local container environment, which allows the application to connect with the correct credentials to the RDS database resource.

Creating the Secrets Provisioner

Let's get started on implementing our secrets management solution by first defining the code that will be executed by the secret provisioner Lambda function. Just like in the previous module, we will create a new project in our course root folder called lambda-secrets-provisioner, and scaffold out the skeleton of the project structure. I'll skip over the detailed description of what we are doing here, given we already covered the project structure in detail in the previous module. We first create a. gitignore file specifying files to exclude from the repository, and then copy the contents of the Makefile we used in the Lambda ECS tasks repository, changing the function name variable to secretsProvisioner. We next create the src folder, create the setup. cfg file required for Mac OS environments, and specify third-party dependencies in requirements. txt. Finally, we create a file called secrets-provisioner. py, which is where we will host the core of our Lambda function, and in the interests of expediency, rather than separate out common components into a lib subfolder like we did in the previous module, we will define all of our code in this file. We first use the same boilerplate structure from the previous module, which loads third-party dependencies from the vendor subfolder, configures logging, creates the handler decorator from the cfn-lambda-handler library, and creates the skeleton handler functions for each CloudFormation request type. We also create a couple of boto3 clients, one for the ECS Systems Manager, or SSM service, and the other for the Key Management, or KMS Service. We next need to validate and generate an input data structure from the received event. Here we use the voluptuous library to define a simple schema that specifies the Name, Kay, and KmsKeyId properties as required values, with the value property defined as optional with a default value of none. In our handle create function, we then create a secret object by passing the received ResourceProperties dictionary from the received event to our schema. Now normally at this point we would introduce a try accept block or an error decorator to capture any validation errors, but again, for the sake of expediency I won't include such logic in this example. Assuming we generate a valid secret object, we need to create a unique identifier for the secret, which we will eventually return to CloudFormation as the physical resource ID of the custom resource. We next determine whether or not we need to generate a random password, which can be determined by simply checking if the value property is none. If this is the case, we call a simple generate_password function, which generates a random password of a configurable length from a configurable set of characters, which requires import of the random and string system modules. In our use case, we will simply hardcode the password length in character set, however, you could make these configurable properties on the custom resource. In the event a secret value is supplied in the custom resource properties, we assume that this is a KMS encrypted value, and use the boto3 KMS client to decrypt the value, which requires us to import the base64 library as KMS encrypted values are base64 encoded. At this point, we have the information we need to provision the secret into the EC2 Systems Manager Parameter Store. The boto3 SSM service is used to interact with the EC2 Systems Manager, and we can call the put-parameter method to create a new parameter. Here we specify the name of the parameter, which must be unique to your account and region, set the parameter type as SecureString, and specify the KMSKeyId that the EC2 Systems Manager should use to encrypt the secret value. Notice that we provision the secret in a format of Key = Value, and this approach will make it very easy for our containers to export the value into the container runtime environment with the appropriate environment variable name and value. Finally, we set the Overwrite flag to true, meaning we will overwrite the parameter if it already exists. We next need to take our parameter with the unique identifier we generated earlier. This is important for being able to uniquely identify parameters, especially when it comes to deleting parameters, which we will discuss later. Here we call the add_tags_to_resource method, specifying a resource type of parameter, the ResourceId as the name of the parameter, and then add a tag with a key of Id and a value of our unique identifier. The final actions we need to perform in our handle create function is to set the PhysicalResourceId to the unique identifier, and return a single data attribute called Value, which is the plain text value of the secret. This attribute can be referenced in our stack for resources such as RDS database instances that do not support interacting directly with the EC2 Systems Manager. Recall that CloudFormation does not expose data attributes externally in CloudFormation logs or via the CloudFormation API. So, in this regard, our approach is considered secure, however, the cfn_lambda_handler library, which handles low-level requests and response processing for our custom resource function, by default logs the entire response, including our data attributes, along with the plain text value of our secret. To overcome this issue, the handler decorator object constructor includes an optional parameter called secure_attributes, which is a list of the attribute values that should not be logged, so we simply need to add the value attribute as a secure attribute in the handler constructor, to prevent our secret value from being logged to CloudWatch logs. Now that we've finished adding support for create requests, we can consider the use case of update requests. In our scenario, given we always overwrite existing parameters, the update use case is identical to the create use case, so we can simply add the handler. update decorator to the handle_create function. As a final task, we need to handle delete requests, and after creating and validating the secret object, we need to determine if we should delete the parameter. Deciding whether or not to delete the parameter is based upon two criteria. First we need to determine if the parameter currently exists, and secondly, we need to ensure the delete request is associated with the current unique identifier of the provisioned parameter. Here we call the get_parameters method on the SSM client to query the existence of the parameter, and if the parameter does not exist, it will be included in the InvalidParameters list that is returned from the call to get_parameters. Assuming the parameter exists and is not included in the InvalidParameters list, we next call the the list_tags_for_resource method, and process the returned TagList property in a list comprehension, which allows us to determine if the Id value tag to the parameter matches the physical resource identifier of the custom resources. If the identifier matches, the next function will return the tag value and the expression will evaluate to true. Otherwise, a value of none will be returned, and the expression will evaluate to false. In the event the physical resource identifier of the custom resource matches the ID tagged on the parameter, we proceed to delete the parameter by calling the delete_parameter method, and we can then return the event object to CloudFormation, meaning CloudFormation will always receive a success response regardless of whether or not we actually need it to delete the parameter.

Creating Secrets Using CloudFormation

Now that we've coded up our secrets provisioner, let's run make build to create the zip file for our function, and then run make publish to upload the zip file to the CfnLambdaS3Bucket. Over in the Microtrader deploy project, we can now create a Lambda function in our stack, which is similar in configuration to the EcsTaskRunner function we created in the previous module. We first create the SecretsProvisioner Lambda function resource, specify this depends on a CloudWatchLogsGroup, and configure the name and description of the function. We set the Handler to the cfn_lambda_handler decorator object, configure the Runtime as python2. 7, reference the correct S3Bucket and Key of our Lambda function distribution, and finally, attach an IAM Role to the function. Let's now create the SecretsProvisionerLogGroup resource, and proceed to create the SecretsProvisionerRoleIam resource. Here we add permissions for the function to be able to get, put, delete, and take SSM parameters, encrypt and decrypt secrets using the CfnMasterKey, and finally, allow the function to write logs to CloudWatchLogs. Notice that we constrain the function to only modify or delete parameters that are prefixed with the stack name, ensuring our function cannot accidentally modify or delete parameters outside of the CloudFormation stack. With our Lambda function and supporting resources in place, we can now create custom resources for the secrets in our stack. In the existing Microtrader stack, the database password and cluster group password are currently configured with plain text values, so we will create database secret and cluster secret custom resources to secure each of these passwords. We first configure the database secret, configuring the ServiceToken property with the Arn of the SecretsProvisioner function, and set the name of the parameter to say StackName /database/password. One important point is whenever you use a path syntax for naming your parameters, you must include a leading forward slash, otherwise, the EC2 Systems Manager will return an error when attempting to create or update the parameter. We also configure a key value of JDBC_PASSWORD, specify the KmsKeyId that should be used to encrypt the secret, and finally, configure a predefined value for the secret referencing the existing database password input parameter, which we will need to define securely by encrypting the secret value using the AWS Key Management service. We can encrypt this credential using the CfnMasterKey that exists in the CloudFormation resources stack by first obtaining the KmsKeyId of the CfnMasterKey using the AWS CloudFormation list-exports command, and then use the aws kms encrypt command to encrypt the secret value with the key-id. We can now copy the returned cipher text blob value as an input to our custom resource. And for now, I will just paste the value directly in the custom resource value property, as we haven't yet added support to our containers for consuming ECS Systems Manager secrets. We next configure the cluster secret in a similar manger to the database secret, specifying a parameter name of StackName /cluster/password, and setting a key that matches the environment variable name of CLUSTER_GROUP_PASSWORD. Notice that we don't specify a secret value, which means a randomly-generated secret will be created by the ClusterSecret custom resource. One other thing to note is when you are generating a random secret, you may be wondering how you can change the secret, which you may want to do periodically or in the event the secret has been compromised. This can be achieved by adding what I refer to as a trigger environment variable to the custom resource, which is some value that is not used by the custom resource itself, but can be used to trigger an update to the resource by simply changing the trigger value. Let's now save our changes, switch to the command line, and deploy our updated stack using the Ansible playbook command. And over in the CloudFormation console, after creating the various supporting resources, the DatabaseSecret and ClusterSecret custom resources should be created successfully. If we switch over to CloudWatch logs, and open the log group for the secretsProvisioner Lambda function, you can see that the secret value attribute has been masked, which ensures the confidentiality of the secret is maintained. We can verify our secrets were created correctly by running the aws ssm get-parameters command, and specifying the name of each parameter. Notice that the secrets were successfully provisioned with a type of SecureString, and that the returned value in both cases is encrypted. You will notice that the database secret encrypted value is different from the input value, and this is because the custom resource first decrypts the input value, and then re-encrypts the plain text value using the configured KmsKeyId, giving you the option to use completely different KMS keys to store your secret securely under version control, and storing your secrets in the EC2 Systems Manager parameter store. If we rerun the command with the with-decryption flag, we can see that each encrypted value is nothing more than a simple key value pair with the returned plain text value of the database secret matching the plain text value of our input database password, and we can also see that a random password was generated for the cluster secret.

Injecting Secrets at Container Startup

Now that we have established a provisioning mechanism for secrets in our stack, we next need to focus on enabling our applications in other resources to be able to access each secret. In our scenario, we have a database secret that represents the database password used by the Audit service to authenticate to the Audit database, and a cluster secret used by all Microtrader services to authenticate joining to the cluster. We will now implement a mechanism that you can apply for injecting secrets in a manner that is completely agnostic of the application running in your container. Earlier in this course, we added an entry point script to the Microtrader base image, which provided the ability to run custom initialization tasks at container startup. Currently, we are using the entry point to run the confd utility to generate configuration files on the fly from environment variable settings passed to the container, and we can now extend the entry point script to also inject secretes securely into the container environment. We will add the ability to inject secrets from the EC2 System Manager Parameter Store by first checking if an environment variable called SSM_SECRETS is defined. If this variable is defined, the value of the variable is expected to contain the names of one or more secrets that should be injected into the environment, and we can obtain the plain text value of each of these secrets by using the aws ssm get-parameters command. Here we specify the value of the SSM secrets variable for the names flag, and use an appropriate JMESPath query, along with the with-decryption and text output flags to only output the plain text value of each secret. If we quickly shift to the console and run this command to query the secrets we have already created, you can see that only the plain text values of each secret are returned in a key = value format, with each returned value printed on a separate line. Back in the entry point script, we next loop through each secret value, and given the secret value is in a key = value format, we simply export the value into the environment, which in the example of injecting the database and cluster secrets, would be evaluated as export JDBC_PASSWORD = the database password, and export CLUSTER_GROUP_PASSWORD = the cluster group secret value. Note that this method of injecting environment variables inside the entry point script is completely internal to the container, and these environment variables will not be leaked to the outside world in Docker configuration, Docker system logs, or via the Docker inspect command. There is one more task we need to perform in our microtrader-base image, which is to install the AWS CLI, as we need this to be able to run the aws ssm command. Here we install the py2-pip alpine package, and then run the pip install awscli command, which installs the CLI package. We can now commit our changes, log into the EC2 container registry, and build and publish the microtrader-base image to the EC2 container registry. We also need to rebuild the various Microtrader images that are based from the Microtrader-base image by running make test, make release, make tag, and finally, make publish.

Consuming Secrets In CloudFormation

We have created database secret and cluster secret custom resources, and added support for injecting secrets to our Microtrader Docker images. We now need to configure the CloudFormation stack to consume these secrets, which requires us to update the various Microtrader ECS task definitions, and also reconfigure the audit database to use the database secret for the RDS instance MasterUserPassword. We will get started by configuring the audit database, which is named ApplicationDatabase in our stack. Currently, the MasterUserPassword property on this resource references the DatabasePassword input parameter, which we earlier changed to be a KMS encrypted value. RDS does not support decryption of KMS encrypted passwords, and also does not support accessing secrets via the EC2 Systems Manager, so we need to reconfigure the MasterUserPassword property to reference the value attribute of the DatabaseSecret resource, which conveniently returns the plain text value of the DatabasePassword. Now that the RDS instance is no longer consuming the DatabasePassword input parameter directly, let's update the DatabaseSecret reference to use this input parameter, and copy the KMS encrypted value to the development environment settings so that the secret is not hardcoded in our main CloudFormation template. We can now move on to configuring the ECS TaskDefinitions for our Microtrader services, and we will get started by configuring the AuditTaskDefinition, which needs to inject both the database and cluster secrets, given the Audit Service needs to access the Audit Database and join the Microtrader cluster. We first add the SSM_SECRETS environment variable, which is the environment variable the entry point script and the Microtrader base image uses to obtain the names of secrets it should inject into the local container environment. We next add an environment variable called SSM_VERSIONS, which is configured with the PhysicalResourceId of each of our secret custom resources. Note that the environment variable name is not important, as our container does not actually consume this environment variable. Instead, the environment variable is used purely to detect updates to the secrets, which in turn will trigger a new deployment of the AuditTaskDefinition and linked Audit Service. This is important if you periodically change your secrets, as once the secret has been updated, your containers need to be restarted in order to inject the new value of the secret. We also need to configure the AWS_DEFAULT_REGION environment variable, which is required for the awscli in the container entry point script to determine the correct regional endpoints for calling the EC2 System Manager, and also configure the HTTPS_PROXY environment variable so that the container can communicate with AWS API service endpoints. Finally, we need to remove the JDBC_PASSWORD and CLUSTER_GROUP_PASSWORD environment variables, as our entry point script injects these environment variables internally at container startup. We can now configure each of the remaining task definitions, and note that for the other Microtrader services we only configure support for the ClusterSecret, as these services do not need to interact with the audit database. After updating the Portfolio, Dashboard, and Quote task definitions in exactly the same manner, we configure support for just the DatabaseSecret in the MigrateTaskDefinition, as this task does not join the Microtrader cluster, and only runs database migrations.

Configuring IAM Roles for Accessing Secrets

In order to allow our Microtrader services the ability to get parameters from the EC2 Systems Manager, we need to update the ECS container instance IAM role, and grant permission to access secrets provisioned for our Microtrader stack. This resource is named ApplicationAutoscalingInstanceRole, and we add a new policy called SSMSecrets, which grants the ssm:GetParameters permission for any parameter prefixed with the name of our stack, and also grants decrypt permissions on the KMSKey used to encrypt our secrets. Although we have now configured the required IAM permissions at an ECS container instance level, I wanted to take this opportunity to demonstration a useful feature of ECS, which is the ability to configure IAM roles at an ECS task level rather than at an ECS container instance level. This feature is useful particularly if you are running your containers on a shared ECS cluster that supports multiple applications, and for security reasons, you don't want to grant application-specific permissions at a cluster-wide level. The scenario doesn't really apply in our use case, given our ECS cluster is dedicated to the Microtrader application, but nonetheless, we can configure the MigrateTaskDefinition to use a separate IAM Role to demonstrate this feature. In the MigrateTaskDefinition, we set a property called TaskRoleArn of a new IAMRole called MigrateTaskRole, which we subsequently create as a CloudFormation resource, specifying a trusted identity of ecs-tasks. amazonaws. com in the AssumeRole policy document. We create a policy called SSMSecrets that is similar to the policy created for the ApplicationAutoscalingInstanceRole, although notice that we only grant access to the database secret as the MigrateTaskDefinition does not need access to the cluster secret. It's also important to understand that the IAM privileges assigned to the Migrate task are not combined with the privileges assigned to the underlying ECS container instance, and if you want to learn more about this and other considerations for configuring IAM roles for ECS tasks, you can refer to the AWS documentation at the URL shown on the screen.

Deploying Secrets Using CloudFormation

Now that we have configured our stack to consume our secrets, let's save our changes, switch to the command line, and deploy our updated Microtrader stack. This deployment may take between 10-15 minutes, as our RDS instance needs to be updated with the new database password, after which our custom MigrateTask will be updated. And finally, each of our Microtrader services will be deployed with the new changes to the ECS task definitions. If we switch over to the CloudWatch logs console, and open the log root for the MigrateTask, notice that the migrations ran successfully, verifying database connectivity is working with our new database secret. We can also take a look at the dashboard service logs, which is always the last service to be deployed, and notice that all four Microtrader services successfully establish a cluster, confirming our services are all able to consume the cluster secret. Of course, the real test here is to verify that the front end of the application is working as expected, and you should see that it is indeed working correctly. One final demonstration I want to show you is how to validate we are in fact consuming our new secrets. We can do this very easily by navigating to the ECS console, opening the Microtrader cluster, selecting the Tasks tab, and clicking on the Run New Task button. After ensuring the MigrateTaskDefinition is selected, we expand the Advanced Options Audit Container Overrides, and we can see that we are definitely not injecting the JDBC_PASSWORD environment variable directly from the task definition. Let's now check the internal state of the container, which we can do by changing the command override to the env command, and then execute the task by clicking the Run Task button. The env command will print out all environment variables, which in turn will be logged to CloudWatch logs, and if we switch over to the CloudWatch logs console, open the ECS Tasks Migrate log group, and open the most recent log stream, we can see all of the environment variables that were printed out. Notice that the JDBC_PASSWORD environment variable has been set inside the container environment, which of course has been injected by the entry point scripts that processes the SSM_SECRETS environment variable at container startup.

Summary

In this module, we learned how to build a secure and robust secrets management solution for ECS applications, leveraging native AWS services, including the ECS Systems Manager and Key Management Service. The solution we created will help ensure the security of your sensitive data and credentials, and you will be able to apply the approaches we learned about to a variety of secret management use cases. We created a CloudFormation custom resource that provisioned SecureString parameters in the EC2 System Manager Parameter Store, and this approach allows you to integrate secrets management directly into your CloudFormation stacks. We learned how we can securely inject secrets into Docker containers by providing a list of secret parameters to query from the EC2 System Manager Parameter Store at container startup. and this approach means we avoid injecting passwords directly as external environment variables, yet still retain the flexibility and ease-of-use of environment variables injected internally inside our containers. Finally, we learned that we can safely expose the plain text value of our secret custom resources to other resources in your stack that do not support interacting directly with the EC2 Systems Manager, making our secrets management solution simple, secure, and universally available to all stack resources.

Managing ECS Infrastructure Lifecycle

Introduction

Hi, my name is Justin Menga, and welcome to Managing ECS Infrastructure Lifecycle. One challenge with operating ECS clusters is managing the lifecycle of the underlying EC2 Auto Scaling group instances that your ECS cluster runs on top of. EC2 constructs such as Auto Scaling groups have no awareness of the implications running on top of them, which means if you instruct the ECS service to remove or terminate an EC2 instance from an Auto Scaling group, by default, this will be performed immediately without any consideration of the ECS cluster or ECS services running on your instances. The scenario of immediately terminating EC2 instances is clearly undesirable as it likely will have very negative effects on your applications, and more importantly, user experience. So before we terminate an EC2 instance, we need a mechanism that will gracefully migrate running ECS services to another instance in our ECS cluster and Auto Scaling group. The good news is that AWS does offer a solution to this. And in this module, we will learn how to consume a feature called Auto Scaling lifecycle hooks, which provide the ability to notify you via the Simple Notification Service of an impending lifecycle event such as the creation or termination of an EC2 instance. We will learn how we can use a lambda function to consume this event, interact with the EC2 container service to gracefully drain all ECS services from an instance that is about to be terminated, and then notify the EC2 Auto Scaling service to proceed with termination of the instance.

Understanding Auto Scaling Lifecycle Hooks

You may be wondering under what scenarios EC2 instances will be terminated in our Auto Scaling groups. In general, there are two main scenarios when this will happen, and you will find that it'll probably happen more often than you might think. The first scenario is when you configure your Auto Scaling group to auto-scale, and although instance termination might happen for scale-out events, it definitely will happen for scale-in events. The other scenario is when you perform patching on your EC2 instances. We are in an immutable infrastructure world. Patching is performed by building new Amazon machine images, and then rolling in the new images into your Auto Scaling groups, which requires termination and replacement of each instance. AWS offers a number of building blocks that enable you to deal with any scenario that requires instance termination with the main building block being what I refer to as Auto Scaling lifecycle hooks. And Auto Scaling lifecycle hook allows you to be informed of a lifecycle event or transition such as the pending creation or termination of an EC2 instance with the Auto Scaling service waiting for you to perform any custom handling of the hook after which you can then signal the Auto Scaling service to continue and actually perform the lifecycle transition. The notification of a lifecycle hook is published using the AWS SNS or Simple Notification Service, which provides a topic for messages to be published to from which one or more subscribers can be notified of the message. An extremely useful capability is the ability to subscribe lambda functions to SNS notifications, meaning you can create a lambda function that will invoked each time a lifecycle hook event is raised. This of course leads to the question of how we should process lifecycle hook events, and in the case of an impending terminate event for an ECS container instance, one feature that ECS offers is the ability to drain an ECS container instance, which basically is a way to take an ECS container instance out of service. When a container instance is put into a draining state, ECS will attempt to migrate all ECS services and associated containers or tasks to other container instances in the cluster using the same connection-draining methodology we have already seen when updating our ECS services. And assuming sufficient resources exist elsewhere in the cluster, once ECS container instance draining is complete, we can signal back to the EC2 Auto Scaling service that the lifecycle hook is complete, and that it can proceed to terminate the instance. The net result here is that we now have a robust mechanism by which we can safely terminate and replace EC2 instances in the Auto Scaling groups that power our ECS clusters without fear of causing disruption to our applications and end users. So let's now implement a solution for leveraging Auto Scaling lifecycle hooks.

Creating a Lifecycle Hook Lambda Function

We'll get started by creating a lambda function that will be triggered by a lifecycle hook when an EC2 instance is about to be terminated, and we'll proceed to drain the corresponding ECS container instance, and finally notify the EC2 Auto Scaling service that the lifecycle hook is complete. Just like in previous modules, we will create a new project in our course root folder called lambda-lifecycle-hooks, and proceed to scaffold out the skeleton of the project's structure. I'll skip over the detailed description of what we are doing given we covered the project's structure in detail in earlier modules. We first create a. gitignore file specifying files to exclude from the repository, and then copy the contents of the makefile that we have already created for our other lambda functions, and we just need to change the function name variable to lifecycleHooks. We next create the src folder, create the setup. cfg file, and for this function, we don't require any third-party dependencies, but we will still create a blank requirements. txt file as our build workflow expects this to exist. We can define the function source code in a file called lifecycle_hooks. py, and use the same boilerplate structure from previous modules for supporting third-party dependencies in logging. One aspect that is different about this lambda function from earlier modules is that the function will be invoked based upon an incoming SNS notification rather than a CloudFormation provisioning request, so we just define a simple handler without any decorators, and also add clients for interacting with the Auto Scaling, ECS, and SNS services in the boto3 library. To understand the structure of the incoming SNS notification event object that will be passed to our function, we can refer to the AWS documentation shown on screen, which provides an example SNS message. The top level field is called records, which is typically a single record that wraps the SNS notification. Here we create a dictionary from the message property of the SNS notification, which carries the lifecycle hook message sent by the EC2 Auto Scaling service. We first inspect the lifecycle transition property, and if this is not related to an EC2 instance terminating event, we simply return and do nothing. We also captured the lifecycle hook name property as we will need this later on when we signal the EC2 Auto Scaling service that we have finished processing the hook. We next proceed to capture the Auto Scaling group name and EC2 instance ID, and then call a function named find_ecs_instance passing in an ECS cluster value and the EC2 instance ID. The ECS cluster value needs to be configured externally via the environment as the lifecycle hook has no knowledge of the specific applications or services running on the EC2 instance. Let's now define the find_ecs_instance function whose job is to return the ECS container instance ARN based upon the input ECS cluster and EC2 instance ID. Recall from earlier modules that the boto3 ECS client list_container_instances method can return paginated results across several responses, so we leverage the same paginator response function that we used previously in the lambda ECS tasks function to construct a fully paginated response. We next call the ECS describe_container_instances method to return detailed information about all ECS container instances in the cluster, and finally use a list comprehension that returns the single ECS container instance that matches the input EC2 instance ID, or returns none if it cannot be found. Back in the handler function, if the returned ECS container instance is none, we raise a ValueError, which we will need to handle later on. Assuming an ECS container instance is located, we now have identified the instance that is about to be terminated. We can now call the ECS update_container_instance_state method, and notice that we set the status to draining, which instructs ECS to drain all running services or ECS tasks to other ECS container instances in the cluster. The call to the update_container_instances_state method is asynchronous, and returns immediately, so we next poll the status of the ECS container instance to determine if all ECS tasks have drained, which we can do by checking the running tasks count property that is returned from the ECS describe_container_instances method. Here we set up a polling loop, which will continuously poll every five seconds until the running task count reaches zero, or the lambda function is nearing its execution timeout. If the count reaches zero, then the EC2 container instance has been successfully drained, and is now safe to terminate, so we signal the EC2 Auto Scaling service by calling the complete_lifecycle_action method specifying the lifecycle hook name, Auto Scaling group, EC2 instance ID, and a result of continue, meaning the Auto Scaling service should continue and terminate the EC2 instance. In the event our function times out, and the count does not reach zero, we simply republish the SNS message using the publish method on the SNS client injecting the SNS topic ARN and SNS messages properties from the original SNS notification, which will invoke a new instance of our lambda function that will step through our handler, and eventually continue polling the ECS container instance. Finally, we need to wrap our code in a try-except block as the EC2 Auto Scaling service is waiting for a response to the lifecycle hook, and in the event an exception is raised, we call the complete_lifecycle_action method on the Auto Scaling client, this time sending a result of abandon. The abandon result does not stop the EC2 instance from being terminated. Instead, it tells the Auto Scaling service something went wrong, and to abandon processing any other lifecycle hooks that may be registered for the terminating event, which may be useful if you are relying on multiple lifecycle hooks to complete successfully. Let's now build and publish our function. And next up, we will configure our microtrader stack to consume EC2 Auto Scaling lifecycle hooks.

Configuring Lifecycle Hooks in CloudFormation

Now that we have created our lifecycle hooks function, we can move on to configuring our microtrader stack to use this function, and intercept Auto Scaling lifecycle events. Let's first review what we need to create in terms of CloudFormation resources, and then move on to configuring each resource. We first will create resources supporting the lifecycle hook that is generated by the EC2 Auto Scaling service. This includes a lifecycle hook resource, which will configure the EC2 Auto Scaling service to generate lifecycle hook transition events, an SNS topic where we will publish lifecycle hook events that will then trigger our lifecycle hooks function, and an ARN role that grants permission for the EC2 Auto Scaling service to publish notifications to the SNS topic. We will next create a lambda function resource that will invoke our function code, create an associated CloudWatch Logs group, and attached an ARN role to the function, which will define the various permissions required by our lifecycle hooks function. Finally, we will create a subscription for our function to the lifecycle hook SNS topic, and create a lambda permission that grants the SNS service the ability to invoke our lifecycle function any time an SNS notification is received on the subscribed topic. Let's get started configuring our stack by first creating a resource for the lifecycle hook, which has a type of AWS, Auto Scaling, lifecycle hook. In the property section, we specify the target Auto Scaling group, and configure a default result of continue, meaning the Auto Scaling service will continue processing lifecycle hooks if the configured heartbeat timeout of 15 minutes is reached, and no result has been returned from our lambda function. We configure the lifecycle transition for EC2 instance terminating events, referencing and creating a target SNS topic that lifecycle events will be published to, and finally, we specify an ARN role for the lifecycle hook, which grants the Auto Scaling service permissions to publish to the lifecycle hook topic. We now will configure our lambda function by creating a resource called lifecycle hook function specifying the location of our code, configuring the maximum timeout of five minutes, and defining the correct handler entry point. Our function requires a single environment variable that defines the ECS cluster associated with the Auto Scaling group that lifecycle hooks will be generated for. And finally, we reference an ARN role that describes the permissions required for our function. We grant the usual CloudWatch Logs permissions, and then allow the function to list, describe, and drain ECS container instances, and ensure these permissions only apply to the ECS cluster in our stack. Finally, we allow the function to republish the original SNS notification in the event the function times out before ECS container instance draining is complete, and grant the ability to call the EC2 Auto Scaling service to complete the lifecycle hook. In order for our lifecycle hook function to receive SNS notifications, we need to create a subscription to the SNS topic we created earlier specifying a protocol of lambda in the ARN of our lambda function, and finally create an AWS lambda permission resource, which is the approach required to grant the SNS service the ability to invoke our lambda function referenced in the SNS subscription. Now that we've configured all of our lifecycle hook resources, let's save our changes, and deploy the updates to our stack by running the Ansible playbook command. And if we switch over to the CloudFormation console, you should see the various resources are created successfully.

Configuring Auto Scaling Rolling Updates

Now that we have created all the infrastructure to support Auto Scaling lifecycle hooks, let's now test the functionality of our implementation. As discussed earlier, one of the scenarios for invoking lifecycle hooks is when we need to replace EC2 instances in our Auto Scaling group. And the most common cause of this is whenever you need to replace the Amazon machine image or AMI ID of the instances. We'll shortly create a new AMI from the Packer ECS repository we created earlier in this course, but before we do this, we need to configure our Auto Scaling group to support Auto Scaling rolling updates. Auto Scaling rolling updates are a feature of CloudFormation where updates to an Auto Scaling group can be managed according to an update policy that you specify for your Auto Scaling group resources. Notice that when we originally defined our Auto Scaling group, we added a creation policy. And in a similar fashion, we can define an update policy specifying the Auto Scaling rolling update property, and configuring various properties the control the behavior of rolling updates. The min instances and service property defines the minimum number of EC2 instances that must be in service during a rolling update, and to ensure your Auto Scaling group does not lose any capacity, I typically recommend this asset to your normal instance desired count. The min successful instance percent setting specifies the percentage of new instances that must be successfully deployed, and I always recommend a setting of 100%, which ensures all new instances are healthy and have registered with our ECS cluster. Finally, we set the wait on resources signal and pause time properties, which configures CloudFormation to wait for up to 15 minutes for success signals generated by the cfn-init bootstrap process. And because our min successful instance percent is set to 100%, CloudFormation will create new instances one at a time waiting for a success signal before proceeding to terminate an existing instance, which will invoke our EC2 instance terminating lifecycle hook, and drain the EC2 container instance that is about to be terminated. Once the lifecycle hook function signals it is complete, and the Auto Scaling service terminates the instance, this process repeats in a rolling fashion with a normal desired instance count always being maintained until all existing instances in the Auto Scaling group have been replaced.

Deploying and Testing Lifecycle Hooks

Let's quickly save our changes, and update our stack using the Ansible playbook command. And once deployment is complete, we need to change that Packer ECS repository, and run the make build command, which will build and publish a new Amazon machine image. Once the Packer build is complete, we need to copy the new AMI ID, and update the development settings in our microtrader-deploy playbook to reference the new image ID. We now need to deploy our stack once again, and observe the behavior of CloudFormation in our lifecycle hook functions as the rolling update is applied. In the CloudFormation console, the Auto Scaling launch configuration resource is first updated with the new image ID, which then triggers an update to the Auto Scaling group resource. We can see a rolling update is initiated, which will terminate our existing single instance in batches of one while skipping at least one instance or 100% of the current desired instance count and service. CloudFormation then updates the Auto Scaling group configuration to trigger the creation of a new instance, and then waits to receive a success signal after which it proceeds to terminate the existing instance. At this point, the EC2 Auto Scaling service will publish a lifecycle hook event to the lifecycle hook SNS topic, which will in turn invoke our lifecycle hook lambda function. And if we switch over to the CloudWatch Logs console, navigate to the log group for our function, and open the most recent log stream, we can review the execution of our function. We first observe the incoming SNS notification event, and the key pieces of information here are the lifecycle hook name, lifecycle transition, Auto Scaling group name, EC2 instance ID, and finally the topic ARN, which is required if we need to republish the message due to a lambda function timeout. We can next see our function draining the ECS container instance, and then see the container instance status that is received from the call to ECS describe_container_instances. The status is polled every five seconds until the running task count on the ECS container instance reaches zero, at which point our function signals a lifecycle hook is complete. If we take a note of the timestamp of final message, navigate via Log messages log group for the application Auto Scaling group, and select the log stream for the old instance, we can see that as soon as our lifecycle hook function completed, and signaled the EC2 Auto Scaling service, the instance was immediately terminated, confirming our lifecycle hooks are working as expected. Once the CloudFormation update is finished, we should check our application is still running okay. And a final test we need to perform is to verify the republish behavior of our function in the event our function times out before the ECS container instance has drained completely. To test this, we first change the execution timeout of our function to 30 seconds, which should trigger the republish behavior of our function as ECS container instance draining typically takes a few minutes to complete. After deploying this update to the stack, we next revert the AMI ID in the development settings to the old AMI ID we replaced earlier in this clip, which of course will trigger a rolling update once again. If we now deploy the change using the Ansible playbook command, and proceed to open the AWS CloudFormation console, once the rolling update process gets to the point of terminating an instance, we can switch over to the CloudWatch Logs console, and check if our lifecycle hook's function is working as expected. You can see that a message is logged indicating that our function has timed out, and is republishing the SNS notification, and that our function is subsequently reinvoked, and continues to drain the ECS container instance. This process repeats until eventually the running task count reaches zero, after which our function completes the lifecycle hook.

Summary

In this module, we learned how to manage the lifecycle of EC2 Auto Scaling groups, and ensure continued availability of our ECS services in the event an EC2 instance is about to be terminated, which can happen due to an Auto Scaling scale-in event, or if we are updating our instances such that each instance needs to be replaced. EC2 Auto Scaling lifecycle hooks provide a mechanism to be notified when an EC2 instance is about to be terminated. And we created a lambda function that drains the terminating ECS container instance, meaning all running services will be redeployed to other ECS container instances in the ECS cluster. Once draining is complete, our function signals the EC2 Auto Scaling service to continue terminating the instance, and the net result is that our applications and users continue to operate without disruption despite the underlying infrastructure changes. We also learned how CloudFormation provides support for rolling updates with a configurable update policy, which allows us to specify properties such as the minimum number of instances that must be in service during an update, and the percentage of healthy, new instances that must be deployed for the update to be considered a success. The support for EC2 lifecycle hooks introduced in this module is a critical foundational component for supporting Auto Scaling. And in the next module, we will leverage this capability to add support for Auto Scaling to our microtrader stack.

Auto Scaling ECS Applications

Introduction

Hi, my name is Justin Menga, and welcome to Auto Scaling ESC Applications. Elasticity is one of the core defining characteristics of cloud computing, and describes the ability to automatically scale your applications on demand to ensure the best possible experience and responsiveness, for your users and applications, whilst optimizing costs by only providing additional capacity when it is actually required. AWS supports scaling docket applications running in the EC2 container service by providing a feature called ECS Auto Scaling, which supports auto scaling at an ECS service level, as well as EC2 Auto Scaling, which supports auto scaling at an ECS cluster, or ECS container instance level, via our EC2 auto scaling group. Combined together, these two features can work very well to effectively scale up and down your applications. And in this module, we will build a powerful auto scaling solution that will leverage the CloudWatch Event service to react in realtime to capacity changes in your ECS cluster, create an ECS Capacity Manager Lambda Function that calculates cluster-wide container capacity metrics, and publish these custom metrics to the CloudWatch service, which will trigger alarms when the capacity reaches thresholds that require scale out or scale in of the cluster. With the ability to manage ECS cluster capacity in place, we will then be able to implement auto scaling policies at an ECS service or application level, increasing or decreasing the number of containers servicing our application, based upon application-specific metrics. Along the way, we will learn how to configure auto scaling in the AWS Console, which will provide a good overview of the core concepts of configuring auto scaling, and then, encode our auto scaling configurations into our microtrader stack, so that we will have fully automated deployments using CloudFormation, that include auto scaling support out of the box.

ECS Auto Scaling Challenges

ECS auto scaling is a challenging, technical concept to understand, let alone, implement successfully in a predictable and reliable manner. The reason for this is literally two-fold, in that we have two levels of Auto Scaling we need to deal with, which includes auto scaling our ECS services and auto scaling our ECS clusters. On the surface, these may seem like similar concerns, but the reality is they are not. ECS service auto scaling is straightforward, and works how you might expect. Scaling container instance counts up and down, based upon current application load, or whichever factors make sense for your application. ESC cluster auto scaling, however, is much more challenging, and is actually based upon EC2 auto scaling as our ECS clusters and the container instances are directly based from EC2 auto scaling groups. Whereas the goal of ECS auto scaling is to ensure we have enough application resources available for a given ECS service, the goal of ESC cluster auto scaling is to ensure we have enough compute, memory, and network capacity to service all ESC services in containers running in our cluster. As you will see, the fact that we need to consider several different types of resources when calculating ESC cluster capacity makes auto scaling quite difficult. And although there are basic examples in the AWS documentation of how to auto scale ECS clusters based upon a single type of resource, there is not much that describes how you can consider and manage capacity in terms of all resources effectively. The good news is that in this module, you will first learn how to implement a powerful EC2 and ECS cluster auto scaling mechanism that considers all of the CPU, memory, and network resources in your cluster. And once we have implemented this capability, you will see first hand how this approach makes life a lot simpler when it comes to auto scaling your customer-facing ECS services and applications.

Understanding ECS Resources

Before we learn how to manage the capacity of our ESC cluster resources, we need to have a solid understanding of each type of resource and how each resource is managed and allocated in our ECS clusters. Although we discussed ECS resources in earlier modules, recall that ECS defines three main types of resources, which include CPU, memory, and network ports. CPU resources are expressed as CPU reservations and configured in terms of CPU units, with each CPU core in an ECS container instance allocated a quantity of 1, 024 CPU units. CPU reservations do not restrict the ability of a container to burst above the configured reservation, and are only used when the system is constrained for CPU time, allocating CPU time to each container based upon the configured proportion of CPU units. CPU units are a finite resource, which once reserved by a container, cannot be allocated to other containers running on a given ECS container instance. For example, in this course, we have configured each of our microtrader services with a CPU reservation of 200 CPU units. And once these units have been allocated to a running container, the assigned units are decremented from the available total units on the ECS container instance. And if there are not enough available CPU units, you will no longer be able to schedule containers with reservations that would exceed the current CPU capacity. The ECS memory resource model is more complex than the CPU resource model, and allows you to configure both memory reservations and memory limits. A memory reservation is configured in terms of megabytes of memory, and is identical to the concept of a CPU reservation in that it provides a guaranteed minimum allocation of memory to a given container, but does not restrict the container from using more memory if required. If you want to set a hard limit on the amount of memory a container can use, you can configure memory limits in conjunction with memory reservations. Whenever a container with a memory reservation is started on an ECS container instance, the instance will deduct the reservation from the total available memory, and it's important to understand that configured memory limits in this context do not affect the available resources of the instance, instead, merely setting a ceiling on memory usage for a given container. The only exception to this rule is the scenario where you just configure a memory limit with no memory reservation. And in this case, the ECS container instance will deduct the configured memory limit from the container instance memory resources. The final ECS resource we need to consider are network ports, which provide the service endpoints for our container applications, and can be provided in one of two ways. The first way is via static port mappings, where each container is allocated a fixed network port on the ECS container instance that is mapped back to the internal container port, or in the case of host networking mode, is exposed directly to the container. One major limitation of this model is that you can only have one instance of a given type of container per ECS container instance, which is particularly challenging in an auto scaling environment, as you need to orchestrate adding a new instance to the ECS cluster in the event you need to provide additional network port resources. The other type of network port resources are dynamic port mappings, where docker allocates a network port from the ephemeral range of ports on the host, and then, dynamically binds the chosen port back to the internal container port. The advantage of dynamic port mappings is that you can run multiple instances of a given type of container on the same ECS container instance, making it much easier to add additional instances of your container without necessarily having to add additional ECS container instances into your cluster. By default, the ECS-optimized AMI that our ECS-optimized container instances are based from provide almost 30, 000 dynamic ports. So for all intents and purposes, you can consider network ports an unlimited resource if you are using dynamic port mapping. Unfortunately for our scenario, the microtrade application is running in host networking mode, meaning we won't be able to take advantage of the dynamic port mapping feature, and we'll need to consider our static port mappings as a finite and critical ECS resource.

ECS Capacity Management

Now that we have a foundational knowledge of the ECS resource model, we can discuss how we can manage the capacity of your ECS clusters, which is very important for being able to support auto scaling. Before we can provide auto scaling for ECS services, we must ensure our ECS clusters have sufficient resources available to increase the number of ECS service instances or containers in response to an increase in demand or load on our applications. From a capacity management perspective, this means, at the very least, we should always have at least enough free resources to be able to schedule a single additional container. And if we don't have such capacity, we need to add more resources to our ECS clusters in the form of additional ECS container instances.
