This then leads to the question of how much resource constitutes enough free capacity for at least one container? And the answer here is that we should always consider the worst case scenario of the maximum-sized container in terms of CPU and memory that needs to be supported on our ECS cluster. The next questions that come to mind include how do we consider both CPU and memory, what about network port resources, and if we have a variety of containers, some of which may have very large CPU reservations, and others that may have very large memory reservations, how do we fit all of these resources and different shaped containers into a solution that always has just enough spare capacity to dynamically support both scale out and scale in auto scaling events? The approach we need to take to solve this complex problem is to first consider separately the worst case scenario for each of the different types of resources. For example, we should consider the maximum CPU reservation. We need to support for any given container in the cluster. And similarly, but independently, consider the maximum memory reservation required to be supported. We next need to calculate the current capacity for each resource in terms of number of containers, and repeat this process for each of our CPU, memory, and network port resources. Once we have the individual resource capacities, the overall capacity can be calculated by simply selecting the lowest value of each individual resource capacity. And if the lowest current capacity across all resources is zero containers, our cluster is at a point where it cannot guarantee it is able to schedule a new container from any of the supported containers on the cluster, meaning our cluster needs to scale out to add additional capacity. Let's now run through an example to understand better how we calculate ECS capacity. Assume we have four different containers that we need to support. The orange container requires the largest amount of CPU units, so we will only consider this container's CPU allocation when calculating container capacity in terms of CPU resources. The blue container requires the largest amount of memory. So again, we will only consider its memory requirements when determining container capacity in terms of memory resources. The green and purple containers each require a static port mapping of TCP Port 80 and 443, respectively, so we need to consider these as separate resources and determine the available container capacity for each of these port mappings.

Scaling out ECS Clusters

Now, let's look at the scenario where we have a single ESC container instance with some of our containers running, and determine the current capacity of our ECS cluster. At the moment, we only have orange and blue containers running, so we can immediately determine that we have a container capacity of one, for Port 80 and Port 443, meaning if a request is received to start either of our green or purple containers, we would be able to fulfill this request. From a CPU perspective, we are currently consuming 400 CPU units, meaning we have 600 remaining CPU units, which equates to enough CPU capacity to support two of our orange containers, which remember, consume the most CPU out of all of the different types of containers. If we now consider memory capacity, we are currently consuming 500 megabytes, leaving 500 megabytes of available memory, which equates to enough capacity for one of our blue containers, who are the largest consumers of memory in the cluster. At this point, we have to determine the free capacity for our resources in terms of the worst case scenario for each type of resource. To calculate the overall minimum container capacity of the cluster, we simply, again, take the worst case, or minimum free capacity for any given resource, which in this scenario is a three-way tie of one, given we have spare capacity to support containers that require static port mappings on Port 80 and Port 443, and also, have enough memory to support an additional blue container. This means in the cluster's current state, we do not need to increase our cluster capacity, because we still have a worst case of enough spare capacity for one of our blue, green, or purple containers. Let's now assume a green container is started on the cluster, and re-evaluate the cluster resource usage. Although our CPU reservation has increased to 500 units, we can still support two orange containers. And despite the instance memory reservation increasing to 600 megabytes, we can still support one more blue container. Given our green container is now using Port 80 on the instance, we cannot start another green container on our existing instance. So, the container capacity for this resource is now zero. And finally, we still have enough capacity to support a single purple container. The minimum value of the current container capacity across all resources is now zero, meaning our overall container capacity has now become zero, and at this point, we have run out of resources for the worst case scenario of attempting to start a green container, so we need to scale out and add additional resources to the cluster by increasing the ECS container instance count. After our cluster has added a new ECS container instance, let's assume that an orange, blue, and purple container are each scheduled onto the new ECS container instance. And now that we have two instances, we need to assist container capacity separately for each ECS container instance. Our first container instance has not changed since the last calculation, hence, our previous calculations remain the same. And now that you understand how to perform each of the individual resource calculations, I have already filled them in for the new ECS container instance. To determine the overall container capacity, we simply need to add the capacities calculated for each instance as shown, and you can see that the minimum container capacity across a cluster is now one. So, we do not need to scale out any further at this point.

Scaling in ECS Clusters

Let's now consider the scenario of scaling in, continuing on for the example from the previous clip. Calculating when we should scale in is a little bit more challenging mathematically, as we need to determine the point at which we can safely eliminate the resources of an entire ECS container instance safely, and still leave an available container capacity of one for each of our cluster resources. Let's assume the orange and green containers on our first ECS container instance are terminated, along with the blue container on our second instance. And let's now consider whether or not we can scale in. We first need to perform our container capacity calculation as we did previously, calculating the free capacity of each container instance and then summing to determine overall capacity. To consider the impact of removing an ECS container instance, we need to subtract the container capacity of a single instance for each of our resources. This equates to three orange containers for CPU, three blue containers for memory, one green container for Port 80, and one purple container for Port 443. You can see that we would have spare capacity for all resources, except for Port 443. And again, we only consider the worse case scenario here of zero, meaning we cannot safely scale in at this point. Another way to express how we calculate whether or not we can scale in is to consider the current idle host container capacity, which represents the number of idle hosts, or container instances in a cluster for each type of resource. We can do this by dividing the current container capacity by the container capacity of a single instance. And in the event this value is greater than one, then we can safely scale in, in terms of the resource we are analyzing. The overall decision to scale in can then be made only if the idle host capacity for all resources is greater than one, which can be achieved by simply selecting the lowest idle host capacity, and we can refer to this value as the overall idle host capacity. As a final example, let's now consider what happens if the purple container is terminated. The most important change here is that resources available for Port 443, which previously did not have sufficient idle host capacity to scale in, have increased from one to two idle hosts, and the lowest in overall idle host capacity is now greater than one. This means that for all resources, we would still have spare capacity to schedule additional containers if we removed an instance from the cluster. So at this point, we can safely scale in. Recall in the previous module, we implemented EC2 lifecycle hooks that drain our ECS container instances when they are pending termination. So in this example, assuming we terminated the ECS container instance on the right, the orange container would be gracefully redeployed to the instance on the left without disrupting our application or users, demonstrating the significance and importance of EC2 lifecycle hooks for supporting auto scaling operations.

Auto Scaling Solution Overview

We have discussed how we can assist and calculate ECS capacity and establish that we should use container capacity to determine if we need to scale out, and idle host capacity to determine if we need to scale in. Now that we understand what we need to calculate to manage ECS capacity and make auto scaling decisions, let's discuss the solution we will implement, to achieve our overall goal of supporting ECS auto scaling. The first aspect of our solution will need to deal with how often and when we should calculate ECS capacity, and we will leverage an AWS service called CloudWatch Events, which conveniently allow us to be notified whenever the resources of ECS container instances in our cluster have changed, meaning, we can react almost immediately to changes in our ECS cluster. We will create a lambda function to consume these events, which will carry the EC2 container service to calculate current resource utilization across all instances in our cluster, both in terms of the overall container capacity and overall idle host capacity calculations we discussed earlier. Rather than scaling in or out our cluster, our Lambda function will instead publish custom CloudWatch metrics for each of these calculations. And we will then create CloudWatch Alarms that will trigger whenever the overall container capacity drops below one, or conversely, the overall idle host capacity is greater than one. CloudWatch Alarms are the standard mechanism for triggering auto scaling actions in AWS, and we will create a scale out and scale in policy for the EC2 auto scaling group that our ECS cluster runs on with each policy being triggered whenever our CloudWatch Alarms bridge the configured alarm threshold. With EC2 auto scaling policies in place to ensure we always have capacity to schedule new containers to our ECS cluster, we will next create ECS auto scaling policies, which will scale our ECS service counts based upon whichever metrics make sense for our application. In this module, we will scale the Audit service based upon the Audit service CPU utilization, and configure CloudWatch alarms with appropriate thresholds for scaling up or down the number of Audit ECS service instances or containers.

Understanding ECS Events

CloudWatch Events is an AWS service that publishes events for a variety of AWS services, including the EC2 container service, with CloudWatch Events currently supporting the publishing of ECS container instance state changes, along with ECS task state change events. ESC container instance state change events are useful as they are raised for any change in resource utilization. For example, if a new container is scheduled, which consumes CPU memory import resources, this will raise an event indicating the updated resource utilization of the ECS container instance. And similarly, this will also happen when a container exits. In other words, an ESC container instance state change event is the exact time we should be re-evaluating current ECS cluster resource utilization, and we will use these events to trigger a lambda function that calculates the current capacity of the ECS cluster. Let's begin exploring CloudWatch Events and what these events look like by creating a simple lambda function in the AWS Console, which we will eventually subscribe to the CloudWatch Event service. After first navigating to the Lambda Console clicking the Create a Function button, and selecting the Author from Scratch option, we configure a function name, set the runtime to Python 2. 7, and create a very simple function that will simply print the ECS event to CloudWatch logs. We next select the Create a Custom Role option for the role setting. Click Allow to to create a lambda basic execution role, and finally, click Next, followed by Create Function to complete creation of the lambda function. We now need to subscribe our function to the ECS event stream generated by the CloudWatch Events service. And if we switch to the CloudWatch Console and select the Rules item under Events, we can create a new event rule by clicking the Create Rule button. We select EC2 container service as the service, specify state change as the event type, and then, filter events to only include ECS container state changes related to our microtrader cluster. We next associate the lambda function we just created by clicking on the Add Target button, selecting the ECS events function. And after clicking the Configure Details button, we specify a rule name, and finally, click on the Create Rule button to complete creation of the rule. Let's now generate an event by switching over to the ECS Console, selecting the microtrader cluster, and then, stop the dashboard task by clicking the Tasks tab, selecting the dashboard task, and clicking the Stop button. If we switch over to the CloudWatch Logs Console, select the log group for the ECS events function, and open the most recent log stream. We can see an event has been logged by our lambda function. The registered resource property defines the CPU memory import resources allocated or registered to the instance, whilst the remaining resources property specifies how much CPU and memory is currently available, and which port resources are currently in use on the ECS container instance. Now that we understand how ECS events work, we can proceed to create a lambda function that will use these events to determine ECS cluster capacity anytime an ECS container instance state change occurs. And before we continue, let's copy the event so that we can paste it into our function for reference purposes later on.

Creating a Capacity Manager Lambda Function

We are now ready to create a lambda function that will consume the ECS container instance state change events we saw in the previous clip. Just like in previous modules, we will create a new project in our course root folder called lambda-ecs-capacity, and proceed to scaffold out the skeleton of the project structure. I'll skip over the detailed description of what we are doing here, given we have covered the project structure extensively in previous modules. We first create a. gitignore file, specifying files to exclude from the repository, and then, copy the contents of the Makefile from one of our existing lambda functions, and change the function name variable to ecsCapacity. We next create the SRC folder, create the setup. cfg file. And for this function, we don't require any third-party dependencies, but we'll create a blank requirements. txt file as our build workflow expects this file to exist. We can now define the function and source code in a file called esc_capacity. py, and use the same boilerplate structure from previous modules for supporting third party dependencies and logging. For this function, we will need clients for the ECS and CloudWatch services in the boto3 library, and also, we'll rely on environment variables that will define the CPU and memory required for the largest containers in our cluster, and also, define any static port mappings that will be used by our containers. Notice that TCP and UDP ports are expected to be provided as a comma separated string, and we use a list comprehension to convert the string into a list of port values or an empty list if the environment variable is not defined. After creating a handler method that will be the entry point for our lambda function, we paste the ECS container instance state change event we copied previously, which we will comment later on. But for now, we'll leave uncommented so that we have a convenient reference for working with the event data structure. The first task we need to perform is to collect resource utilization data for each ESC container instance in the ESC cluster. And we first obtain the ECS cluster from the event detail clusterARN property, after which, we determine the maximum available resources per ECS container instance. We can obtain this from the input event object by using a generator expression that extracts the resource integer value from the CPU and memory resources configured in the event detail registerdResources property, and the next function returns a single value, which in the case of this example event, would be 1, 024 CPU units, and 993 megabytes of memory. We next need to query the ECS service to obtain current resource utilization for each instance in the cluster, and we create a function called describe_ecs_instances, which will return this information based upon the input esc_cluster. We've seen this pattern used in previous modules, and we use a paginated response function to recursively pass each paginated response until all container instances have been returned. In our use case, we only want to calculate resource usage for active ECS container instances. So, we use a list comprehension to only return active instances to our main handler function.

Calculating ECS Container Instance Capacity

Now that we have collected current resource utilization information for each ECS container instance, we proceed to calculate the current spare container capacity for CPU and memory for each active ECS container instance. We can determine this by taking the current available CPU and memory, and dividing by the respective CONTAINER_MAX_CPU and MEMORY environment variables configured for our lambda function. Here, we define helper functions for obtaining available CPU and memory, which use less comprehensions to sum the integer values for the memory and CPU resources specified in the remainingResources property. For example, if we defined CONTAINER_MAX_CPU as 200 units and there were 800 CPU units currently available on a given ECS instance, the capacity for that instance would be calculated as 800 divided by 200, or four containers. Once the for loop has iterated through ECS container instance, we will have a total count of available container capacity that could be scheduled onto the ECS cluster, based upon current CPU and memory usage. We next proceed to calculate the current TCP and UDP port resources available in our cluster, which references a couple of helper functions that return the current number of instances on which the TCP or UDP port is currently registered as in use. This calculation iterates through each port, defined by the TCP and UDP port resources environment variables. It populates a simple list of integers that is intended to express the number of instances in the cluster that do not currently have the port in use. Ultimately, we are only interested in the smallest value in this list, so we can initialize the list with the number of active instances in the cluster, which ensures we have at least one default value in the list that reflects the maximum capacity of the cluster when we perform the minimum calculation. To calculate the currently available port resources, we subtract the number of instances that currently have registered the port as in use from the total number of instances in the cluster, and proceed to add the available counts to the TCP and UDP port capacity lists. For example, if we had four instances in our cluster, in the check_tcp_port function for a given port, returned that two of these instances had the port currently in use, then the available capacity for the port in our cluster would currently be two containers. With the resources that are currently in use calculated for CPU, memory, and network ports, we can now calculate the overall container capacity, which needs to consider the worst case scenario by returning the minimum of the current capacity for each of the resources. This value will be used to control the scale out behavior of our cluster, and we can now focus on calculating the overall idle host capacity of the cluster, which will be used to control scale in behavior. For CPU and memory, the idle host count is determined by dividing the current container capacity of the cluster by the number of maximum sized containers that will fit into a single ECS container instance for each type of resource. For example, if we assume we have a current CPU capacity of six containers and we can fit four, 250 CPU unit containers into a single 1, 000 CPU unit instance, this would mean we have six divided by four, or 1. 5 idle hosts. Conveniently, the idle_host network port calculation is identical to the container_capacity calculation, as only a single network port can be registered on a given instance, and the final idle_hosts calculation is made by returning the minimum value of the idle host capacities for each type of resource.

Testing the ECS Capacity Manager

We haven't quite finished programming our function yet, but now is a good time to pause, comment out the reference event data so our code will compile, and test out our progress to date. To do this, we will work with the Python interpreter, which is very useful for testing out the functionality of your code without having to deploy the code to an actual lambda function running in AWS. We first need to ensure our environment is configured with the correct AWS profile, and then, need to change to the SRC folder where our code is stored. Before we start the Python interpreter, we need to set the various environment variables that our function expects, and we first set the CONTAINER_MAX_CPU and MEMORY variables to 200 and 195, respectively, which matches our current microtrader task definition settings for each ECS service. We also configure the TCP_PORT_RESOURCES variable with the various ports that we register in our task definitions, which includes Ports 8000 through 8002, for the quote, ordered, and dashboard HTTP endpoints, as well as Ports 15701 through 15704, which are used for vertex of in bus communications. We next start the Python interpreter, and can access our code by importing the ecs_capacity module, which by convention will import a Python file of the same name in the current directory. Notice that we are prompted for our MFA token, which is due to our code creating the ECS and CloudWatch boto3 clients. And one thing to note is that the boto3 library does not support session caching. So if we exit out of the interpreter, restart the interpreter, and once again, import the ecs_capacity module, you will be prompted for your MFA token once again. Our handler function expects an input event and context object, and we used the event we copied and pasted into our function earlier to provide the input event. We can now call the handler function passing in the event object, and just specifying none for the context object, which is safe as we don't use the context in our function. The handler function will now execute, and you can see that the handler first logs the received events, and then, calculates the current CPU, memory, and port capacity, from which the lowest capacity value is chosen as the overall container capacity. We are currently running a single t2. micro with four ECS services that each use 200 CPU units, and 195 megabytes of memory, resulting in a total resource utilization of 800 CPU units and 780 megabytes. And you can see our function calculates we have a container capacity of one for both CPU and memory. However, because we only have a single instance in our cluster, the container capacity of our TCP port resources is zero, as all ports are currently in use on our single instance. This results in the overall container capacity across all resources being calculated as zero, which means we need to add extra capacity to our ECS cluster so that we can support future ECS auto scaling activities that will schedule additional containers onto the cluster.

Publishing CloudWatch Custom Metrics

Let's shift back to our lambda function and finish the remaining task, which is to publish CloudWatch custom metrics, then publish the current container and idle host capacities. We first publish the container capacity metric using the put_metric_data method on the boto3 CloudWatch client, and specify the AWS ECS namespace along with a metric name of ContainerCapacity in the metric data property. The Dimensions property indicates the resources that the metric applies to, which in this case is the entire ECS cluster, so we specify ClusterName and the Name property and split the ecs_cluster ARN value to return just the ECS cluster name as the value. The final properties that we configure include the Timestamp property, which much be configured in UTC time, and the Value property, which is the value of our container_capacity metric to publish. We next publish the idle host capacity, which is identical in configuration to the ContainerCapacity metric, except we name this metric IdleHostCapacity and reference the correct idle_hosts variable for the value to publish. Let's now switch back to the Python interpreter, and because we modified our code, we need to reload the ecs_capacity module by using the reload command. We can now rerun the call to the handler function, which will now calculate and publish our custom metrics. Let's now exit the interpreter, switch over to the CloudWatch dashboard in the AWS Console, and select Metrics, which provides the ability to select and graph CloudWatch metrics. If we click on ESC Metrics, select the ClusterName dimension, and type in capacity in the search bar, you should see the ContainerCapacity and IdleHostCapacity metrics are present for our microtrader cluster. We can select each metric, which will include them on the graph above. And you should see that each metric has logged a value of zero, which reflects the current container and idle host capacity of our ECS cluster. Let's switch back to the command line, build and publish our function by running make build, and then, run make publish, which will publish our function to the E3 lambda bucket in our account.

Creating CloudWatch Alarms

Now that we have successfully published CloudWatch metrics for the container and idle host capacities of our ECS cluster, we can create CloudWatch alarms that will raise an alarm when the capacity of our cluster reaches thresholds that require a scale out or scale in action. If we navigate to the CloudWatch dashboard, select Alarms and click on the Create Alarm button, we will now create an alarm for the microtrader cluster container capacity by selecting ClusterName under ECS metrics, choosing the ContainerCapacity metric, and clicking on the Next button to continue. We configure an appropriate name and description for the alarm, and then, specify the criteria for when the alarm shall be raised, which at a minimum, is whenever the container capacity is less than one. In the alarm preview section, we change the period to one minute and choose the maximum statistic, which means the maximum value will be evaluated for all published metrics over the configured period. Using the maximum means if the container capacity fluctuates between zero and one within a single minute period, these fluctuations will be ignored, and an alarm will only be generated if all metrics received over a minute period are all value zero. Because our metrics will be invoked by ECS events and won't be collected periodically, we configure the alarm to ignore missing data, meaning any previous alarm state that has been generated will be retained if no metrics are published for a period of time. Finally, we delete the notification action, and then, click the Create Alarm button, which will create our new alarm. Let's now create another alarm for idle host capacity, selecting this metric from the microtrader cluster for the ECS cluster name dimension and configure an appropriate name for the alarm. The idlehostcapacity metric is used to scale in the cluster, and should be raised when the idle host capacity is greater than one. And just like the container capacity alarm, we ignore missing data, remove the default action, and set the period to one minute. Instead of selecting the maximum statistic, we choose the minimum statistic, meaning this alarm will only fire if the idle host capacity is consistently greater than one for the configured period of one minute. With our alarm configuration in place, we now need to send some new metric data to the CloudWatch service. So, let's switch back to the Python interpreter and repeat the steps of importing the ecs_capacity module, entering your MFA token and pasting the example ECS event into an object. After running the handler method on the ecs_capacity module, after a minute or two, the CloudWatch Alarms Console should update, indicating the ContainerCapacity alarm is in alarm state, whilst the IdleHostCapacity alarm is in an OK state.

Creating EC2 Auto Scaling Policies

With our CloudWatch alarms in place, we can now define auto scaling policies for the EC2 auto scaling group associated with our microtrader ECS cluster. If we switch to the EC2 Console, scroll down, and select Auto Scaling Groups, and then, select the microtrader development group, we can see that we currently are operating with a desired count of one instance, and on the Instances tab, we only have a single instance in service. We can add auto scaling policies by clicking on the Scaling Policies tab, and clicking the Add Policy button. And we will first create a scaling policy for scaling out, or increasing the capacity, of the cluster. There are three types of auto scaling policies that you can create, and I won't cover off the various types of policies in this course. However, if you are interested in learning more, you can read about each type of auto scaling policy at the URL shown. For our purposes, we can use the simplest of the auto scaling policy types, which is aptly called the Simple Scaling Policy. And after specifying a name for the policy, we configure the policy to execute when the container capacity CloudWatch alarm is raised, take an action of adding a single instance, and then, configure a cool down period of 600 seconds, or 10 minutes, before allowing any further auto scaling activities. This cool down period must allow for enough time for an EC2 instance to boot from cold and join the ECS cluster, and subsequently allow the ContainerCapacity alarm to be cleared based upon the new capacity added to the cluster. We can now add a scale in policy, again, choosing the Simple Scaling policy type when the IdleHostCapacity CloudWatch alarm is raised, taking an action of removing a single instance, and again, with a cool down period of 10 minutes. After creating our auto scaling policies, if we switch over to the Activity History tab and wait for a couple of minutes, you should see new activity appear, indicating a new EC2 instance is launching due to the microtrader ContainerCapacity alarm currently being in aN alarm state. And notice that the scale out policy has increased the desired capacity of the auto scaling group by one. Once this activity updates to a state of successful, if we switch over to the ECS dashboard and select the microtrader cluster, we should now see that we have two ECS container instances in service. Now at this point, because we haven't wired up our ECS capacity lambda function to the ECS event stream, we need to manually update the ContainerCapacity metric for our cluster. And if we switch back to the Python interpreter, and rerun the handler function on our ecs_capacity module, we can now see that the overall container capacity has increased to one, as we now have a new empty ECS container instance in the cluster, which means at a minimum, we can schedule a single container that consumes any of the static TCP ports that our microtrader containers use. Note that running our function updated the ContainerCapacity metric, such that the alarm state for the associated CloudWatch alarm was updated to OK, which is very important, as EC2 will continue to scale the auto scaling group up to the configured maximum capacity if the CloudWatch alarm remains in an alarm state. This is why it is important to suspend auto scaling actions for a period of time after a scaling action is invoked, to give time for the CloudWatch alarm state to be updated once a new ECS container instance and its associated resources are added to the ECS cluster.

Managing ECS Capacity Using CloudFormation

Now that we have had a walkthrough using the AWS Console of how EC2 auto scaling for our ECS cluster will work, let's now proceed to define all of this in CloudFormation, so that our auto scaling infrastructure will be set up automatically as part of our microtrader stack. Before we do this, we need to remove the manual changes we made, first deleting the auto scaling policies from the microtrader EC2 auto scaling group, and then, setting the desired capacity of the auto scaling group back to our configured stack value of one. If we switch over to the CloudWatch Console, we also need to remove each of the CloudWatch alarms we created. And finally, we need to remove the lambda function and event rule we created for inspecting the ECS events, generated by the CloudWatch Events service. We can now open the microtrader deploy project, and we will get started by adding the EcsCapacity lambda function to the resources section. We first configure a dependency on an associated CloudWatch logs group for the function, which we create as a separate resource in the same manner we have used for other log groups associated with other lambda functions in our stack. In the properties section for the function, we configure an appropriate name and description, configure the S3 location of our function code, and specify appropriate settings for the Runtime, MemorySize, and Timeout properties. We configure the handler method on our ecs_capacity function as the entry point, and then, configure the various Environment variables that our lambda function expects. Setting the CONTAINER_MAX_CPU and MEMORY variable settings based upon the configuration of our microtrader task definitions, and configure the TCP_PORT_RESOURCES variable with a list of all static ports used in our microtrader cluster. Finally, we add a reference to an IAM role for the function, which we create as a separate resource specifying the lambda service as the trusted entity, and add the various permissions required for our ECS capacity function. Along with the usual permissions required to write to CloudWatch logs, our function needs to be able to list and describe ECS container instances, and also, publish CloudWatch metrics to the CloudWatch service. With the ECS capacity lambda function resources in place, we can now define the CloudWatch Event rule we created manually earlier in this module, which will be used to subscribe our function to ECS container instance state change events for the microtrader cluster. Here, we create a new resource of type AWS events rule, configure a suitable description, and proceed to configure the EventPattern property, which defines a filter pattern that expresses which events we are interested in. Recall that when we created this event rule by hand, that an event pattern was generated automatically for us. And here, I have simply converted this pattern from JSON to YAML for readability and consistency. The pattern filters events to those generated by the aws. ecs service that represent ECS container instance state changes, and relate to the microtrader ECS cluster in our stack. The final property we configure is the Targets property, which specifies the destination that events should be routed to. Here, we specify the ARN of our EcsCapacity lambda function, and finally, configure the Id property with a suitable name for the event rule. One final resource we need to create to allow the CloudWatch Event service to invoke our lambda function is to add a lambda permission resource. And in the properties of this resource, you can see that we grant permission for the CloudWatch Event service to invoke our EcsCapacity lambda function in relation to events generated by the EcsCapacityEvents rule.

EC2 Auto Scaling Using CloudFormation

Now that the machinery for our EcsCapacity lambda function is in place, we can focus on creating CloudWatch alarms and define the EC2 auto scaling policies that will scale our auto scaling group up and down based upon our ESC capacity metrics. We first create a CloudWatch alarm for the ContainerCapacity custom metric, and you can see the various properties very much relate to the manual configuration we performed earlier. We first specify the Namespace and Dimensions of the alarm, configure the custom metric name published by our EcsCapacity function, to find that the alarm should be generated if the minimum metric value within a 60 second period is less than one, and finally, ignore missing data, so that the current alarm state is maintained in the event we don't publish any metric data. The IdleHostCapacity alarm is virtually identical, except this references the IdleHostCapacity metric and is triggered if the minimum metric value within a 60 second period is greater than one. The final resources we need to create are two auto scaling policies, one for scaling out the cluster based upon the container capacity alarm being raised, and the other for scaling in the cluster when triggered by idle host capacity reaching an alarm state. We first create the scale out policy and recall that the auto scaling policy for our requirements is the SimpleScaling PolicyType, which you don't actually need to define explicitly, as this is the default PolicyType in CloudFormation. We next configure the AdjustmentType property as ChangeInCapacity, and set the ScalingAdjustment property as one, given we will be increasing the capacity of the auto scaling group by one whenever this policy is invoked. Finally, we bind the policy to the application auto scaling group resource in our stack by the AutoScalingGroupName property, and specify a cool down period of 600 seconds, or 10 minutes, which prevents additional auto scaling activities immediately after an action associated with this policy is invoked. Now, you may be wondering how to link the auto scaling policy to our CloudWatch alarm. And this is actually performed back on the CloudWatch alarm resource, where we configure the AlarmActions property, and simply add a reference to the auto scaling policy resource we just created. Let's now create an auto scaling policy for scaling in, which is similar to the scale out policy, except we change the ScalingAdjustment to negative one to reduce the number of instances in the auto scaling group. After performing the final configuration step of specifying the scale in policy as an alarm action associated with the idle host capacity alarm, there is just one more small, but important detail to cover off. Now that we've introduced auto scaling support to our stack, we need to consider the impact of auto scaling on rolling update operations, which we discussed in the previous module. The best practice here is to suspend auto scaling processes whenever a rolling update is taking place, and this can be performed by simply adding the SuspendProcesses property to the UpdatePolicy for the auto scaling group, and then, specifying the list of processes as shown on screen, which will effectively disable auto scaling whenever a rolling update is taking place. At this point, the configuration of our CloudFormation template is complete, and we can now save our changes, switch to the Command Line, and deploy our updated stack using the Ansible Playbook command. Once the CloudFormation deployment has finished, because we have added our auto scaling infrastructure to our existing ECS cluster, we need to manually trigger an ECS event to ensure the initial alarm and auto scaling states are initialized correctly. We can do this by switching over to the ECS Console, selecting the microtrader cluster, and opening the Tasks tab, where we proceed to stop the task associated with the dashboard service, which will trigger an ECS container instance state change, given resources associated with the task will be released. If we now switch over to the CloudWatch Logs Console, open the log group for the ECS capacity function, you should see in the log stream that an ECS event has been fired, with our function calculating a value of zero for both the overall container and idle host capacities. In the CloudWatch Alarms dashboard, you should see that this causes the ContainerCapacity alarm to go into an alarm state. And over in the EC2 Auto Scaling Console, we can see that a scale out activity has been invoked. Once the new ECS container instance comes into service, back in the CloudWatch Logs Console for the ECS capacity function, we can see new events fired due to the new ECS container instance joining the cluster, which results in the container capacity increasing to one and eventually causes the ContainerCapacity CloudWatch alarm to transition to a state of OK.

Configuring ECS Auto Scaling

With EC2 auto scaling in place, automatically ensuring our ECS cluster always has spare capacity for at least one service instance or container, the task of configuring ECS auto scaling is simplified, in that we only need to consider which metrics we should use to scale our ECS service instance counts with the EC2 auto scaling configuration, taking care of ensuring sufficient container capacity in the underlying ECS cluster. To demonstrate how we can configure ECS auto scaling, we will configure the Market_Period environment variable that the Quotes service uses to control the frequency with which stock quotes are generated. By default, this is every three seconds. However, we can configure this right down to one millisecond, which certainly will drive a lot more load through all components of the system. Now, recall that the Quote service is a single instance service. In other words, we would never want more than one Quote service instance running. So, we will need to scale one of the other microtrader services that does support horizontal scaling. And the Audit service is critical service that supports horizontal scaling and is significantly impacted by the increase in stock quote frequency. To configure ECS auto scaling, we first navigate to the ECS Console, select the microtrader cluster, click on the Audit ECS service, and then, click on the Update button. On the Update Service screen, we click the Configure Service Auto Scaling button, and after selecting the option to enable auto scaling, we specify the minimum and maximum number of tasks, which allow you to define the upper and lower bounds of auto scaling. The Setup Wizard will automatically create an IAM role for us, and we need to add at least one scaling policy by clicking on the Add Scaling Policy button. We configure a scale out policy by selecting the Create New Alarm option, and creating an alarm that will be triggered when the average CPU utilization of the ECS service exceeds 50% over a three minute period. If we click Save to create the alarm, we can now add a scaling action, which will add a single task to the ECS service, and configure a cool down period of six minutes, which allows time to deploy the new task and for the increase in capacity to have an impact on reducing service CPU utilization. Note that setting the evaluation and cool down period is very specific to your application, so be prepared to invest some time in testing and fine tuning these values. After clicking the Save buttons to create the scaling policy, we can now add a scaling policy for scaling in, where we create a new alarm that will be triggered when the average CPU utilization falls below 25%, and we configure a scaling action of removing a single task from the ECS service. Once we click Save to create the scaling policy and complete the service auto scaling configuration, if we click the Update Service button, the Wizard will perform the various configuration tasks required. With the auto scaling configuration complete, we can now click on the Auto Scaling tab for the Audit service and we can see that our scale in and scale out policies are now represented on screen. If we now navigate to the CloudWatch Console, you should see a couple of new CloudWatch alarms have been created, and the LowCPU alarm should be in an alarm state, which is completely normal given the Audit service is currently not operating under any type of load. Now, let's introduce some load on the Audit service by modifying the QuoteTaskDefinition in the microtrader CloudFormation stack, adding in a new variable called Market_Period, and setting the value to five, meaning quotes will now be generated every five milliseconds, as opposed to every three seconds. After saving the changes and deploying the updates using the Ansible Playbook command, within a few minutes, we should start to see some fireworks. With the Audit service HighCPU alarm first being triggered, which will cause ECS auto scaling to be invoked, and we can confirm this by viewing the Events tab in the ECS services configuration for the Audit service. One thing to also note is that by scheduling another instance of the Audit service into our ECS cluster, given we currently have two ECS container instances in service, the container capacity metric drops to zero, which in turn triggers an EC2 auto scaling scale out activity, demonstrating our EC2 auto scaling configuration is dynamically increasing capacity as we add or remove containers in our cluster. If we take a closer look at the HighCPU alarm, by selecting the alarm, clicking on the graph in the Details pane, and then, adjusting the period to one minute, we can see the initial impact of reducing the market period when we only had one Audit service instance, which was then followed by a brief spike in CPU utilization as a new instance of the Audit service was started due to auto scaling. And finally, a definite drop in CPU utilization to just below 50% with two Audit service instances running. Now, one thing to point out is that running the application at this level of load will eventually cause failures, as the increase in stock quote frequency causes many more stock trades to be completed, which in turn causes a high write workload on the RDS instance that stores the Audit database. Due to the small size of the EBS volume attached to the RDS instance, this volume will eventually run out of EBS burst credits and will cause database connection errors. If we now switch back to the microtrader deploy project and modify the Market_Period variable back to its default value of 3, 000 in the Quote task definition, after saving and deploying the changes, once the deployment is complete, we should see that CPU utilization for the Audit service will settle down, eventually triggering the scale in alarm for our ECS service, and finally, causes an IdleHostCapacity alarm to be raised, given we have an extra ECS container instance in service that is no longer required given the Audit service has scaled back to a single container.

ECS Auto Scaling Using CloudFormation

Now that we understand how to configure ECS auto scaling manually via the Console, let's add support for ECS auto scaling to our microtrader stack via CloudFormation. Before we get started, we need to delete the CloudWatch alarms created for the Audit service. Switch over to the ECS Console, open the microtrader cluster AuditService, and click on the Update button to re-configure the service. After clicking the Configure Service Auto Scaling button and disabling Service Auto Scaling, we can save our changes, update the service, and switch back to the microtrader deploy playbook. We'll first create resources for the CloudWatch alarms that are required to trigger our auto scaling actions. Here, we define a resource called AuditServiceHighCPUAlarm, specifying a Namespace of AWS/ECS, and configuring cluster name and service dimensions, which reference the microtrader ECS cluster and Audit service, respectively. We then configure the alarm such that it should be triggered if the average CPU utilization is greater than 50% over three consecutive periods of 60 seconds. We next create the alarm for low CPU utilization, which is very similar, except the alarm is triggered if the average CPU utilization is less than 25% for at least three consecutive minutes. With our CloudWatch alarms in place, we next need to configure auto scaling for our ECS services. ECS auto scaling is defined in terms of application auto scaling resources, where we must specify an auto scaling target that we want to auto scale, along with an auto scaling policy that controls the auto scaling behavior. We first create a resource called AuditServiceAutoscalingTarget, which is defined as a type of AWS application auto scaling ScalableTarget, and then, proceed to configure properties that define the target. The ServiceNamespace must be configured as ecs, and the ResourceId property must also be defined in the format service/cluster name/service name, which we configure using the Fn Sub intrinsic function. The ScalableDimension property defines the ECS service DesiredCount as the property we will modify when auto scaling. And the MinCapacity and MaxCapacity set the lower and upper bounds of how much we can scale up or down. Finally, the RoleARN property references an IAM role, which we will now create, and this role grants the application auto scaling service various application auto scaling, CloudWatch, and ECS permissions required for ECS auto scaling to work. We can now create an application auto scaling policy, and we first create a scale out policy, which must be configured with a policy type of StepScaling, and is linked to the AuditServiceAutoscalingTarget resource by ScalingTargetId property. The StepScaling policy configuration defines the auto scaling behavior, and we configure the policy with the simplest possible configuration to add an additional instance when the alarm is triggered with a cool down period of 360 seconds, or six minutes. The MetricIntervalLowerBound property is a lower bound relative to the alarm threshold, and if we omit the MetricIntervalUpperBound property, then the upper bound is assumed to be infinity. So for example, in our use case, the alarm threshold for scaling out is 50%, and this means that for metric values lower than the lower bounds of 50 plus zero, or 50 up to infinity, we will increment the services count by one. We also create a scale in policy, which is similar in configuration to the scale out policy, although we change the StepAdjustments to decrement the ECS service count by one, when the metric value is between negative infinity, as configured when we omit the MetricIntervalLowerBound property, and the alarm threshold, which is configured as 25% CPU for our use case. The final configuration task we need to perform is to link the CloudWatch alarm actions with our auto scaling policies. This is achieved by simply adding a reference to the appropriate auto scaling policy on each of the alarms we created earlier, linking the HighCPU alarm to the scale out policy and the LowCPU alarm to the scale in policy. Before we continue on, there is just one more piece of configuration we need to perform, which is to modify the deployment configuration for the Audit in dashboard services, which at the moment, are configured to allow the EC2 container service to take the ECS service instance count to zero, when the input desired count is one. Because we are now managing ECS cluster capacity and ensuring there is always at least room for one container, we no longer require this configuration, as there will be available capacity to perform a rolling update and temporarily increase ECS service instance count. Let's now save our changes, switch over to the terminal, and deploy our updated stack by running the Ansible Playbook command. Once deployment is complete, we can verify CloudWatch alarms have been created. And if we open the ECS Console and browse to the microtrader Audit service, we can see that the auto scaling policies have been added to the Audit service auto scaling configuration. At this point, we should verify the auto scaling behavior of our application by reducing the market period sitting on the Quotes service and introducing load into the system. However, given we have already tested this out previously, I will leave this as an exercise for you to complete.

Summary

In this module, we learned how to auto scale ECS services and applications, and how to auto scale the underlying EC2 auto scaling groups that provide critical CPU, memory, and network port resources to our ECS clusters. ECS auto scaling is typically very application-specific, but at a basic level, involves adding or subtracting containers from your cluster in response to an increase or decrease in demand for your application. In order for your ECS cluster to support this dynamic auto scaling behavior, it must be able to scale out when the cluster has run out of resources to support additional containers and scale in when there are enough resources available if we removed and ECS container instance from the cluster. To achieve this, we learned that we need to calculate separately the worst case container capacity for CPU, memory, and static port mappings by expressing this in terms of the containers that consume the most of each type of resource. Once we have calculated this for each resource, we can then take the lowest of the calculated values to determine the overall container capacity. And if this is less than one, we need to scale out and add additional resources in the form of ECS container instances in the cluster. We also learned that calculating idle host capacity is a useful metric for determining when we can safely scale back our cluster resources. And if we have a worst case idle host capacity across all resources that is greater than one, we can safely scale in our cluster. To perform these calculations, we created a lambda function, which is triggered by ECS container instance state change events published by the CloudWatch Event service and we created custom CloudWatch metrics for ContainerCapacity and IdleHostCapacity, with CloudWatch alarms triggering auto scaling policies for the underlying EC2 auto scaling group that provides our ECS cluster. With our ECS capacity management solution in place, we were then able to define application-specific metrics and alarms for triggering ECS auto scaling policies, knowing that the ECS cluster will always be dynamically scaling our cluster resources to ensure our ECS services are always able to add additional containers to cope with increases in application load. Before we wrap up this module, I just wanted to point out an important observation that hopefully has emerged for you, which is that static network port mappings are not idea if you need dynamic auto scaling in your ECS clusters. Although this configuration is not possible with the sample application for this course, ideally, you should always use dynamic port mappings if possible, as this will greatly simplify your ECS capacity management concerns to just CPU and memory.

Continuous Delivery Using CodePipeline

Introduction

Hi, my name is Justin Menga and welcome to Continuous Delivery Using CodePipeline. Continuous Delivery paves the pathway from development to production. And if implemented with the correct software development and operational practices, helps to dramatically accelerate the speed and quality of application delivery, and significantly increase developer productivity, and reduce costs. Throughout this course, we have created a number of building blocks that work together to deliver our docker applications to production, using AWS. We have an application that uses a local docker workflow to test, build and publish docker images. And have created production-ready CloudFormation template that deploys our application, along with all supporting application dependencies and operational resources. As the final module of this course it is fitting that we now leverage these building blocks to create an automated continuous delivery pipeline, using the AWS CodePipeline, CodeBuild and CloudFormation services. We will learn how to create a pipeline that defines a source stage that integrates with our GitHub repositories. And then defines a build stage that automatically tests, builds and publishes docker images using the AWS CodeBuild service. Whenever a source code change is committed to GitHub. We will also configure our pipeline to generate CloudFormation templates and configuration files, using the Ansible playbooks included with our microtrader deploy project. And the proceed to continuously deploy new published docker images output by our previous build actions. To our microtrader development environment. By defining our development stage, which leverages CodePipeLine's integration with the CloudFormation service. Although we could continuously deploy to production, many organizations today are not quite ready to adopt such an approach. And we will create a production stage that will automatically create a new, CloudFormation change set for every source code change. And subsequently requires human approval before the change set can be applied to our production environment. Finally, we will learn how to define our pipeline, using the same infrastructure as code approach, we have taken throughout this course. Creating a CloudFormation stack that can be used to deploy and manage our microtrader pipeline.

Solution Overview

CodePipeline is a service that allows you to create sophisticated pipelines that define the path from development all the way to production. And to deliver this outcome, CodePipeLine uses several core building blocks that we will discuss now. At a top level, CodePipeLine defines stages. And at a minimum, you must always define a source stage, which provides the various source materials for your pipeline. An example of which would be the source code repository for your application. Each stage includes actions which can be executed in a specific order or in parallel. And for our use case we will define two source actions. One that connects our microtrader GitHub repository to our pipeline and another source action that connects our microtrader deploy repository. A very important concept to grasp with CodePipeLine is the concept of artifacts. Which can be provided as inputs to actions. And can also be created as the output of given action. For example, when each of the source actions in our pipeline is triggered by a commit to GitHub, CodePipeLine will check out the commit, create a Zip archive of the clone source code and publish the archive to an S3 bucket. This archive is then represented as an output artifact of the source action which then can be consumed by later actions in our pipeline. In a minimum, every CodePipeLine project requires that you always define a second stage in addition to the initial source stage. Which in our case will be a build stage but for some scenarios you might skip the build stage and proceed directly to a deploy stage. Depending on what you are trying to achieve. Note that you can call these stages, whatever name you like. But it is important to understand in general, there are three distinct types of stages. Which include source, build and deploy. For our scenario, the build stage will define two build actions. The first build action will be called image. Which consumes the microtrader source action output artifact and will be responsible for testing, building and publishing docker images for our microtrader application. This build action will out put an artifact, called Application Version which captures the tag of the newly published docker images. They will be used as an input for later deployment related stages. The second build action will be called Templates. Which consumes the microtrader deploy output artifacts and is responsible for generating CloudFormation template and configuration files. They will be included as an output artifact for use in later stages. Both of our build actions will use the AWS CodeBuild service which is a fully managed build service that provides a docker build container for running the various workflow tasks, associated with your source code repositories. The final stages are deploy stages that represent deployment of the development and production environments for our application. The development stage will include a single deploy action that will leverage CodePipeLine's integration with CloudFormation to deploy the could formation template in development environment configuration, output by the templates build action. It's important to note here that this is a departure from our previous approach of using Ansible to deploy from the command line. Instead we will only use Ansible in the templates build action to generate our CloudFormation template and environment configuration files. With CodePipeLine, subsequently using the generated template and configuration files, to deploy via CloudFormation. The final production stage will include three actions. First we will generate a CloudFormation change set, which calculates the changes for the new deployment that needs to be applied to the existing production environment. Change sets are very useful for deployments to production or controlled environments. As they provide an opportunity to review and approve the changes before they are actually deployed. To this end, the second action will be and approval action, where a human will need to manually approve the change set, before the third and final action of executing and deploying the change set to our production environment, can be evoked via CloudFormation.

Creating a CodeBuild Image

Now that we have sketched out our pipeline and understand how CodePipeLine works at a high level, let's get started creating a pipeline, using the AWS console. We will use the CodePipeLine wizard to create an initial pipeline that includes just the source and build actions, related to the microtrader repository. And then later on, extend the pipeline to include the microtrader deploy repository, along with corresponding source, build and deployment actions required to deploy our development and production environments. Before we can create our initial pipeline and start defining build actions that will be using the AWS CodeBuild service, we need to consider the docker image that CodeBuild will use to run our workflow and perform the various tests, build and publish tasks. The microtrader workflow is an advanced docker workflow that expects access to a local docker environment and uses tools such as Make and Docker Compose to run the various workflow tasks. This means we need a custom build image with the various required tools installed. And also need support for Docker-in-Docker or D-N-D for short, which you might guess, allows you to run a docker daemon inside a docker image. CodeBuild allows you to specify your own custom docker images. So let's now create a new docker image for CodeBuild and then publish this image into a new ECR repository. We'll get started by first creating a repository in the ECR Resources Playbook that we created earlier in this course, which we named Docker Production AWS slash CodeBuild. And to allow the CodeBuild service to be able to pull this image from ECR, we need to add the CodeBuild service to the read-only services list, which behind the scenes, will generate an appropriate IAM policy, using Jinja templating and attach the policy to the repository. After saving our changes and deploying, using the Ansible playbook command, if we switch over to the ECS console and select repositories, we can see that our new CodeBuild repository has been created. And if we select the permissions tab, you can see that the CodeBuild service has been granted pull access to the repository. We next will create a docker file in a new folder, called docker slash CodeBuild in the microtrader repository. And we will base our image from the docker DND image, which conveniently provide Docker-in-Docker functionality, out of the box. Meaning we only need to add the various tooling, required for the microtrader workflow. We first add python and pup, which are required for Docker Compose and add Make, Jq and Git, which are also used in our workflow. We next temporarily install a virtual set of build dependencies, required to install the python packages we need and notice that in addition to Docker Compose, we also install the AWS CLI, which is required for logging into the EC2 container registry. And also install Ansible. As we will reuse this image later on for deployment related actions that require Ansible. Let's not switch over to the console, change to the docker CodeBuild folder and build our new image using the docker build command. Taking the image with the fully qualified name of the repository we created earlier. And once the build is complete, we can then log on to the EC2 container registry and push the image to our ECR repository.

Adding CodeBuild Support for Build Actions

Now that we've created our CodeBuild image, we next need to add CodeBuild Support to our microtrader repository. The CodeBuild service requires you to specify build instruction that it should execute. And by default, these instruction are defined in a file, called buildspec dot yml at the room of the repository. The build specification is defined in a yml format and must include a version property with 0. 2 being the latest version as of the recording of this clip. The phases dictionary is then used to define the tasks you want to perform. At the various phases that CodeBuild executes. And you can see the list of available phases at the URL sign-on screen. One key point here is that some phases are executed regardless of success or failure. For example, if you define tasks in the build phase that fail, the post build phase will still be executed, even though the build phase failed. We first will define the pre-build phase which allows us to perform tasks or commands that are required to prepare for the build phase. Here we first use the nohup command to start the docker daemon running in the background. And then use a basic timeout script that waits up to 15 seconds for docker to start successfully. We then execute the make login command, which is just a shortcut for running the AWS ECR Git login command for logging into the EC2 container registry. We next define the build phase. And the commands here we need to execute, are the same commands we have run from the command line in earlier modules. Which includes make test, make release, make tag default and make publish. Now one thing to note here is that it's very important to define all of these tasks in the build phase. If for example, you specified make tag and make publish in the post-build phase, these commands would always be executed regardless of the success or failure of the previous build phase. And by moving the command into build phase, any errors in the tasks immediately prior, will result in the build phase existing immediately with an error, insuring the make tag and make publish commands are only ever attempted if the previous commands have succeeded. In the post-build phase, we define the make logout command, which ensures our container, won't leave any credentials behind. And this is a good place to invoke the make logout command, as it will always be executed even if the build phase fails. At this point there is only one more piece of configuration we need to perform. Which is to configure the app version environment variable in the env dictionary. And you can see that we set this to a CodeBuild environment variable, called CodeBuild resolve source version. The reason for this is that the microtrader workflow uses Git metadata to create an automatic versioning scheme for the application. However a current limitation when you use CodeBuild in conjunction with CodePipeLine is that the source repository output artifact provided to CodeBuild does not include the dot Git folder which stores all the Git metadata, required for our automatic versioning scheme. The microtrader workflow does however, let you override the auto-generated application version by setting the app version environment variable. Hence we do this in the CodeBuild specification with the CodeBuild resolve source version variable, providing the current Git commit hash as our application version. Let's now save and commit our changes and insure that you push the changes to GitHub. As CodeBuild will shortly be checking out, our new code changes.

Creating a Pipeline

Now that we have performed the prerequisite tasks for supporting CodeBuild, we can get started creating a pipeline that is responsible for running unit tests, building and testing docker images and finally, taking and publishing our images to the EC2 container registry. Let's first open the AWS console, navigate to the CodePipeLine dashboard and click on the get started button to create a new pipeline. After naming the pipeline microtrader, we are prompted to create a source action for the initial source stage. And after specifying the source provider is GitHub, and clicking on the connect to GitHub button, you will be redirected to GitHub where you are prompted to login and then asked to authorize access to your GitHub account from the AWS CodePipeLine service. After clicking the authorize AWS CodeSuite button, you'll be redirected back to the CodePipeLine wizard and you should now be able to select the master branch of the microtrader repository. We can leave the pipeline execution method configured as the default and after clicking next step, we are prompted to select the build provider that will provide the build action in our build stage. After selecting AWS CodeBuild, we then choose to create a new build project, specifying a name of microtrader and then select the specify a docker image, choosing Amazon ECR from the custom image type and selecting the CodeBuild repository and latest image, we created earlier in this module. We next need to create an IAM role for the CodeBuild service. Which the CodePipeLine service will create automatically for us. And after expanding advanced section, we need to insure the privileged option is checked. As this is required for Docker-in-Docker containers to run the docker daemon. To complete the configuration of the new CodeBuild project, we click on the save build project button and once the project has been created, we select next step to continue. On the deploy stage wizard, we select no deployment for now and after continuing, we next need to create a service role for CodePipeLine by clicking on the create role button. At which point we are redirected to the IAM console, where we click on the allow button to create the new role. After clicking next step to continue, we can review our configuration and click on the create pipeline button to create our new pipeline. Once our new pipeline has been successfully created, the CodePipeLine service will immediately trigger a new execution of the pipeline. After a few seconds, you can see that the source stage succeeds. Checking out the microtrader source code. And the CodeBuild build action is subsequently initiated. After a minute or two you will notice that the build task fails and if we click on the details link followed by the link to execution details, we can see that the pre-build phase failed and the build logs indicate the make login task is failing, due to an IAM permissions issue. Let's open the IAM dashboard, click on roles, and after selecting the CodeBuild microtrader service role and clicking on the show policy link. You can see that the role does not have any permissions for accessing ECR repositories. We can fix this by clicking the attached policy button and attaching the EC2 container registry power user role. Which includes both push and pull rights, given we also want to be able to push new images as part of our docker workflow. If we now switch back to the CodePipeLine console, we can retry the failed build task by clicking on the retry button. And we can click on the details link to view the build job and CodeBuild. Which should complete successfully after a few minutes. And our pipeline will now update to show the build stage, as having succeeded. If we switch over to the ECS console and select repositories, you should be able to see a newly published image. And notice that compared to the other images, the tag that is applied is a full commit hash. Given we have to rely on the CodeBuild resolve source version environment variable to set the application version in corresponding image tags.

Creating an Application Version Artifact

We have successfully set up CodePipeLine to automatically test, build and publish docker images on every commit to our application source code. We are almost ready to extend our pipeline to deploy our newly published images to the development and production environments. However, before we do this, we need some way of being able to pass the newly created build image tag to subsequent deployment actions in our pipeline. I refer to this artifact as an application version artifact. And ultimately it only needs to provide a simple string value that specifies the image tag that should be deployed. To add this artifact to our pipeline, we first need to create an output artifact for the build stage. Which we can do by clicking the edit button in our pipeline. And then editing the CodeBuild stage. You can see that the CodePipeLine wizard, automatically creates artifacts for us. However, I prefer these names to be much more representative of what they actually are. In our use case we want to create an application version artifact so we rename the output artifact to application version. Click the update button and finally, click the save pipeline changes to save our configuration. We next to actually create the artifact which we will perform inside the local docker workflow for the microtrader application. We first open the make file and modify the current make version task to output the application version a json object, rather than a string. Which is the required format, when we set up deploy actions later on in this module. We then open the build spec dot yml specification and add a command to the build task which simply captures the output of the make version command and writes it to a local path of build slash version dot json. The final configuration we need to perform is to tell CodeBuild where our artifacts are located which we can do by adding in artifacts property that includes the path to the version dot json file in the files list. Let's now save and commit our changes and then push our changes to GitHub. Which will trigger our pipeline and create the application version artifact, once the build stage completes successfully. We can verify this by opening the S3 dashboard and notice that in S3 bucket, prefixed with CodePipeLine, has been automatically created as part of the CodePipeLine's setup process. This bucket includes a folder for each pipeline in your account and region. And if we open the microtrader folder, notice that a folder named from the first ten letters of our application version artifact is now present. Which is used to store our application version artifacts. At the moment there is a single file and if we download and open this file, notice that the file is in a Zip format and contains the build slash version dot json artifact that was output during the build action.

Adding CodeBuild Support for Deploy Tasks

We have successfully completed the initial implementation of our pipeline and can now focus on adding microtrader deploy repository to the pipeline and add source build and deploy actions that will deploy the microtrader application to our development and production environments. Before we do this, we need to add support for CodeBuild to our microtrader deploy repository. Which we will use to generate CloudFormation template and environment configurations to deploy environments, using CloudFormation. This requires us to create a build spec dot yml file in the root of the microtrader deploy project. And after specifying the latest version of zero dot two we add a pre-build phase, which runs the Ansible galaxy install command to install the Ansible role required for the playbook. We next define a build phase, which runs the Ansible playbook command for our development environment. And sets a number of flags that control the template generation process. Given we are only generating templates and not deploying directly from our Ansible playbook, we set Sts disabled flag to true, which stops the AWS Sts Ansible role from attempting to assume the administrative IAM role that normally is required for deployment. We also sit the stack build folder variable to the build folder. Which will output the generated template and environment configurations to this folder. And finally, specify the generate tag, which will only run the template generator task in the AWS CloudFormation Ansible role. The final configuration we perform is to define the location of artifacts output during the build action. In given we set the stack build folder to the local build folder, we specify the location as all files within the build folder. Before we save and commit our changes, let's verify locally that the command that we configured actually works. And if we never go to the build folder, you can see that yml and json versions of the CloudFormation template were generated. Along with an environment config file for the development environment. This configuration file is structured in the format, required for CodePipeLine to deploy to CloudFormation and defines the input parameters that need to be passed to the stack for the development environment, as well as stack policy and stack tags that should be applied. Let's now save, commit and push our changes to GitHub and switch over to the CodePipeLine console to configure our deployment pipeline.

Creating Deployment Templates

We are now ready to start configuring deployment related actions in our pipeline. And we will get started by adding support for generating deployment templates. The first thing we need to do is add the microtrader deploy repository as a source. In currently in CodePipeLine, all source must be configured in the first source stage of a pipeline. So after clicking edit to start configuring the microtrader pipeline, we add a new action to the source stage, named microtrader deploy. After selecting GitHub as the source provider, we connect to GitHub, select the master branch on the microtrader deploy repository and define an output artifact name of deployment repository.
