Which we will need to specify as the input artifact for our subsequent template generation build action. We next add a new build action for generating templates which can run in parallel with the microtrader build action. Specifying AWS CodeBuild as the build provider and creating a new CodeBuild project, called microtrader deploy. We configure the project in a similar fashion to the CodeBuild project for the microtrader repository, specifying our custom CodeBuild image and creating a new service role for CodeBuild. After clicking the save build project button, to create the CodeBuild project, we next need to configure the input artifact as deployment repository. Which recall is the name of the output artifact of the microtrader deploy source action. And finally we specify an output artifact name of deployment templates, given our build action will create CloudFormation templates and environment configuration. At this point we save our pipeline changes and because we have added a new source action, our pipeline will be automatically executed. One thing to note is that any change to either of the microtrader or microtrader deploy repositories will trigger our pipeline and this does raise some concerns around the mutability of our microtrader docker images. For example, once this current execution of the pipeline completes, because our microtrader repository has not changed and is on the same commit hash as the last execution, the docker image created by the build action for the microtrader repository, will actually overwrite the previous published image with the same commit hashtag. We will learn how to deal with this later on in this module. However, I just wanted to point this out now, given this is an example where our pipeline is trigger by changes to the microtrader deploy repository, rather than the microtrader repository. After the new pipeline executes successfully, we can open the S3 console, navigate to the microtrader folder, inside the CodePipeLine bucket and open the newly generated folder called deployment. And if we download and unzip the most recent artifact, we can see that this artifact includes the generated templates in development environment configurations, created by our microtrader deploy build action.

Creating a Deployment Action

We have now generated deployment templates and environment configuration in a format that can be consumed by CloudFormation deployment actions in CodePipeLine. So we can now add a new stage called development to our pipeline and create a new deployment action, called deploy with a deployment provider of CloudFormation. In the action mode dropdown, you can select from a number of different CloudFormation actions. And because we want to create or update our microtrader development stack, we can either choose create or update stack or alternatively choose the replace a failed stack option. Which also creates or updates the stack but will also automatically delete and replace a CloudFormation stack that is in a failed rollback state. Given the nature of a development environment and the fact we want to continuously deploy to this environment, I typically recommend the replace a failed stack option and we next proceed to specify a stack name which we define as microtrader development. The template and template configuration properties, need to reference the deployment templates and configurations, generated by the microtrader deploy build action. And we first specify an input artifact name of deployment templates, followed by a double colon and then specify the paths to the CloudFormation template and configuration files, generated in the deployment templates artifacts. We next select capability named IAM for the capability setting. Which simply acknowledges that you grant CloudFormation the ability to potentially create IAM resources with a name variant required, if you have IAM resources with custom names. We also need to an IAM role. It grants the CloudFormation service the ability to deploy our stacks. And at this point in time, no such role exists. So we need to create and IAM role that grants us capability. After opening the IAM dashboard in a separate tab and selecting roles, we next click the create new role button, selecting AWS CloudFormation role from the AWS service role type and then to keep things simple, we attach the administrator access policy, meaning we won't encounter any permission issues when deploying stacks using this role. After naming the role CloudFormation deploy and clicking the create role button to create the new role, we can switch back to CodePipeLine and after clicking the refresh button, you should now be able to select the newly created role. We next need to expand the advance pane and configure a parameter override. Which provides a mechanism to override the input parameters, pass to the CloudFormation stack. If we quickly take a look at the config dot json file, we generated earlier for the development environment, notice that we are not passing in the microtrader image tag input parameter. Which defines the tag or version of the image that we want to deploy. By default, our CloudFormation template specifies this as latest. However, we actually want to override this and provide the application version that was output by the microtrader build action in our pipeline. And parameter overrides provide this capability. Parameter overrides are expressed as a json object with the top level properties, referencing any input parameters that you want to override. Here we specify the microtrader image tag parameter. And then use a special intrinsic function, called Fn GetParam, which allows us to extract the value of a given property in a json file. Because we have output the application version to the version property in the build slash version dot json file, located in the application version artifact, we can use the Fn GetParam function to extract this value as demonstrated on screen. The final configuration task we need to perform is to specify the input artifacts for the deployment action. This must reference the deployment templates artifacts which we use to obtain the CloudFormation template and environment configuration files As well as the application version artifacts which is used to override, the microtrader image tag parameter. We can now click on the add button to complete configuration of the deployment action. And after saving our pipeline changes, we can manually run our pipeline with the newly added development stage by clicking the release change button. Once the pipeline reaches the development stage, unfortunately, our deployment action fails. And if you click on the details link, you can see that the action failed due to the template link exceeding 51, 200 bytes. This is a well-known limit for CloudFormation that is applied when ever you upload a template directly to the CloudFormation service and the normal workaround for larger templates is to upload the template to an S3 bucket first and then provide the S3 URL of the template to CloudFormation. Which in this scenario will accept much larger templates of up to 460 kilobytes. Although this may be fixed in the future, unfortunately, at the time of recording, CodePipeLine does not support the higher limit. But luckily for us, the template generation process, also generates a modified compact json version. Which falls below the 51, 200 byte limit. So we can fix this easily by modifying the deployment action to use the json version of the template and subsequently update and save our changes.

Ensuring Immutable Artifacts

We are now ready to execute our pipeline and deploy automatically to our development environment. But before we do this, recall we earlier discussed the problem of previously created artifacts being overridden. In the scenario the pipeline is invoked without any source code changes to the application repository. Given the commit hash of the application repository is the same for each pipeline execution in this scenario. The principle of your artifacts being immutable and correctly versioned is fundamental. And clearly our pipeline is currently violating this principle. So we need to come up with a solution to fix this. One feature of the workflow included with the microtrader application is the ability to attach an optional build ID to the docker image tag that is created. In the concept of a unique and immutable build identifier for each build is critically important for any continuous delivery system. As it can be used to create a unique and immutable version and also allows us to trace a specific application version to a corresponding build, created by your continuous delivery system. Let's now open the CodeBuild specification and the microtrader repository and see how we can add a build identifier to our CodeBuild configuration. CodeBuild includes a build identifier that is provided via an environment variable, called CodeBuild build ID. Which is expressed in the format of project name, followed by a colon and then a unique identifier or U-U-I-D value. This unique build identifier looks back to CodeBuild job execution history. So clearly this is the identifier we want to include in our docker image tags. One problem with the CodeBuild build ID format is that it includes a colon, which is not permitted in a docker tag value. So we need to strip the project name and colon from the build ID, leaving just the UUID value, with tag-friendly alphanumeric values and dashes. We can do this very easily in the build phase by exporting the build ID environment variable. With the value of the CodeBuild build ID, passed by a simple said expression. And because we are overwriting, the app version environment variable, we now need to set this by an export command after calculating the build ID. Creating an application version in the format commit hash dot build identifier. Because we are now setting app version within the build task, we can remove app version, as a top level environment variable. After which we save, commit and push our changes to the microtrade repository. Which conveniently will also trigger our pipeline. And this time, I'll deploy action actions should succeed, given we previously updated the deploy action to use these smaller json template for CloudFormation. If we switch over to the CloudFormation console, select the microtrader development stack and open the parameters tab. You can see that the microtrader image tag includes both, a commit hash and build identifier value. Confirming our CodePipeLine deployment action is working as expected.

Deploying to Production

We have successfully configured our pipeline to test, build and publish docker images. And continuously deploy these images to our development environment using CloudFormation. Once our code has been successfully deployed to our development environment, the next stage of our pipeline is to deploy to production. Which typically we don't want to happen automatically and instead want some form of manual approval gate that requires human intervention before a release can be deployed into production. We will now create a new stage called production. And create several actions that will facilitate this workflow. In the first action, we'll create a CloudFormation change set which calculates the changes CloudFormation will make, when deploying a template to an existing stack. We specify a name of change set and select CloudFormation as the deployment provider. And then in the action mode setting, we select the create or replace a change set option. Specifying microtrader production as the stack name and production change set as the change set name. The template and template configuration settings are configured to extract the appropriate production template and configuration files from the deployment templates artifacts. And finally, we specify the named IAM capability, given our stack creates IAM resources. And configure the role to use CloudFormation deploy service role, we created earlier. In the advanced parameter override section, we use the same override we specified for the development deploy action. And finally, we specify both deployment templates and application version as input artifacts. We next add an action that will be invoked after the change set action has completed. Which we specify as an approval action and select the manual approval time. Which allows us to specify optional manual approval configuration settings. We won't configure any of these in this module. However you can, for example, add the resource name of an SNS topic, which could then be used to notify an approver via email, whenever an approval is required. After adding the approval action, the final action we create, is a CloudFormation deployment action called deploy. Which executes the change set that we previously created and the change set action. Here we must specify the correct stack name and the name of the change set we created previously and because the change set, already includes the CloudFormation template, configuration settings and parameter overrides, we don't need to provide any other configuration for this action. After we complete creating the action, and saving our changes to the pipeline, we are almost ready to deploy our production environment. Although there is one more thing that we need to do, which is to add production environment settings to our microtrader deploy playbook. And I will first copy the development settings and paste these into the group vars production dot yml file. The only setting we need to change here is the application DNS name. And although you would also, typically change the database password, I will just use the same value here for expediency purposes. Now that we have our production settings in place, we also need to update the buildspec dot yml file to generate the CloudFormation template and configuration files for our production environment. Which we can do by simply copying the command for the development environment and changing the env variable to production. If we now save, commit and push our changes to GitHub, back in the CodePipeLine console, our pipeline will be triggered by the most recent commit to the microtrader deploy repository. After our build actions complete and deployment to the development environment is successful, a production change set is first created after which the manual approval action is invoked. And at this point, the pipeline is paused and will not proceed to the next action until manual approval is completed. Let's first review the change set that was created by clicking on the details link in the change set action. And we can see, because we haven't yet deployed our production stack, the change set indicates all resources in the stack will be created. If we open the CloudFormation stacks view, notice that the microtrader production stack has been created and has a status of review in progress. Meaning CloudFormation is waiting for approval of the change set. Assuming we are happy with the changes, we can switch back to the CodePipeLine console and now click on the review button. Which allows us to add comments and either approve or reject the change. Once the change is approved, the deploy action will be invoked. Which executes the change sets and in this case, will create our production environment. Let's now switch back to the CloudFormation console and notice that our new microtrader production stack has been created. Once that creation is complete, we can select the outputs tab, copy the public load balancer URL and verify our microtrader application, has been successfully deployed to production.

Creating Pipelines in CloudFormation

We have successfully created a continuous delivery pipeline, using CodePipeLine, CodeBuild and CloudFormation. And as a final configuration task, we will now learn how to define our pipeline, using CloudFormation. In the course root folder, we first clone the AWS starter repository into a new folder called microtrader pipeline. Create a new Git repository and using make roles command to install the required Ansible roles for our playbook. We only need single instance of our pipeline and given we are using a single AWS account that includes our production environment. We define our environment called production in the inventory file, create the appropriate file for the production environment settings, configure the correct role to assume for the Ansible STS role and finally open the group vars, all vars dot yml file and change the stack name to microtrader dash pipeline. We are now ready to configure our CloudFormation stack. And we'll get started by defining a resource called Pipeline. Which represent a pipeline resource, managed by the CodePipeLine service. We first define a name for the pipeline and next configure the artifact store, which recall, needs to be in S3 bucket. So we create an associated pipeline bucket S3 resource and reference this, using the artifact store location property. We next attach and IAM role to the pipeline, which we define as separate resource that needs to specify the CodePipeLine service as a trusted entity. The list of permissions we need to provide to CodePipeLine is quite extensive. But in summary, we grant S3 access to the artifact store, allow CodePipeLine to execute CodeBuild jobs, grant permission to pass a service roll to the CloudFormation service and finally allow CodePipeLine the ability to perform various CloudFormation operations for our microtrader stacks. Let's now also quickly, define the CloudFormation service role which must specify the CloudFormation service as a trusted entity. And we attach the administrator access manage policy as a quick way to ensure the CloudFormation service, can perform all required provisioning tasks.

Defining the Source Stage in CloudFormation

Back in the pipeline resource, we are read to define the various stages of our pipeline. And rather than handcraft this manually, one convenient way to obtain these stages configuration, we require is to use the AWS CLI CodePipeLine command to query the microtrader pipeline we created manually, earlier in this module. The stages property defines the configuration that we need to specify in the CodePipeLine resource. So we can use this as a reference point to help determine the necessary configuration required. We first create the source stage and at a source action for our microtrader project. With the action type ID property defining the action as a GitHub source action. And next configure the output artifacts property, which defines the name of the output artifact. The configuration property is an arbitrary collection of key value peers that varies depending on the type of action. And in this case, we add configuration that specifies the GitHub account name repository and branch and finally needs to provide and OAuth token which will allow the CodePipeLine service to access the specified GitHub account. We will generate a token shortly over in GitHub but before we do this, we need to insure we can encrypt and secure the token in our stack. And we will use these secrets provisioner function, created in earlier modules to expose the GitHub token securely to the pipeline resource in our stack. Here I am copying the relevant secrets-related resources from the microtrader deploy stack and after modifying a few settings to work with out pipeline stack, we define a custom resource called GitHub secret that requires the token to be provided as a Kms encrypted value, which will provision the secret into the EC2 systems manager parameter store. But more importantly, can expose the token securely to other resources by referencing the value property of the custom resource. We now need to define and input parameter, called GitHub token that will inject the encrypted secret and define a corresponding environment setting that will supply the encrypted GitHub token, as an input parameter to the stack. We can now switch over to GitHub, select settings from the user drop-down and select personal access tokens under developer settings, where we can create a new token that will grant the CodePipeLine service, access to your GitHub account. We need to grant access to all repo and repo hook permissions. Once we have generated and copied the token, if we switch over to the command line, we can use the AWS Kms encrypt command along with the Kms key ID of the Cfn master key to generate a version of the token which we then configure for the config GitHub token environment setting. We next need to create another source action for the microtrader deploy repository. Which is very similar to the microtrader source action. Except it references the microtrader deploy project and is configured to output an artifact name of deployment repository.

Defining the Build Stage in CloudFormation

We can next move on to defining the build stage which recall, requires to actions. One to test, build and publish microtrader docker images from the microtrader repository and the other to generate CloudFormation templates in environment configurations from the microtrader deployment repository. We named the first action image and in the action type ID property, we specify the action as a build action that is provided by the AWS CodeBuild service. This action requires the microtrader repository as an input artifact and will ultimately output an artifact called application version. In the configuration section, we need to configure the CodeBuild project name which references a CloudFormation resource, called microtrader build. And we next proceed to create this resource. To understand the configuration required for CodeBuild projects, we can use the AWS CodeBuild command, specifying both the microtrader and microtrader deploy projects, we manually created earlier in this module. The microtrader build resource is configured with a name of microtrader, defines a source as type CodePipeLine and when using CodePipeLine to drive CodeBuild, you must specify the output artifact packages, none, in the type as CodePipeLine. In the environment property, we must define the type and size of the build machine that we require. And because our workflow uses a custom docker image, we specify the fully qualified name of our docker image and enable privilege mode to support the Docker-in-Docker functionality we require. The final property we configure is the service role property which attaches an IAM role that grants the various permissions that CodeBuild service requires. Here we grant access to the log groups, used by CodeBuild, permit S3 access to the pipeline artifacts store and finally grant EC2 container registry permissions, so that our CodeBuild agents will be able to login to ECR and push and pull any microtrader image. Back in the pipeline resource, we can now add our second build action which we call templates. Given this action generates CloudFormation templates and environment configurations from the microtrader deploy repository. This action requires the deployment repository artifact as an input. And will output artifacts that we configure with a name of deployment templates. In the configuration for the action, we reference a new CodeBuild project, called microtrader deploy build. Which we define alongside the microtrader build resource and the configuration follows a similar pattern. Although the build environment, does not require privileged access, required for Docker-in-Docker support, in the linked microtrader deploy build IAM role that grants permission to the CodeBuild service, only requires permissions to write to logs and access the pipeline artifacts store.

Defining Deploy Stages in CloudFormation

The next stage we need to configure is the development stage where we define an action called deploy which has an action type ID property that defines AWS CloudFormation as the deployment provider. Our deploy action requires two input artifacts. The deployment template artifact, which provides the CloudFormation template and configuration files and the application version artifacts which is used to overwrite the microtrader image tag input parameter. The configuration section specifies an action mode of replace on failure which means CodePipeLine will create or update the stack even if the stack, is in a failed rollback state. And we define appropriate settings for the stack name, capabilities, template path and template configuration properties. For the parameter overrides property, we use the Fn GetParam syntax to retrieve the correct version from the application version artifacts. Which we must specify as a json object, in a string value format. And finally we attach the CloudFormation service role which we defined earlier and grants the CloudFormation service, administrative privileges to be able to to create all resources in our stacks. We next, to find the production stage which recall contains three actions. The first action is to create a change set. And the configuration is similar to the previous development stage deploy action, except we specify an action mode of change set replace to find a change set name and reference the production template in configuration files. Notice that we also add a run order property which ensure this action will always run first in the stage. The next action we create is the manual approval action. Which has a very simple configuration and is configured to run as the second action in the stage after the change set action has completed successfully. The final action we create is the deploy action. Which can only be invoked after the approval action has been manually approved And executes the change set created by the previous change set action which will deploy to the microtrader production environment.

Deploying a Pipeline Using CloudFormation

We have completed the CloudFormation template for our pipeline. And before we deploy our pipeline stack, we first need to remove the pipeline we created manually as it has naming conflicts without our new pipeline stack. Over in the CodeBuild console, we also need to manually remove the microtrader and microtrader deploy projects that were created via the CodePipeLine wizard. And finally, you should also empty and remove the CodePipeLine S3 bucket created as part of the wizard. As well as the AWS CodePipeLine service, CodeBuild and CloudFormation deploy IAM role which all supported our pipeline. With our cleanup out of the way, we can now switch to the command line and run the Ansible playbook command. Referencing the single production environment, we have defined for our pipeline. The deployment should complete fairly quickly within a few minutes and if we switch over to the CodePipeLine console, you will see that CodePipeLine automatically attempts to run our new pipeline on initial creation. Although our source code repositories have not changed, since the last deployment, using our previously manually created pipeline, because every execution of the pipeline, generates a new build ID in the microtrader image build action. This will result in a new deployment to our development environment. And if successful, will proceed to the production stage, create a new change set and trigger the manual approval gate. Given we have a preexisting production stack, you can see that the change set is now much smaller, indicating the ECS task definition in ECS service resources will be affected. Given the only change is new image tag, output by the previous build stage. Finally, after approving the change set, the final deploy action is invoked and once complete, we have continuously delivered and update to production using CodePipeLine.

Summary

In this module, we learned how to implement continuous delivery for our applications, using the AWS CodePipeLine, CodeBuild and CloudFormation services. CodePipeLine allow you to model and manage the pathway from development through to production by defining a number of stages which each consist of one or more actions that perform various tasks. The source stage is always the first stage in CodePipeLine and defines all of these source materials, such as source code repository. Required to test, build and deploy your application. CodePipeLine supports integration with GitHub and once integrated, will automatically detect any source code changes to your depositories and publish your source code as an output artifact that can be consumed by later stages. The next stage we created was the build stage. Where we created an image action that used the AWS CodeBuild service to execute the docker workflow that tests, builds and publishes docker images for microtrader application. We learned how to create a custom dock image for CodeBuild that supports Docker-in-Docker operation. Which is required anytime you need to build a docker image as part of your workflow or work automated acceptance tests, using a local docker compose environment. CodePipeLine artifacts provide the input and output, between the various stages and actions. And we enhanced the microtrader workflow to output and application version artifact, allowing the docker image tag, published during the build stage to be available to later deployment stages. We also created a second build action that generates CloudFormation template, in environment configuration files from the microtrader deploy repository. Which are subsequently used to support deployment of our CloudFormation stacks in later deployment stages. We next proceeded to define a stage called development. Which is responsible for continuously deploying, the latest application code changes into our development environment, leveraging the artifacts output by the previous build stage to provide the CloudFormation stack and configuration required for CloudFormation. Finally, we created a stage called production. Which provides controlled release of our application into the production environment. By first creating a CloudFormation change set, it defines all of the changes that will be applied if the change set is released into production and then requiring manual approval, allowing a human to review the change set before approving or rejecting the deployment into production. As this is the final module of this course, I would like to personally thank you for your time and attention. And hope that you have significantly increased your learning of how to work docker based application in production, using AWS. I certainly have enjoyed creating this course and sharing with you, just how you can leverage, the wonderful technologies provided by Docker, AWS and the other tools we've used in this course. And I'm sure that you now feel empowered to apply these learnings with confidence to you own real-world problems and solutions.
